# Server

The J3 Bots server is the product. It owns what the operator sees and decides what every bot does next. This document describes its data, its one central mechanism (the wake pipeline) and the features built on it.

## Data

J3 Bots keeps two kinds of state, in two places, for two different readers.

**The database** (`<J3_HOME>/j3.sqlite`) holds what the server needs to query and what clients render:

| Entity | What it holds |
| --- | --- |
| Settings | One row: the operator's name, their time zone and whether it was set, the budgets, notification preferences, the update channel, whether J3 Bots updates its own copies of the harnesses by itself, and whether the harnesses suggest the operator's next message ([Suggested replies](#suggested-replies)) |
| Bot | Profile, model and its effort ([Effort](#effort)), permission mode, whether it is hidden, the bot that made it while that bot exists, and whether the operator chose its visibility ([Visibility](#visibility)), whether it may use the browser, whether it may fill saved logins ([Filling a login](#filling-a-login)) and how many times it lost it that are not yet withdrawn from its project, how many times its model, effort, permission mode or browser switch changed, workspace path and the real path it had when first provisioned, engine project id, whether its conversation was told that its main session is not ready, the count of model or permission mode changes at which the engine refused its permission mode, and the refusal of it its conversation was told of |
| Conversation | One per bot. A sequence of entries, and how far the operator has read |
| Section | A named group of bots in the roster: its name and its place among the others. A bot names the one it is in, or none |
| Room | Name, members in order, status (active or archived), when it was archived, whether it is hidden, whether it is of bots only (`bots_only`, [Rooms](#rooms)), the bot that started it while that bot exists, and whether the operator chose its visibility ([Visibility](#visibility)), and the turns whose post relayed the operator's word there (`room_relays`), and how far each call that read the room or was refused a post there was shown it (`room_views`). A sequence of entries, each room message with its round ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)) |
| Entry | A message or a card, with its author, its time, its delivery state and its fixed place (`order`) in its conversation or room. A message keeps what it quotes, as it stood when it was sent ([Quotes](#quotes)) |
| Reaction | One emoji on a message, by the operator or by a bot, one per reactor and message, with when that reactor first reacted there; and, for the operator's reaction on a bot's message, when its few seconds of changes settle ([Reactions](#reactions)) |
| Session | J3 Bots' own id, its bot, its purpose (main or side), the engine thread id once the engine has one, when the engine was first asked to open it under its current key, how many times the engine refused to open it, the prompt version it was created with, the provider it runs on, how many changes of its bot's settings it has not been brought to (a main session) or how many its bot had when it was written (a side session), the browser switch its tools were last brought to, the rotation it is due for and why, when it was retired and when the engine took it out of use after that, the cursor of the engine events applied so far, the turn it has admitted with that turn's audience, the engine queue's current hold and the hold it last resumed, the turn whose stop its AI ignores and the turn a Force stop was asked for, when the engine answered that it has no such session and, for a side session, the routine wake it runs |
| Wake | Kind, priority, target bot, audience, payload, hop count, status (pending, dispatched, finished, cancelled or failed), the dispatch that delivered it, for a room wake, whether its bot left the room before it ran, and, for one a bot's turn for one room posted in another, that bot and the room its turn spoke for |
| Dispatch | One delivery to the engine: its sequence number, the order it was written in, the target session, the audience, start or steer, the rendered envelope and its files, how many times it was sent, how many times staging its files failed, the disposition the engine reported first if it reported one, for a room dispatch the room's newest entry when its envelope was written, the turn that delivers it, its status and, when it failed, why, a pending continuation of its turn, the Stop that fenced it, the outcome. Several coalesced wakes share one |
| Stop | The operator's Stop of one bot (scope `bot`), or of a room's work when the room is archived (scope `room`): its command id, the highest dispatch sequence number when it was recorded, and per target session and round the engine key, when the engine acknowledged it or refused it, when the events were observed past that acknowledgment, and whether they confirmed it stopped |
| Ask card | For an `ask` question: the wake it came from and the audience of the turn that asked |
| Tool call | A bot tool call pinned to the turn and audience it came from and to the engine's record of the call, with its result and, for a teammate message, the teammate it reached |
| Engine request | For an approval, an engine question or a secret request: the session, the engine's request id and the turn it belongs to; for a secret request, whether a submission may be in flight, never its value |
| Catch-up note | What a bot's main session has not seen yet, consumed by its next main-session dispatch, and for a routine run's message, that message's place in the conversation |
| Held message | A non-urgent teammate message waiting for the recipient's next envelope: its sender, its text, its hop count and the handoff card it put in the recipient's conversation |
| Teammate message | Where a teammate message stands in its pair's teammate conversation: the pair (its two bot ids, sorted), the sender and recipient, its two handoff cards while their bots exist, and its place among all teammate messages ([Teammate conversations](#teammate-conversations)) |
| Blob | File bytes, stored once per digest: size and where they are stored |
| Attachment | A file the operator sent or a bot produced: a name and a media type over a blob |
| Routine | Bot, name, instruction, trigger, enabled, next run, run count, webhook secret, and the runs it missed since its last run started (how many, the first and the last). Its runs: status, when a scheduled run was due, start and end, the wake of its latest attempt, how many attempts started and when the next may start, trigger data, the webhook request's idempotency key, and why its last attempt failed when J3 Bots can name it |
| Engine instance | The engine the sessions and the bots' projects belong to, recorded at each time the engine becomes ready, or none: an engine this home does not have, as after an import ([Another engine](#another-engine)). One row |
| Device | A paired client: its token hash, when it was revoked |
| Push registration | Where to push to one device: the push service, the token, the platform, the language to write in, when it was registered, its generation (new at each registration, also one made again with the same token) and how many sends failed since. One per device at most, deleted with the device's revocation |
| Push service | When the next send to the push service may begin, after a request it did not answer. One row |
| Push delivery | One notification to push to one device about one entry: its words until the push service takes it, with and without a preview, how many sends began, the generation of the registration the last went to, the service's receipt, when it is next due and the deadline of its stage. Deleted once it settles |

A column that holds one of a fixed set of values carries a `CHECK` listing them, as a push registration's provider and platform do. SQLite cannot change a `CHECK` in place, so a new push provider means rebuilding that table, in the migration that comes with its sender ([decision 0014](../decisions/0014-push-goes-through-a-push-sender.md)).

Migrations are numbered and applied in order, each once, all in one transaction. They run with foreign keys off, which is what rebuilding a table needs: SQLite cannot drop a column a table `CHECK` names, and dropping a table that other rows reference would otherwise delete or clear those rows. Before the transaction commits, `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` must find every reference naming a row that exists, or nothing is applied; foreign keys are back on once it ends. Migration 0032 rebuilds `rooms` this way, without the host and the hop limit it had and with the count of its bots' messages, every room's at 0 (0034 drops that count for rounds kept with each message); in the same step it renames the room wake reasons it replaced, `host` to `user` and `mentioned` to `addressed`. The migrator applies only those numbered past the last one the database recorded, so the server refuses to start on a database that recorded a later migration than one it lacks, naming the one missing, rather than run without it. It also refuses a database that records a migration it does not know, naming it: a newer release migrated it, and `j3-bots update --rollback` puts back the database from before ([decision 0016](../decisions/0016-an-update-keeps-a-snapshot-to-roll-back-to.md)).

Entries are ordered by `order`, set when the entry is created and never changed. The change sequence that clients resume subscriptions with is separate: it records when an entry changed, not where it sits.

**The filesystem** (`<J3_HOME>/bots/<bot>/`) holds what a bot reads and writes with its own tools, and what the operator may want to open in an editor:

```
bots/scout/
  .j3/
    system.md        generated: base prompt + profile + pinned memory
  AGENTS.md          generated: the same identity for harnesses that read it
  memory/
    profile.md       small, always in front of the bot
    log/2026-11.md   dated history
  skills/            procedures, one folder each
  .claude/skills/    a link to each of them, for Claude Code
  .agents/skills/    the same, for Codex
  files/             what the bot produces
shared/
  memory/
    user.md          facts about the operator every bot can read
    log/2026-11.md   shared dated history
  skills/            skills available to every bot, the shipped `j3-` ones among them
providers/
  claude-code/       Claude Code's home for every bot (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`)
    skills/          a link to each shared skill, made at every engine start and each change
  codex/             Codex's home for every bot (`CODEX_HOME`)
    skills/          the same
harnesses/
  claude-code/       J3 Bots' own copy of Claude Code, when it installed one
    current.json     the version the provider runs, and its executable in that version's folder
    state.json       the newest version the maker listed at the last check, and how the last install ended
    versions/<v>/    an installed copy, checked; only the current one stays
    <v>.partial      a download cut off, resumed by the next install of that version
    <v>.staging/     where a download is unpacked, before it moves into versions/<v>/
  codex/             the same for Codex
logs/
  server.log         the server's own log, JSON lines; moved aside to server.log.1 to .4 at 4 MiB each
state/
  shipped-skills.json  the SHA-256 of each shipped skill text the server wrote
  runtime.json       the release this home's data was last handed to, whether by a rollback, and a transition under way
  release-feed.json  the newest release each channel last offered, whether it verified, and when the feed checks next
vault/               private to the server's user; in no snapshot
  vault.sqlite       the operator's sealed secrets and the connectors' ([Secrets](#secrets))
  key                the data key they are sealed under
backups/
  <snapshot>/        the databases and settings a version transition copied before it changed release, never the secrets or their key
  update.lock        held by the one version transition or rollback running
```

Generated files are written by the server from the database and the memory files. A bot never edits them by hand, and the server rewrites them whenever their inputs change.

**Private state stays in the home.** Every location the server writes (each entry of the home but `web`, the shared memory and skills, each bot's workspace with its memory and generated files, the attachments, and the engine's folder with the folders the engine adapter declares it writes there, `j3/` among them) must resolve, symbolic links followed, inside the home's real path and outside the web client's folder. A bot's `skills/` and `files/` are its own: the server creates them once, and writes there only the operator's files it copies into `files/inbox/`, each through the check below, so a link there is the bot's business and refuses nothing at start. Start refuses a home where a written location does not resolve as it should, naming it. Every write of private state, the adapter's included, checks its folder the same way first, then writes a new file (mode 0600) of a random name there that must not exist yet and renames it into place, so a link made while the server runs (by a bot's own tools, say) is never followed and cannot publish memory or a generated file through the web client. The engine download and the upload of a screen recording are the files written in place, so that an interrupted one resumes: each must be a regular file, its folder and itself are checked the same way, and it is opened so that a link at its name is refused, even one made after the check. Windows has no such way to open a file, so there a link made after the check is followed; making a symbolic link there takes a privilege an ordinary user does not hold unless Developer Mode is on. A removal (a deleted bot's memory and generated files, an attachment's bytes once nothing refers to them) checks the folder above what it removes the same way, and removes a link there rather than following it. A refused write or removal is logged, and only that bot's workspace or memory, the attachments, or the engine's folder is affected: a memory write or an upload fails with `Conflict("private-location")`, also when a folder inside the memory, such as `log/`, is the link; a file copied into `files/inbox/` fails its staging; a generated file or a removal is skipped; the engine is not installed; and every other bot carries on. What this rule does and does not defend against is in [Security](security.md). To keep state on another disk, put the whole home there.

Memory lives in files and not in the database because the bot's own tools can read, search and quote files, and because the operator can read, edit, diff and back them up without J3 Bots. See [decision 0006](../decisions/0006-memory-is-files.md).

## The wake pipeline

Everything a bot does starts as a **wake**. There is one pipeline for all five kinds, and it is the only path to the engine.

```
 operator message ─┐
 room message ─────┤
 teammate message ─┼──> wake (persisted) ──> held by the server ──> dispatch (persisted) ──> engine session ──> turn
 routine trigger ──┤                              │
 system event ─────┘                              └── priority, coalescing, concurrency
```

### 1. A wake is recorded before anything happens

Creating a wake and the entry that caused it (the operator's message, the room message, the answered card) is one transaction. A wake that exists will be dispatched or visibly cancelled; one that does not exist never was requested. If the server stops between recording and dispatching, it picks the wake up again at start.

A bot created without a model gets the first model of the first provider that can run it now, unless another bot's `create_bot` made it: then it takes its maker's model, the same provider and model, while the engine offers that model and its provider is ready, so a team runs where the bot that made it does. Otherwise, it gets the default as any other bot does: the first model of the first provider that can run it now; with none ready, of the first that is at its limit, then signed out, then still in the engine's first check since it started, then one whose harness is not installed yet, among those that list a model. The last is there because the server installs a missing harness itself as it starts ([J3 Bots' own harnesses](#j3-bots-own-harnesses), [decision 0064](../decisions/0064-the-harnesses-install-as-the-server-starts-and-fail-with-the-true-reason.md)), and the engine lists Claude Code's catalog also when Claude Code is not installed (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `checkClaudeProviderStatus` in `ClaudeProvider.ts`): the operator's first bot is made while its AI still downloads, and its opening waits for a provider as below ([decision 0066](../decisions/0066-connecting-an-ai-is-picking-the-account-you-have.md)). A provider the engine found and cannot run, one that failed to start or is turned off, is never the default, though the engine lists Claude Code's catalog then too: a bot put there would wait for a provider nothing is bringing. So a first bot is not refused for want of a connected provider: its opening waits for one, as below. An engine with no such provider refuses it with `Invalid("model")`. A bot created without a permission mode gets Auto on a provider whose harness has the engine's reviewer (Claude Code and Codex; **Assumed** for any other harness), Supervised otherwise.

The bot records whether the server chose its model and its permission mode, or the operator did (`bots.model_by_server`, `bots.permission_mode_by_server`); a bot created before that was recorded counts as the operator's on both. A bot whose model the server chose moves to the default model while the engine does not have its opening yet (its wake pending, or in a dispatch never sent), its provider is not ready and the default model's provider is. The server looks for such bots in one pass at a time, each bot in the order it was created: whenever a provider's state changes or it lists its first model or its last one goes (a ready provider whose catalog fills in has not changed state), and, for the new bot alone, after each creation. Each bot's move is decided and saved in one transaction, inside the gate of its main session, from the engine's state as it is then: a bot the operator gave a model meanwhile, one deleted meanwhile, or one whose new provider stopped being ready meanwhile is left as it is, so the operator's own choice always wins. The change is applied as the operator's change of provider is applied (below) and recorded as the server's choice; when the server also chose the permission mode, the bot takes the new provider's default mode, recorded the same way. A bot that cannot move is logged, and neither stops the pass nor fails a creation. So a Codex-only operator whose first bot landed on Claude Code while both were still being checked gets the hello once Codex is ready. A model the operator chose, at creation or since, is never moved.

Creating a bot writes its first wake with it, in the same transaction: a `first-run` system wake for its conversation, so the bot's first turn opens the conversation ([Experience](../product/experience.md#first-run)). Its envelope says that none of the operator's bots knows what to call them while the operator has no name and no other bot's `first-run` wake is dispatched or has finished without failing: one whose turn failed, was stopped or was lost asked nobody, retried or not. This is read in the transaction that writes the dispatch, from the wakes and their dispatches, so the operator is asked by the first bot to say hello, again when that same opening is retried, and by the next bot to say hello when every opening before it failed. A bot deleted with its wakes leaves no trace of having asked, so a bot created after it may ask once more. That is the only place one is written: a retried `bot.create` finds its receipt, and a restart, a replaced session, a fresh start or a change of model write none. A bot made from a template records so on its wake (`fromTemplate`), and its envelope says that the template's author, not the operator, wrote its job, skills and routines, so its hello starts none of that work. A bot the primary bot creates with `create_bot` gets one too; its cause is the operator's, as the creation is. Its wake records the turn that created it (`madeIn`) and waits until that turn has finished, or its session was retired or lost: a `start_room` later in the same turn may place the bot in a room, and then cancels the hello in its own transaction ([Rooms](#rooms)). The wake waits like any pending wake until the bot's main session is opened and can take it, and an operator wake goes first. It also waits while the engine reports the bot's provider as anything but ready (signed out, at its limit, unavailable), or does not report it at all, as while the engine is down or just after it started: a turn that fails before the provider starts helps nobody, and the dispatcher looks again as soon as a provider's state changes. An opening already written into a dispatch never sent waits at its first send the same way, as one a restart finds, or one written just before its provider stopped being ready: the send reads the provider's state again, in its claim's transaction too, and releases the dispatch, so the hello waits as a pending wake and the bot can still move. The wait has no deadline: the provider turning ready, a move, the operator's change of model or their Stop ends it. What ends it before it runs:

- **The operator writes first.** Whether the opening gives way depends on whether it waits for its provider:
  - **Withdrawn.** Ordinarily, `message.send` to the bot's conversation cancels the opening in its own transaction while it is pending, and also once it is written into a dispatch never sent: that dispatch is released, which the send's claim cannot race, since a claim needs it still `intended` with no attempt. Otherwise the envelope would say the operator has not written. A dispatch sent at least once may be in the engine, so it runs, and the operator's message reaches the same turn as a steer or the next one; the base prompt tells the bot to answer a message that reaches it in that turn instead of greeting.
  - **Held.** While the opening is not with the engine and its provider is not ready, or while it already holds a message, it is not cancelled: the message's wake records the opening it waits behind (`wakes.behind_opening_id`) in the transaction that writes it, and so does every message after it until the opening has ended, so neither a later message nor the provider turning ready withdraws the hello. A held wake is not dispatchable while its opening has not ended: it never joins the opening's envelope or steers its turn. The opening has ended once its wake has (finished, failed, refused or cancelled) and no turn that delivers it still runs: a hello that says it is done finishes its wake while the engine still runs its turn, and the held wakes wait for that turn's end. A hello whose turn stopped at a usage limit, with a continuation pending, has ended: the held messages go as any operator message would beside that continuation, which the engine then drops ([Continuations](#5-the-outcome-closes-the-dispatch)). Once the opening has ended, the held wakes are ordinary `user` wakes in the order they were written, which go as the bot's next turn: the bot greets, then answers. Until the opening goes to the engine, each such message's `pending` delivery says it waits behind the hello (`behindOpening`), so clients can say what it waits for ([Experience](../product/experience.md#first-run)); once the hello has gone, the message shows as any queued one, and again behind the hello if the hello comes back unsent: its send given back, released, refused or dropped.
- **The operator's Stop and a fresh start** cancel it like any pending wake, with the messages held behind it.
- **A provider change does not.** Its Stop spares the opening, and the operator's messages held behind it while it has not ended: a pending one stays pending, and one written into a dispatch never sent is released back to pending, so the hello runs on the new provider, the messages after it. Once the opening has ended, a message that was held behind it is ordinary work, and the Stop cancels it like any other. A bot with no live main session gets no Stop at all, so it is the same either way. An opening the engine may already have is stopped with the rest of the work.

If the opening's dispatch fails before its turn starts, the wake has no message to show it, so the conversation shows a retryable `turn-failed` notice, as for an `ask` answer ([3. A dispatch is written](#3-a-dispatch-is-written-before-the-engine-is-called)). If its turn fails, or its session is lost while the turn runs, the notice offers a retry like an operator's turn. Either retry records the `first-run` wake again. Once the operator has written in the conversation, no opening is offered again: `message.send` takes the retry off every notice for it, a notice written later has none, and `notice.retry` refuses one a client still shows. An opening that ends without a word is only a quiet bot: no `undelivered` follow-up and no notice.

### 2. The server holds waiting wakes

J3 Bots owns waiting and priority. A pending wake stays in the server's database until its session can take it; J3 Bots never asks the engine to queue a message. The engine still queues a start that meets a turn it began itself, and the server follows that (`queued`, below). Each wake has a priority from its kind: `user` first, then `room`, `bot`, `system`, and `routine`, and within a priority they go in the order they were written, which also orders the messages in one envelope. The operator's message in a room is a `room` wake with the priority of a `user` one: it goes before a bot's room wakes, and it may steer as a `user` wake does. Wakes written in one transaction share a time, so the order is the database's own insertion order, never the time or the id.

For each pending wake the dispatcher applies, in order:

- **Readiness.** A session the engine no longer has is skipped ([Sessions](#sessions)). A wake is dispatched only into an idle session: no admitted turn, no dispatch that may be in the engine and is not yet finished (sent but unanswered, accepted, or delivered), and no turn the engine started on its own; a pending continuation holds back every wake but the operator's. The one exception is below: a wake at `user` priority for a running turn with the same audience. Nothing at all is dispatched into a session while a Stop of it is unsettled ([Stop](#stop)). Nothing is planned into a session whose events are not known yet ([Observation](#4-the-turn-is-observed-and-admitted)), since they decide how it can take the wake, nor into one whose first send was refused as busy, before its busy deadline passes or its next event is applied ([Refusals](#3-a-dispatch-is-written-before-the-engine-is-called)). Each of these holds back only that session's wakes: the dispatcher goes on with every other bot.
- **Rooms.** The members of a room run in parallel ([decision 0040](../decisions/0040-the-members-of-a-room-run-in-parallel.md)). A room wake starts a turn as any other wake does, when its member's main session can take one (**Readiness**, **Concurrency**), whatever another member runs for the room, so the operator's message starts every member it wakes at once, as far as the turn slots allow, in the order the wakes were written. A member runs one turn at a time in its main session, so it never runs two for the same room: what reaches it while its room turn runs waits, and its waiting wakes for the room share its next envelope (**Coalescing**). The operator's room message steers each member's running turn for that room (the table below), and the steer, a room envelope like any other, carries with it what the room said since the member's last envelope, the other members' replies included: their waiting wakes go with it. A member whose permission mode the engine refuses takes no wake until the operator acts ([Sessions](#sessions), Every bot has a main session), and the room shows one `member-mode-refused` line naming it; what was said to it reaches it once, after. The transaction that writes a room dispatch reads the room again: an archived room's wakes, and a former member's, are cancelled there instead. The transaction that claims a room start's first send checks the bot is still a member, and releases it otherwise. A room start that cannot be sent when it is due, for want of a turn slot or because a usage limit holds it (**Blocked providers**), is released before anything of it is staged rather than wait unsent: its envelope carries the room as it was when it was written, and the other members may speak for as long as it waits. Its wakes wait as pending wakes and are planned again, with what was said meanwhile, once what held them is gone.
- **Coalescing.** Pending wakes of a bot with the same kind and the same audience that are ready for the same session become one dispatch: three messages in one room that arrived while the bot was busy are delivered together. Wakes for different audiences never share a dispatch, so one turn always speaks to one place. A room envelope is cut differently, and never fails for its size ([Rooms](#rooms)). Coalescing stops at the engine's limits for one message: its files together (`attachmentLimitViolation`) and the length of the rendered envelope (`engineInputLength` against `ENGINE_INPUT_LIMIT`, 120,000 characters, measured as the engine measures it, after expanding any assistant-quote link). The envelope holds the head wake, then as much of what the session has not seen as fits, oldest first, then as many of the other wakes as fit, in order. What is left out waits for the next dispatch in the same order, so a batch that does not fit becomes consecutive dispatches. A single wake always fits: every path that writes the operator's text into a wake checks that wake alone before it commits, with the same margin of 1,000 characters under the limit, which covers what renders longer at dispatch, such as the date, the zone and a room's name. `message.send` checks each message's files and its envelope (`Invalid("message-length")`, nothing written), a room message's as long as it can render: in the room at its widest (the longest name, as many members as a room can have, and the longest names, titles and ids), since the room may be renamed or given members before the wake goes, with the longest closing line and a line counting entries left out; `card.answer` checks an `ask` answer's envelope (`Invalid("answer-length")`, the card left open for another answer), a room card's with the longest room name. A retry sends again a wake that passed that check, and a system event is far below the limit. The dispatcher still never lets a wake hold the queue: a head wake that does not fit alone gets a dispatch of its own that fails as `rejected` without being sent, so it shows where it came from as a refused dispatch does ([Refusals](#3-a-dispatch-is-written-before-the-engine-is-called)), and the wakes behind it go on. Something the session has not seen that would not fit even beside the smallest wake, a system event, is left out for good, in order, with a `too-long` notice in the bot's conversation; both cards of a teammate's message left out so show `not-delivered`, and keep showing it whatever becomes of the urgent wake it was held from.
- **Concurrency.** At most N turns run at once across all bots. A slot is held by every session with a turn running or a dispatch the engine may have (sent and unanswered, accepted or delivered, a pending continuation included). A dispatch never sent holds none: it takes a slot when it is sent, and the slots are counted again before that send, so a session that cannot take its work yet never keeps a slot another bot could use. Every start is admitted by the same rule, whether it is planned now or sent again after its files failed to stage: a slot is free, or its session already holds one, as an operator's message beside a pending continuation does. The rule is read when the start is planned, and again in the transaction that claims its first send, since the budget or the sessions holding it may change while its files stage: a start refused then stays unsent, for the dispatcher's next pass, except a room start, which is released (**Rooms**). Higher-priority wakes take a free slot first, and only work that can take a slot now competes for it: a routine's start, which the routine runner writes ([Routines](#routines)), is sent only after every bot's waiting wakes were planned in the same pass, and not while its session's provider is at its usage limit (**Blocked providers**, below).
- **Blocked providers.** If the bot's provider is at its usage limit, every wake but the operator's waits for the reset: a routine's, a teammate's message, and a room wake a bot's `say` caused alike. The engine's status may say so; when it does not, the turns the server saw end on that provider, in any session, each on its session's provider, do: the newest one that completed or failed at the usage limit, when it failed with a reset still ahead. An index over exactly those turns, newest first, keeps that read short. A wake held so is looked at again at the reset, when the limit says when that is, and as soon as the engine reports the provider's status changed. A start of such work written before the limit was reached, and not sent yet, is released instead, and the transaction that claims its first send checks the limit again, since it may have been reached while the files staged: unsent, it would keep its session busy, and the operator's word to the bot would wait behind it until the reset. Its wakes go back to pending, where they are held as above. A routine's start, in a side session that takes nothing else, is held unsent until the reset instead. An operator wake is dispatched anyway so the operator gets the engine's own limit message. A `first-run` wake waits while the engine reports the bot's provider as anything but ready (1. A wake is recorded).

| Wake | The session is idle | The session is in a turn |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `user`, and the operator's `room` wake | Start a turn | If the running turn has the same audience (the conversation, or the room the operator wrote in), steer it, so they can redirect work in progress. Otherwise wait, first in line, so a private message never lands in a room turn; the operator's later word for the running turn's audience still steers it. A wake carrying an `ask` answer always waits ([Questions](#questions-approvals-and-secrets)) |
| A bot's `room` wake | Start a turn | Wait |
| `bot`, `system` | Start a turn | Wait |
| `routine` | Start a turn in a new side session | Not applicable: side sessions run alongside the main one |

### 3. A dispatch is written before the engine is called

Before anything is written, a message's files are checked against the engine's limits (`attachmentLimitViolation`: no empty file, inline images at most 10 MiB each and 80 MiB together). A message that breaks one is refused with nothing written. A file the engine does not take with a message (`engineCarries`: any but an inline image over 50 MiB), such as a screen recording, counts toward none of them: it reaches the bot through its workspace alone.

In one transaction the dispatcher renders the envelope, writes a **dispatch** with the target session, the audience, the delivery (start, or steer with the turn), that envelope and its files in order, links the coalesced wakes to it, and consumes the bot's catch-up note, held messages and what apps set as context for it ([Connector apps](#connectors)) into the envelope. A dispatch never changes its session, its audience or its envelope afterwards; the database refuses it.

Then, inside the session's gate ([One gate per session](#one-gate-per-session)), it reads the dispatch again and goes on only if it is still due: still `intended`, its deadline passed, and, for a first send, its session's events known and unfenced in a session with no unsettled Stop. A resend waits for no observation: its key makes the engine apply it once whatever the server knows yet, and a Stop may be waiting on its answer. A steer goes only into the turn it was planned for: if that turn is no longer the session's admitted turn when its first send comes, the dispatch is released and its wakes wait for the next dispatch. Before a first send, it copies each file into the bot's workspace, at `files/inbox/<attachment id>/<name>`, where the envelope names it beside the name the operator gave: the folder of its own keeps two files apart, and the name is made one path segment (a name made only of dots becomes `_`) of at most 200 bytes in UTF-8, shortened if need be with its extension kept, so the bot can open it with its own tools, replacing whatever is there. The copy streams, so a recording of gigabytes is never held in memory. Before each copy it reads the dispatch again: one settled meanwhile, or a room start whose bot left the room while an earlier file staged, gets nothing more, and is released if it is still `intended`. A resend copies nothing, since the engine applies the envelope at most once and the inbox is the bot's since. It stages each file the engine carries in the engine (`stageAttachment`) and stores the id the engine minted, and stages none of the others, which the envelope names in the workspace alone; **claims** the attempt, and calls `dispatch` with the dispatch id as the key. The claim is one conditional update, right before the call, that raises the attempt count only if the dispatch is still `intended` and at the attempt count read before staging, and, for a first send, unfenced, in a session with no unsettled Stop that is neither retired nor lost, and, for a first start, admitted by the turn budget as it stands then ([Concurrency](#2-the-server-holds-waiting-wakes)). A first steer is checked again in the same transaction: if the turn it was planned for ended while its files were staging, the engine would make it a turn of its own, outside the budget, so the dispatch is released instead and its wakes go through a start's admission. When the claim changes nothing, the dispatch was settled meanwhile (a Stop fenced it while its files were staging, say) and the engine is not called. A resend claims whatever the fence or the Stop: the key may already be in the engine, and only the recovery can learn its answer. A staging the engine did not answer, or a file the store no longer has, is tried again with the recovery's backoff, by whichever worker owns the send: the dispatcher for a first send, under the turn budget like any other, and the recovery for a resend. Each worker hands the sender its own claim to the send, and the sender checks it again inside the gate before anything else: a first attempt given back because the engine was unreachable makes the dispatch the dispatcher's again, so a recovery that read it while it was claimed leaves it alone; after five failures the dispatch fails, as `rejected` when it was never sent and as `unresolved` when it was. A staging refused because the engine is unreachable counts no failure: the dispatch waits for the engine. A send that fails unexpectedly counts against the same bounds and is never tried again at once: before the claim, as a staging failure, since nothing was sent; after it, as an indeterminate answer for the recovery ([Recovery](#3-a-dispatch-is-written-before-the-engine-is-called)), since the call may have reached the engine. A defect that repeats so ends visibly within its bound, and a Stop that waits on the dispatch is not held for a day. The dispatcher and the recovery read their candidate rows one by one, so a row that cannot be read is the only one held back. When counting the defect fails too, or the defect comes before the gate (a dispatch that cannot be read, say), one write that touches nothing but the deadline of a dispatch still `intended` puts it thirty seconds ahead, so even then it is never retried at once, and a broken dispatch stays deferred while the others go on. A send into a session that is gone settles at once, without waiting out a backoff. The adapter derives the engine's command id and message id from that key ([Engine: the adapter's interface](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)), so sending the same dispatch again is the same command. The staged ids are the one input that may change between sends: a file is staged again whenever its `usableUntil` has passed before a send, including a recovery, because the engine claims the files before it looks at the key; a copy a recovery does not use is removed by the engine. A retried staging after a lost reply leaves an unused copy the same way.

The engine's answer records **acceptance**. Its first disposition is stored and never changes:

| Disposition | Meaning | What the server does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `started` | A new turn started | The turn is admitted when its `turn-started` arrives |
| `queued` | A turn the engine began while the start was on its way runs first; this message's turn waits behind it | The turn is admitted when its `turn-started` arrives, with this dispatch's audience, whatever the turn ahead of it was for |
| `steered` | The message was given to that turn | The dispatch waits for the steer to settle (see below) |
| `new-turn` | A steer arrived too late, or the provider can take no steer: the message became its own turn, possibly after the running one | The turn is admitted when its `turn-started` arrives |

A dispatch accepted through a recovery has no disposition: the engine does not say how a key it already had was applied. The events say where it went, as for any other.

**Delivery** is what the events say, not the disposition: a dispatch is delivered when `turn-started` arrives with the cause `dispatch` and its key, or, for a steer, when it settles as taken. A steer settles only after the turn it steered has ended, with one event right after that turn's `turn-finished`, decided by the engine's own state: `steer-requeued` (the engine queued it as a follow-up turn: the dispatch and its wakes move to that turn, which is admitted when it starts), `steer-dropped` (Stop ended the turn first: the dispatch is dropped, and the engine shows its own notice) or otherwise `steered` (taken: the dispatch finishes with that turn's outcome). `steered` is the engine's claim; on some provider paths it cannot prove the model read the message ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)). While the turn runs, `steer-taken` says when the turn took the steer, once the engine no longer holds it back for a follow-up turn; it settles nothing, and only budgets count it ([Budgets](#budgets)). Until it settles the dispatch is accepted and undecided, and no `undelivered` wake fires for it. A steer rides a turn it does not own: it finishes with that turn and never follows its continuation, which belongs to the dispatch that owns the turn.

Events about a key can arrive before the engine's answer does, after a lost reply. They prove it applied: the server records the dispatch as accepted, with no disposition, and applies them. `turn-finished` names its turn's cause as `turn-started` does, so a turn that ends without starting (cancelled from the engine's queue, or failed before its provider took it) still settles its dispatch by key, including a dispatch the server knows only through a recovery, which names no turn.

**Refusals.** Only two answers prove that the engine applied nothing, and only on a dispatch's first send, which they refuse before anything is sent:

- `SessionBusy`: the adapter already saw a turn in the session. It is a best-effort check; a turn that begins just after it gives `queued` instead. The adapter saw a turn the server has not applied yet, so the session gets a **busy deadline**: nothing is planned into it before the deadline passes, two seconds doubling with each refusal in a row up to thirty, or before its next event is applied, which clears the deadline and nudges the dispatcher. A deadline rather than a mark, so a turn whose last event committed before the refusal was recorded never strands the session.
- `SessionNotFound`: the adapter has no such session. The session is recorded **lost** ([Sessions](#sessions)).

Either one **releases** the dispatch: its wakes wait again, and its catch-up notes, held messages and apps' context go back for the next envelope. `EngineUnavailable` proves the same and gives the attempt back.

`PermissionModeRefused` proves it too, on a first send or a recovery alike: the engine refuses to run a turn in the permission mode its session has, as the ceiling of an administrator's policy installed after the session was opened in that mode does, and keeps no receipt for the key, so neither this send nor an earlier one of the key was applied ([Engine: the adapter's interface](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)). In the bot's main session the dispatch is released, and in the same transaction the bot records the refusal as for a refused opening (**Every bot has a main session**, below): nothing is planned into the session until the bot's settings change or the engine is ready again, its conversation is told once, and the message waits as `pending`. Its wakes then go in a new dispatch, under a key of its own, so it is delivered once. In a side session, the routine's attempt fails as for a side session the engine refused to open ([Routines](#routines)).

`DispatchRejected` means the engine's reply named a refusal, so it applied nothing and the key is spent. The dispatch **fails** as `rejected`, and its wakes fail visibly: each shows, where it came from, that it did not reach the bot. They never return to pending on their own, because the same wakes would be refused the same way; `message.retry` sends an operator message again as new wakes, under a new key. An `ask` answer, like a new bot's `first-run` wake, has no message of its own, so its failure shows as a retryable `turn-failed` notice where it was for, written in the same transaction as the failure, whether the dispatch failed or the engine dropped the turn it was to start. Its class says why: `stopped` when a room's Stop dropped it, `engine-unavailable` when its send was never resolved, `unknown` otherwise. Whether such a notice offers a retry is one rule, read again from durable state after every change to its dispatch or that dispatch's wakes, and again when the retry is pressed: the dispatch failed or the engine dropped it, so the engine cannot still have the answer and never delivered it, and the answer's (or the opening's) wake failed and was not sent again. A key the engine accepts after all takes the retry away whichever arrives first, its reply or its events; the engine dropping it before its turn started gives the retry back; a delivery ends it. A notice for a turn that ran and failed is retried on its own terms ([The outcome](#5-the-outcome-closes-the-dispatch)).

**Catch-up notes and held messages** go back only when the engine certainly never applied the dispatch that consumed them: it was released, proved never sent, failed as `rejected`, or dropped by the engine. An `unresolved` dispatch keeps them: it may still land and deliver its envelope, and a note handed to the next envelope as well would be told twice. A routine run's note from before the bot's latest fresh start does not go back: it is dropped ([Sessions](#sessions)).

**Recovery.** When the server cannot tell whether a dispatch reached the engine (it crashed during the call, or the call failed with `EngineIndeterminate`), it recovers the dispatch with `redispatch`: the same key, envelope, delivery and files, with none of `dispatch`'s local checks, because the key may itself have started the turn that makes the session busy. A lost reply is never taken as "not applied", and a dispatch with no recorded attempt was never sent, which is the only other proof of that. A recovery that succeeds means accepted, with no disposition. One that answers `DispatchRejected` fails the dispatch as `rejected`, and one that answers `PermissionModeRefused` releases it as above. Anything else is tried again with backoff, doubling from two seconds to ten minutes; while the engine is unreachable the server waits until it is ready again, or 30 seconds, since the engine's status can trail it, and the wait counts no attempt. Three things end the recovery without an answer: ten indeterminate answers, the session being retired, lost or removed (a rotation, a fresh start, a deleted bot, a session the engine no longer has), or the dispatch being older than a day. A session lost after the engine accepted the dispatch, and before its turn ran, ends it the same way: no event will ever report the turn, so it fails as `unresolved` and keeps the engine's first answer. A recovery that fails unexpectedly counts as an indeterminate answer. The engine keeps its receipts for good, so the day is J3 Bots' own bound, matching how long the engine keeps a session's events. The dispatch then fails as `unresolved` and its wakes fail visibly, never re-queued.

**Late delivery is legal.** If a turn later names a failed dispatch's key, the engine had applied it after all: the dispatch moves on to `delivered` and its turn goes to the dispatch's audience, and its wakes and messages show as delivered. If the events show the engine holding it before its turn starts (a `turn-queued`, or a replacement that lists its turn waiting), it is `accepted` again, and its messages show as pending with no retry: a failed wake counts as failed only while its dispatch is `failed` or `dropped`, and a message's retry sends only such wakes, so nothing the engine may still deliver is sent twice. A message the operator retried meanwhile went again under a new key, by their choice. A Stop recorded over such a dispatch's work still covers it: the Stop fences it, so its landing reopens the Stop and the turn is stopped on sight ([Stop](#stop)), while the dispatch stays failed and is the recovery's no more. A dispatch moves to a replacement session only through retried wakes. The server never guesses, so a wake is never delivered twice by J3 Bots' own doing, and never silently lost.

### One gate per session

Every step that sends a session something runs through that session's **gate**: one serial lock per session in the server process. A dispatch, a recovery, a held-queue resume, a Stop round and a room archival's Stop each take the gate, read the state they need inside it, make their engine call and record the result in one transaction, and release it. They never hold it while they wait for events or back off. So a Stop recorded while a dispatch waits for the gate fences that dispatch before it is sent, a resume never runs between a Stop's commands, and two recoveries of one key never overlap. Different sessions do not share a gate. A Stop round records durably, inside the gate and before its call, that it was issued.

### The workers

Seven workers act on durable state: the **dispatcher** (pending wakes and every first send, also one whose files wait to be staged again), the **recovery** (every claimed send: dispatches sent at least once whose answer was lost), the **Stop runner**, the **responder** (the operator's answers to engine requests), the **resumer** of held queues, the **rotator** (main sessions to open, align or replace, and every retired session's archive, [Sessions](#sessions)) and the **routine runner** (routine runs and their side sessions, [Routines](#routines)). Each is a reconciler. A pass reads what is due from the database and does it; nothing it needs lives only in memory. A signal from whoever changed that state only shortens the wait before the next pass: a pass also runs at the earliest durable deadline it knows of (a recovery's backoff, a resume's or an answer's retry, a busy deadline, a routine run's next attempt), five seconds after a pass that left due work undone (a session not ready, a call that timed out), and once a minute otherwise. Each of these is counted from when the pass began, not from when it ended: work that comes due while a pass runs is done as soon as the pass ends, and a pass that ran long has spent that much of its wait. A signal fired before a pass begins is answered by that pass, and causes no pass after it. So a timeout, a readiness change or a lost signal never leaves work waiting for good, and a pass that changed nothing schedules nothing extra: signals are fired only when state changed. Passes of one worker run one at a time, also those the startup sequence runs. Outside the turn machinery, the **push worker** ([Notifications](#notifications)) is a reconciler in the same way; it sends nothing to the engine, so it never waits for the engine to be ready.

**Sessions are isolated.** A session that is lost, not yet known from its events, within a busy deadline, held by a Stop or not observable (its events cannot be applied, or a marker did not come in time) holds back only its own work: the pass skips it and goes on. Only the engine refusing a command (`EngineUnavailable`) ends a pass, since nothing else can be sent either; that worker then waits until the engine is ready again, or thirty seconds, since the engine's status can trail it. Ready again means since the pass began: an engine that came back while the refused pass was still running releases the worker at once. A catch-up (`synchronize`) that fails the same way is that session's alone: the adapter's catch-up of one stream failed or timed out, so the Stop runner and the resumer defer that session and go on.

**Each worker is supervised.** A defect in one item (a send, one session's Stop round, one answer, one resume) is logged and the pass goes on with the rest, and the item waits for a durable deadline. A send's defect counts against the dispatch's bounds, so it ends visibly; one the sender could not count is caught at the send itself, in the dispatcher and the recovery alike, and the pass looks again in thirty seconds. An answer or a resume, whose key makes every send of it one command, is sent again on the backoff of a lost reply, as long as its request is open or its hold is current. A session's Stop work waits thirty seconds, a deadline stored on its targets. A defect that escapes a pass is logged and the worker waits thirty seconds before its next pass. Every loop of the machinery (each of the five workers, the stall watcher, the engine status loop) is supervised on its own, the same way. A failure is never retried at once, and one loop's failure never stops another.

**Idempotent work retries for as long as it is due.** An answer to an engine request and a held queue's resume carry a key the engine applies once, so a lost reply is never final: each is sent again with the recovery's backoff, doubling up to ten minutes, its deadline stored with it, for as long as its request is open or its hold is the session's current one. A refusal is final, as for every keyed command: the engine keeps it for the key, so an answer it refused is never sent again ([Questions, approvals and secrets](#questions-approvals-and-secrets)). A dispatch is different: an unresolved one fails visibly at its bounds, because the operator must know that it may never arrive.

### 4. The turn is observed, and admitted

The adapter streams each session's events, and the server derives the bot's state from them: thinking, working, waiting on a request, blocked, idle. A working bot shows its newest step still running, since steps run in parallel: its label (`activity`) and its kind (`activityKind`), from which clients say in plain words what the bot is doing. A running turn with no step, tool call, request, speech or answer from the operator for ten minutes is shown as stalled, which a client says with no time. While the engine tries a turn's AI again after a failure (`turn-retrying`), the turn's state says so (`retrying`, stored on the turn): clients show, quietly and in place of the step, that the bot is having trouble reaching its AI and is trying again. A retry that works leaves nothing behind: no notice, no entry, and the line goes back to the step. An answer counts from when the engine recorded it (the close's `closedAt`), so a close the server sees again after a reconnect or a restart does not make the bot look active anew. Every state of a running turn carries the time the turn started, as the server recorded it, so a client knows a turn is under way, the same after a reload or on another device: thinking and working always, and waiting while the request it waits on holds a running turn, from the start of that request's own turn, whatever runs beside it; an `ask` card the bot waits on after its turn ended carries none. A bot can run a turn in its main session and a routine's in a side session at once: its state is the main session's turn while one runs, since that is the one its conversation shows, else the newest routine's. Clients see that state live. The server opens a session's stream before it relies on it: the engine answers `synchronize` only on the streams open when it is called, and a stream counts as open from when it is subscribed ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)), so a stream is started before anything waits for its marker. Nothing is planned or sent into a session before its state is known from a committed cursor, or from the first replacement once it is applied: until then the planner leaves its wakes pending, taking no slot, a send into it is skipped, and the event that makes it known nudges the dispatcher. Asking whether a session is known never opens its stream; the workers open it (`watch`) where they need it. Each event is applied and its cursor stored in one transaction, so a restart neither skips nor repeats one; once it commits the session is marked known before the change is published, so whoever sees the change finds the session ready. A wait for a marker is tied to its stream: it fails, to be retried, when the stream closes, and every `synchronize` has a deadline of ten seconds, shorter than the adapter's own ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)), so a stalled stream defers only its session's work. A stream that ends is opened again with backoff, doubling up to a minute; until then the session is not known, and nothing opens it sooner. So is a stream that could not be opened, and one whose end could not be handled: what was subscribed is closed, and nothing is left looking open. Waiters on a stream fail only once its end was handled, so one that retries finds the session as that end left it. Once a session is recorded lost, or its archive by the engine is recorded, checked inside each event's transaction, its stream is no longer read: what it had buffered is never applied. Whichever worker records it, the transaction nudges the observer, which closes the stream of every session it follows that is lost or archived, since a stream otherwise ends only with its next event, and the engine keeps the stream of a thread archived from its own console open with nothing more to say. An applied event nudges the dispatcher only when it can change whether the session is idle (a turn starting or finishing, a dispatch or steer settling, a replacement, a request opening or closing), when it ends a busy deadline, or when it makes the session known; it nudges the Stop runner only in the first case. An event the server cannot apply is not skipped: it is logged with its cause, shown once in the conversation as an `observation-failed` notice, and read again on each reopening. The notice is written in its own transaction and counts as shown only once it commits: one that cannot be written is logged, tried again the next time, and never keeps the stream from being opened again. Until an event of the session applies again, nothing is sent into it, because what the server knows of it is behind. A stream that ends with `SessionNotFound` records the session lost. A stream that resumes too late to replay starts with one replacement of the session's state, and the server reconciles against it by stable ids (turn, request, speech and dispatch key), so a turn, a speech or a resolution it already applied changes nothing when it comes again ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)).

A session has at most one **admitted turn**: its running turn, once the provider took it, with its **audience**, where its speech goes (the bot's conversation, or one room). A turn caused by a dispatch is admitted when it starts, with the dispatch's audience; a `queued` start keeps its own audience even when the turn ahead of it was a room's. A turn the engine started to continue another after a usage limit reset is admitted with the audience of the turn it continues ([Continuations](#5-the-outcome-closes-the-dispatch)). A turn the engine started for background work that finished is admitted with the bot's own conversation as its audience.

What the bot *says* does not come from the event stream. It comes from the bot calling J3 Bots' `say` tool, which appends an entry to the audience of the turn the call came from and returns ([Bot tools](#bot-tools)). A `say` or `ask` that cannot be matched to a call of the session's running turn fails with a typed `TurnEnded` result, and nothing is published or recorded. A call to the engine's own `send_to_user` tool arrives as host speech and is delivered once per speech id, like a `say` without `done` or `mention`, so a slip is not lost: in a room it takes its round, as a bot's message does, and wakes every other member, as such a `say` does ([Rooms](#rooms)).

A `say` with `done: true` **closes the delivery**: the delivered dispatch that owns the turn has its wakes finished, and the operator sees the bot's reply as delivered. A steer riding the turn is not closed by it: it settles as the engine reports. It does not interrupt the run. The bot is expected to end its turn; if it keeps working, what it says next still reaches the same audience. The server never interrupts a run to end it, because an interrupt may drop the delivery of a delegated task's completion ([Engine](engine.md#behaviors-j3-bots-designs-around)).

The turn's final reply text is never published: the base prompt tells the bot it reaches nobody, and the bot may have used it for notes. If a `user` wake's turn ends without a `say` carrying `done: true`, a pending question or a background handoff, the server wakes the bot once more with an `undelivered` system wake. If that turn also delivers nothing, the conversation shows a notice with a retry action. Neither comes for work a Stop covers ([Stop](#stop)).

### A turn's plan and its little ones

A turn's **work** is its plan and its little ones (decision 0070), from the engine's `turn-work` ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)):

- **The plan** is the checklist the bot keeps of what it is doing, as its harness's list of what is left to do last stood: each step with its text and whether it is to do, under way or done. A newer plan of the turn replaces it whole, and a turn that ended keeps its last one. A plan is cut to its first 100 steps, and a step to 300 characters.
- **A little one** is part of the turn's work the bot handed to a copy of itself that works beside it: a subagent of its harness, nested ones flattened into the turn's, or a task the engine delegated. It carries a few words of what it does (the harness's short description of it, else the first words of its instructions, never the instructions themselves, else nothing, which a client words), whether it works, is done or failed (stopped included), when it started and ended, and while it works the last line of progress the engine reports. Never a name of its own, nor what it found: the bot tells that. A turn's work names at most 16 little ones, every one still working first, then those that ended last, and counts all of them (`working`, `total`), so a client that draws four says how many more there are.

The server keeps a turn's work with the turn (`turns.work_json`), and sends it only to the turn's audience. An ended turn keeps every little one it listed: a later turn can take one up again, and the engine then lists it under that turn alone, so the earlier turn keeps it as it last saw it. A private turn's goes on its work fold, which it writes when the work is the turn's first thing to show, so a reopened conversation shows each turn's last plan. A room turn's goes on the room, by the member whose turn it is, while that turn runs or works on after it ends, the member's turns there showing as one (the latest plan, which can be an earlier turn's final one while the later turn has none yet, and every little one, one taken up again by the later turn counted once, as the later turn's); it is never on the member's work fold, which stays in its own conversation, and a room turn's work is not kept past it in the room. A routine's turn shows none. A turn that ended with a little one still working goes on telling it until it ends, and its work fold shows it working meanwhile ([decision 0095](../decisions/0095-little-ones-a-turn-handed-off-stay-until-they-are-home.md)). One the engine stops telling of is settled as failed: when the session is lost, at the time it was found gone, and when the engine's report of the session's background work, naming a turn for every piece, no longer names its turn, at the report's time, since the engine counts a live subagent as its turn's pending work. A bot's state counts its little ones still working (`littleOnes`), for the roster: a working bot's, its turn's and those its ended turns still have at work; any other state's, only the latter, and only when there are some; a quiet turn's are not counted. A turn whose only work so far is a plan or a little one shows the bot working, with no step to name.

A Codex subagent's own subagents are not among the turn's little ones: the engine records them in the subagent's thread, which J3 Bots does not follow ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)).

A client that predates the turn's work reads neither field and shows the turn's other steps: the steps the work replaces (the plan's update, a subagent's or a delegated task's launch) are no longer written. Steps already stored keep their words.

### 5. The outcome closes the dispatch

When the turn ends, the dispatch records how: completed, failed (with the engine's error class), interrupted, or waiting on the operator, and its wakes are finished. A failed routine wake is retried according to its routine's policy. An operator's turn that failed, or that something other than the operator's Stop interrupted (a provider, an engine restart), shows a `turn-failed` notice in the conversation with a retry action, and so does a new bot's opening turn; an interruption carries the class `unknown`. A turn that fails before it starts never delivered its dispatch, so its wakes fail as `rejected` and its messages show that they did not reach the bot. The dispatch keeps the outcome: a routine run's attempt that failed so, a provider's passing error say, is tried again, unlike one whose envelope the engine refused.

The adapter knows which turn delivers a dispatch because every engine run names the message that started it, and that message's id comes from the dispatch key; a steer's follow-up turn keeps the same message, and a run the engine restarted to take a steer keeps the message of its first attempt. A turn the engine started on its own closes no dispatch.

**Continuations.** A turn that fails at a usage limit may end with a pending continuation: the engine will resume it at the reset ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)). Only the dispatch that owns the turn follows it. Its `turn-finished` then leaves the dispatch delivered rather than finished, with the outcome so far and the reset time recorded, and no `undelivered` wake fires. When the continuation turn starts, its cause names the turn it continues; the server follows that one link, moving the dispatch's turn and its audience to the new turn, so a chain of continuations is followed a link at a time. A continuation turn that ends before it started (it failed, or a Stop cancelled it while it waited) follows the link the same way, by its cause, as its end is applied: once the engine has a turn continuing another it sends no `continuation-dropped`, so nothing else would end that work. The dispatch finishes when a turn ends with no continuation pending, or when `continuation-dropped` arrives, with the last outcome; only then does an `undelivered` wake fire if nothing was delivered. A last turn that did not complete (it failed, was interrupted, or was cancelled before it started) gives a `turn-failed` notice instead, unless a Stop covers the work. A continuation cancelled before it started ran nothing, so its notice shows the outcome of the turn it was to continue, as when one is dropped. A pending continuation keeps the session busy for automated wakes, which wait for the reset anyway. An operator wake is still dispatched; the engine then drops the continuation, because it resumes only the session's latest turn. A Stop disarms it too. J3 Bots never snoozes a session: snoozing would only delay the continuation. The engine's continuation after its own restart is turned off, so a turn a restart cut short ends `interrupted` like any other.

### Stop

A Stop is a separate path, never a side effect of `done`, and a durable one: it is written down before the engine is called, and finished after a crash. It has a **scope**: `bot`, the operator's Stop of one bot, which ends all of its work; or `room`, recorded when the operator stops a room's work (`room.stop`), archives or deletes the room, or removes a member, which ends only work whose audience is that room. The server also records a Stop of its own that ends one turn, the one that asked for help in the bot's browser ([A Stop of one turn](#stop)).

1. **Record.** In one transaction the server writes the Stop under its command id with its **targets**: every session of the bot with a turn running, or a dispatch that may have been sent (`intended` with an attempt, `accepted`, `delivered`, or failed `unresolved`, whose key may still land, a turn waiting in the engine included), in the Stop's scope. A `bot` Stop also targets every live session of the bot that the engine has, for background work the server does not see, except one whose engine thread the server has already archived, which runs nothing more. A `room` Stop targets the room's turns and dispatches, and every session where background work one of the room's turns started may still be pending, flagged or not reported since the turn ended ([Rooms](#rooms)). Each target's first round gets an engine key derived from the Stop's command id, the session id and the round. The same transaction **fences** every dispatch in scope that has not been accepted yet, so it is never sent for delivery again, and cancels every wake in scope the server still holds; each shows as cancelled where it came from, except a teammate's urgent message, which waits on as a held one ([A Stop of the recipient](#teammate-messages)). A `room` Stop also cancels the wakes the bot's work for that room wrote outside it, but a teammate's message: the room wakes of a `message_bot` posted in another room from a turn of the room, and the starter's `room-paused` wake that the bot's post wrote when it paused a room of bots only ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)). It releases a dispatch never sent that carries one, so the recipient's other wakes are planned again without it; the wake records the bot and the room whose work caused it. A post in the room its turn spoke for is that room's own wake, which this Stop cancels as such only when it is the room's own Stop, not a member's leaving: what the member said there was said. A dispatch that carries one and does not run to its end later, released or failed, cancels it instead of giving it back, when the Stop was recorded after that dispatch was written, so after the wake. One the engine may have is the recipient's turn, which that room's Stop does not end. The operator's Retry sends such a wake again unless a Stop cancelled it: one this Stop cancelled stays cancelled, and one whose turn ran and failed goes again. A fenced dispatch that was never sent is released, its wakes cancelled. It marks every turn in scope still running as stopped, which covers that turn's work also when no dispatch began it ([What a Stop covers](#stop)). And it covers what those turns sent their teammates, and nothing else the bot sent: each such message no earlier Stop marked is marked with this one, an urgent one's wake still pending is cancelled unless it is the operator's Retry, and a held one not yet in an envelope is removed ([A Stop of the sender](#teammate-messages)).
2. **Settle the dispatches in flight.** A dispatch of the target session whose call may still be in flight is waited for, and so is every one whose reply was lost, fenced or not: confirmation needs the engine's answer for each. They belong to the recovery, which tries a fenced one at once and every one with its usual backoff and bounds, recovering it with its key to learn the answer: if the engine had applied it, the recovery applies nothing; if it had not, the recovery applies it, and the stop that follows ends it. The Stop waits for them in that session, and meanwhile the session's running turn keeps running: no round is sent before the recovery settles them. Either way nothing the bot was sent lands behind the Stop. A fenced dispatch that may have been sent never waits again: if it fails, refused or unresolved, its wakes are settled by the one rule below ([What a Stop covers](#stop)). Only a dispatch proved never sent gives its wakes back. A dispatch that failed `unresolved`, whose key may still land, is fenced too, without returning to the recovery: it waits for nothing, and its landing reopens the Stop (step 4).
3. **Stop each target.** A `room` round waits, unsent, while its session has work for another audience: a turn running, or a dispatch the engine took whose turn the events do not show ended: one starting, or running unseen. A dispatch the engine answered as `started` holds it from that answer, before any `turn-started` shows its turn. The operator's steer into a turn holds it until the engine settles the steer, which it does after that turn's end: taken, dropped or requeued. A dispatch whose turn the events have not shown start, and that the engine did not answer as started, does not count while that turn cannot start as far as the server sees: the room's own turn runs ahead of it, or the engine holds the queue; the room's Stop cancels it, and it fails as `stopped`, retryable. That view is brought up to the engine's report first (below), so it trails the engine only from that report to the engine's stop: a private turn queued behind the room's that starts then, when the room's turn ends meanwhile, is ended with the room's work, and its dispatch fails as `stopped`, retryable, the same outcome as the cancel. A steer the engine makes a `new-turn` while it holds the queue is **Assumed** not to start until the queue is resumed. While the round waits, the only send the dispatcher can make into the session is the operator's steer into the admitted turn ([A session with an unsettled Stop takes nothing](#stop)), and the engine can make that steer a turn of its own two ways. If the turn it was for already ended in the engine, the steer becomes a new turn (`new-turn`); if that turn ends without taking it, the engine requeues it as a follow-up turn (`steer-requeued`). Either turn can start while the round waits, and the round waits for it too, unless the engine holds the queue: a turn that fails holds it, so a steer requeued behind a failed turn cannot start, and the round goes and cancels it. The engine's stop ends the whole session, and a room's Stop does not end the operator's private turn or another room's while the server can see it. It does disarm a usage-limit continuation pending for private work in the session: that dispatch finishes with its last outcome and shows a `turn-failed` notice, retryable. That work's end nudges the runner. A `room` round is judged again inside the session's gate, right before it is issued, since every send into the session is claimed there: work that started meanwhile holds it. **Bound.** A turn of another audience the events show running holds the round for as long as it runs: it ends on its own. Work the server cannot see run holds it for at most two minutes (`ROOM_ROUND_HOLD_BOUND`): a dispatch the engine took whose turn no event shows, or a runnable turn the engine's report lists that no event told of. Such a run starts in seconds; one stuck in startup would otherwise hold the room's Stop for good. The bound counts from when such work alone first held the round, recorded on the round so it holds across a restart, and again from the next time once a turn of another audience was admitted, recorded in the transaction that admits it, so a turn that starts and ends between two passes of the runner counts too. A round past its bound goes as a bot Stop's would, logged, while the room still has work running or flagged there, and what it ends fails as `stopped`, retryable; with nothing of the room left, it settles with no round. Unless a turn of another audience the events show running holds it, a `room` round is judged in the gate on a report of the session's pending work and runnable turns that the runner asks the engine for in that pass, outside the gate, which also brings the events up to it; without one, also from an engine that cannot be reached, the round waits for a later pass, and only that session waits. Each runnable turn the report lists is attributed by the server's turns and the dispatches that name it (`engine_turn_id`), a dispatch for the room not admitted yet included: one that is not the room's holds the round, within the bound, as it may start or run with no event telling of it, unless a dispatch of another audience names it whose turn cannot start as above, which the round cancels, so an engine-queued run no dispatch names, such as a background command's wake, whose work may be the room's, holds it also behind a held queue; otherwise the round goes while the room's work runs or the report lists a runnable turn of the room's, which may be active, or the background work its turns started (the engine lists no background work while a run is active), and settles the target with no round when it lists neither. A session the engine no longer has is lost meanwhile. Its fences stay, so a fenced key that lands after all reopens it. The engine can still start a turn on its own, a harness's wake or a continuation, between that check and the engine's stop, and the stop ends it too ([0013](../decisions/0013-background-work-belongs-to-the-turn-that-started-it.md)). The round is **issued** first: recorded, inside the session's gate, before its commands are sent. Then `stop` with the round's key ends the running turn and the delegated work the engine can reach, including background work a turn left running after it ended, cancels every turn waiting in the engine, drops untaken steers and disarms a pending continuation. The adapter sends the engine's own stop to every target, also one with no running turn.
