The adapter derives each engine command from the key, so a retried round, or one resumed after a crash, sends the same commands. Its success only acknowledges the commands, and is recorded as the round's acknowledgment. Each command is resolved by its key, as a dispatch is: a call that gets no answer, or an error reply that proves nothing, leaves the round issued and unacknowledged, and the runner sends it again with the same key on a later pass, with no other signal needed; the engine's receipt for the key answers it. When the receipt shows the engine refused the round while something it targets can still run, the round's key is spent: the round is marked refused and the target gets a fresh round, with a new key, due after the recovery's backoff, doubling with each round the engine refused since it last acknowledged one, up to ten minutes. No fresh round follows a target that settled meanwhile: every writer of a round checks, in the transaction that writes it, that the target is unsettled and its session not lost, so a refusal the runner hears after the session's loss committed is recorded on its round and opens nothing. Each round waits only on its own deadline: another target's round that is due, such as the operator's new Stop, goes out meanwhile. A refusal while nothing it targets can still run is moot, and counts as acknowledged. A refusal while a tool whose persistence the adapter could not decide is live is not: a refused stop interrupted nothing, so the tool may still run ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)).
4. **Confirm from the events.** One rule governs every target: it settles only when the engine's own state shows nothing the Stop targeted can still run, no run waiting, preparing, starting or running and no background work pending that the engine's stop ends (`stoppable`); and as long as it is unsettled, something keeps acting on it. Its latest round is always in a state a durable deadline or an event moves on: not yet sent, and sent once due (step 3); being sent, and resolved by its key; refused, and followed by a fresh round after a backoff; acknowledged, and watched until the target settles, with a fresh round once the round's grace passes while the engine still shows work it targets. Waiting on a round the engine refused or ignored is never an option. The acknowledgment of step 3 only says the engine took the commands; their effects can lag it, and the engine can lose them without saying so (an interrupt it could not plan, a run whose provider never starts). After it, the server synchronizes the session, which **observes** the round: every event the engine had by then is applied. A marker that reports what no event has told of leaves the round unobserved, and the runner synchronizes again a few seconds later ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)): background work the engine still counts as pending, which in a session whose last turn already ended the engine's stop interrupts only after it answered, and which an interrupt the engine failed to plan leaves running though the stop was acknowledged (a tool whose persistence the adapter could not read from the stream counts as the engine's own projection of its pending work shows it, and as pending while that cannot say); and a run the engine can still run: one it is starting, created after the round's commands or held by a provider that never starts it, or one it queued on its own after the round's cancels, such as a scheduled message, which no dispatch of the server's names and no event will start while the Stop holds the queue. **Grace.** An acknowledged round gives the engine 30 seconds to show its effect, doubling with each round of the target the engine acknowledged, up to ten minutes. When a synchronization after that still shows work the Stop targets (a run waiting, preparing, starting or running, a dispatch waiting in the engine's queue, a continuation pending, background work pending), the target gets one fresh round, due at once, whatever the events did or did not announce. The deadline is the round's own, so it holds across a restart. A Stop the engine can never end therefore costs a bounded amount: one round per grace, at most one every ten minutes once the grace has reached its cap, and a synchronization of the session every five seconds. A target is stopped when its latest round is observed and the session's events then show nothing left: no running turn, no dispatch the engine may still start, no continuation pending. A steer the engine took counts until the engine settles it, which it does once the turn it rode ends; one whose turn has ended and that is still unsettled when such an observation shows nothing the engine can run has nothing left to settle it, as after an engine fault. It fails then as the engine dropping it would: its wakes are cancelled when a Stop covers it, and fail as `stopped`, retryable, otherwise. It is recorded as `unresolved`, so a settlement the engine sends after all is still applied, a `steered` showing the message delivered, and its key's landing reopens a Stop. Background work the engine's stop does not reach, which a provider session the engine kept from before a fresh-context start still runs, is not waited on: no round would end it, and the session's retirement does ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)). This holds for a `room` Stop too: the engine's stop ends the whole session, its background work included, so a session a `room` Stop targets confirms only once the engine shows no background work pending there that its stop ends, whoever started it.

    Such a Stop settles by no deadline of its own: it stays unsettled for as long as the engine counts the work as pending, and the session takes nothing meanwhile, while the server keeps sending a round after each grace. That is the engine's own account, and the engine keeps acting on it: an interrupt the provider does not confirm leaves the turn open for the operator to force-stop it in the engine's console, and a command queued behind it waits for the turn to end (**Read** at `bc0f04d`: `ClaudeAdapterV2.ts`, L14963-14977; `Orchestrator.ts`, L14673-14681). The operator can also stop the bot again, which records a fresh round for every live session of the bot ([Record](#stop)) and goes out at once, whatever the backoff of earlier rounds. The Stop runner confirms only from such an observation, never from one event as it is applied, because the next event may start a turn the engine had waiting; each event that is not itself a marker makes it observe again. A turn whose start is applied once the latest round was issued, also one that started while the round's commands were on their way, opens a new round, since the commands may have missed it; so does a fenced dispatch the engine applies after its target was confirmed, which shows as a turn with its key: it is stopped on sight. A dispatch the events first show waiting in the engine's queue (a `turn-queued`, or a replacement that lists its turn waiting) opens one too, as it is accepted: its key may have landed after the round's cancels, held in the queue the Stop left, where no turn would start to reopen the Stop. Only that first sight does; a replacement that lists the turn again finds the dispatch already accepted. The Stop is finished when every target is stopped.

**A session with an unsettled Stop takes nothing.** From the moment its target is recorded until it is confirmed stopped, the server sends that session no first send (only the recoveries of step 2) and resumes no held queue in it. One send is let through: the operator's steer into the admitted turn while every unsettled target of the session is a `room` Stop for another room that has issued no round there yet. That Stop waits for the turn anyway (step 3), and the steer keeps it waiting, so the operator's word reaches the turn they are talking to; the planner, the send and its claim read the rule alike. No first send of a new turn goes: what that steer becomes when its turn does not take it (step 3) is the only new turn the server's sends can give the session meanwhile. The engine's Stop is several commands, and a message arriving between them could restart a turn the Stop is ending ([Engine](engine.md#behaviors-j3-bots-designs-around)). The rule is the database's, so it holds across a restart: each round records when it was issued, acknowledged and observed, and the Stop runner's first pass at start, once every session's events are followed again, sends the rounds that were never acknowledged and confirms only from an observation made after an acknowledgment. Once a target is confirmed, what it held back is nudged: the dispatcher, the recovery and the resumer.

**What a Stop covers.** One rule decides whether work is the operator's to have stopped: a Stop of the bot, or of the room the work was for, recorded after the dispatch that began the work was written. The order is exact, not a time: every dispatch has a sequence number, higher than any written before it and never reused, and a Stop records the highest one when it is recorded. Work is covered when its dispatch's number is at or below that mark. SQLite serializes writes, so a dispatch committed after the Stop is always above it, even in the same millisecond or after the clock stepped back. Whether that Stop has settled does not matter: nothing is planned into its sessions while it is unsettled, so work that began after it was planned after it, as new intent. Work it covers starts nothing new of its own accord: a turn that ends, even on its own before the interrupt lands, gets no `undelivered` wake and no notice. A turn the engine started on its own has no such dispatch. One that continues an earlier turn of its session (a usage limit's continuation, or a delegated task's completion that names the turn that delegated it) is that turn's work: a Stop that covers any turn of its chain, or marked one stopped, covers it. One that names none, such as a harness's own wake, is covered by a Stop recorded while it ran. Either way, a Stop of the bot still unsettled in its session when J3 Bots first sees the turn start covers it too: the engine may have begun it before the Stop and shown it only after, and the Stop's round, which has not settled, reaches it. A Stop that had settled by then never covers it: the turn began after the Stop was done. A room's Stop marks no such turn: one it would cover goes on with a turn of the room, whose dispatch it covers. The rule applies wherever work, not the operator, would create a wake, also a teammate's: a message to a teammate is its sender's work, so none is sent from work a Stop covers. Once sent, it is that work's result: only a Stop that reaches its sending turn while the turn runs ends what it has not delivered ([A Stop of the sender](#teammate-messages)). Such a wake also needs a place to go, so a room archived or deleted meanwhile gets none. An operator's own wake after a Stop is never covered: it is new intent.

The same rule settles every dispatch that will not run to its end, read in the transaction that settles it, so a Stop recorded or a room archived meanwhile counts:

| The dispatch | Covered by a Stop | Not covered |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Never sent (released) | Wakes cancelled | Wakes pending again |
| Dropped by the engine (a waiting turn cancelled, a starting turn interrupted, a steer dropped) | Wakes cancelled | Wakes failed as `stopped` |
| Failed (`rejected`, `unresolved`, its session lost) | Wakes cancelled | Wakes failed with that reason |

A notice follows from the outcome: cancelled wakes get none, and failed ones show where they came from, retryable. The events close the rest: the running turn ends interrupted, a waiting turn ends cancelled and its dispatch is dropped, a dropped steer drops its dispatch. The engine's Stop ends a whole session. A `room` Stop waits for another audience's turn to end (step 3), but still reaches private work waiting in the engine behind the room's turn, and private background work pending in the session, which it does not cover: a private dispatch it fenced before sending is released, its wakes pending again, and one the Stop drops fails as `stopped`, visibly and retryable, because the operator did not stop it, also one whose recovery had given up and that the engine took late: its wakes fail again, as `stopped`, and an answer keeps the notice it already shows, which now says so. So does work a Stop sent from the engine's own console drops: the server recorded no Stop. Work for the room, and all of a bot's work under a later `bot` Stop, is covered. A room's dispatch that was never sent is fenced and cancelled even when no session needs the engine's Stop. Background work the engine could not reach may still wake the bot later, as a background turn.

**A Stop of one turn.** Once a bot's request for help in its browser is in ([Help in the browser](#help-in-the-browser)), the server records a Stop that names the turn that asked (`stops.engine_turn_id`), in the same transaction as the card, so the bot can do nothing more in the browser until the answer wakes it in a turn of its own. Its `scope` and `room_id` are the turn's audience, and it targets only that turn's session. Its round is judged as a `room` round is (step 3), with the turn and the dispatch that names it as its own work in place of the room's: it waits while other work runs in the session, within the same bound, and settles unsent when the turn already ended. The same transaction fences the session's dispatches not yet accepted, as any Stop does. It is not the operator's: it covers no work, so a wake an expiry of the card records, which continues that work, still goes; it cancels no wake, and marks no turn stopped; the turn's interrupted end shows no `turn-failed` notice and gets no `undelivered` wake, since the card ended its delivery; and an operator's steer is held by it, as a `room` Stop that issued a round holds one. It holds the session like any unsettled Stop, so the answer's wake waits for the stop to be confirmed and starts a fresh turn.

**Force stop.** An AI can ignore the engine's stop: the engine marks the stop request unresponsive once its AI has not confirmed it in time, and the adapter reports the turn as `stop-ignored` (`null` once nothing ignores a stop). The server stores that turn on its session, and while one does, its bot offers Force stop (`Bot.forceStop`), and so does a room whose turn it is, or whose own Stop went to that session and is not settled (`Room.forceStop`): an idle session's stop reaches the engine's latest turn, which can be a private one while work a room's turn left goes on. A room's Stop that still waits for another audience's turn to end has asked the engine nothing, so a stop the session ignores meanwhile is not the room's. `bot.forceStop` and `room.forceStop` record the same Stop as `bot.stop` and `room.stop`, so a Force stop reaches everything that Stop reaches and settles the same way. In the same transaction they mark the session's ignored turn as asked to be forced (`sessions.force_stop_turn_id`), for a room, each session whose ignored stop is the room's as above. The Stop runner then sends `forceStop` for each such session through its gate. The engine kills the session's own agent processes and ends the turn as interrupted, or settles the background work it left, then lets the provider session go; once it has, the request is done. A newer turn the engine started meanwhile is not ended, since nothing then ignores the stop the operator saw. If the engine cannot be reached, the request stays and goes on a later pass. The Stop that comes with it asks the engine again, which clears the engine's mark until its wait runs out once more, so a request outlives the AI's stop no longer being reported ignored. It is dropped when its turn ends with no work of the session left running, its own or an earlier turn's, or when the AI ignores a stop of another turn. Once the engine took it, the turn's stop no longer counts as ignored. Nothing is offered while a request waits for the engine. When no AI ignores the Stop, Force stop is the Stop alone. What a force stop leaves behind: whatever the AI already did, such as files it wrote or commands it ran, stays done. Turns queued behind the stopped one are left alone, so they go to the Stop that came with the force stop. The bot's next turn starts a new provider session in the same engine session, so the conversation and the session's history carry on. No failure notice is shown, since the Stop covers the turn.

**Held queues.** The adapter reports each hold of a session's engine queue as a `queue-held` event, with the turns waiting behind it. Applying it only stores the hold's cursor on the session; the resumer, a worker like the others, resumes it with `resumeQueue` naming that cursor, once: it stores the cursor of the hold it last resumed, and a hold equal to it is not resumed again. Before a resume it synchronizes the session, so a replacement that shows the queue no longer held, or a hold replayed from before, is applied first, and it then reads the hold again inside the session's gate. A replacement that shows the queue not held clears a hold recorded before. A resume whose reply was lost, or that failed unexpectedly, is sent again for the same hold with backoff, doubling up to ten minutes, for as long as that hold is the session's current one ([The workers](#the-workers)); a resume's result is recorded only if the hold it answered is still current, so a newer hold is never taken as resumed. Each session's resume runs on its own: one that fails never holds up another's. It never resumes while a Stop of the session is unsettled; the hold is resumed once the Stop is confirmed, if turns still wait. Nor does it resume a hold of a retired or lost session, whose queue holds nothing the bot waits for, or of one not yet brought to its bot's settings (**Settings alignment**, in [Sessions](#sessions)), whose alignment nudges it; it reads that again in the gate, so a session retired or changed meanwhile is left alone. The engine refuses a resume on an archived session or at a usage limit, which leaves the queue to the engine's own rules.

## Questions, approvals and secrets

Two kinds of card wait on the operator, with separate lifecycles. A third, a bot's request for help in its browser, has its own ([Help in the browser](#help-in-the-browser)).

**A bot's `ask`** is a J3 Bots question. The card stores the wake it came from and the audience of the turn that asked: the conversation, or the room. It always shows in the bot's own conversation: a room turn's question is the operator's to answer, like an approval, so the room shows only its `waiting-in-conversation` notice ([Rooms](#rooms)). The bot ends its turn after asking. The card does not expire. Answering or dismissing it closes the card and creates a `user` wake carrying the answer in the same transaction, with the stored audience; that wake's turn delivers there. An answer is never steered into a running turn and never shares an envelope with a steer: one that arrives before the turn that asked has ended waits for it, and then starts the next turn. The answer's envelope is defined in [Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes).

**The engine's requests** belong to a running turn: an approval, a question asked through the harness's own tool, or a secret request. The card stores the session, the engine's request id and the turn. Its card goes to the bot's conversation, also during a room turn, because the operator answers for their bot there. While the request is open, the engine can report a fuller record of it (`request-updated`, such as an approval that becomes exact once its file change is fetched), and a replacement lists it as it then shows: the card shows that record in place and keeps its status, so an answer already recorded is the one sent. "Allow for session" is taken only while the card offers a grant (`sessionGrant`). An engine question takes a choice or a typed answer, not both: the engine receives a typed answer alone, so an answer with both is refused (`Invalid("answer")`) rather than shown with a choice the turn never got. An `ask` question takes both. An engine question takes no dismissal: the engine refuses one for every question a harness asks while its turn waits ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)), so `dismiss` is refused (`Invalid("not-dismissible")`), nothing is recorded, and the card stays open. The clients offer Dismiss only on an `ask` question. The turn goes on once the question is answered, or is stopped. Answering it records the answer durably and then sends it to the engine for that turn, keyed by the card, so the answer is applied once however often it is sent, and the turn continues. A card takes one answer: a second command finds it closed. The responder sends each answer the engine has not taken; one whose reply was lost, or whose send failed unexpectedly, is sent again with backoff, doubling up to ten minutes, for as long as its request is open ([The workers](#the-workers)). An answer the engine refuses while its request stays open (`AnswerRejected`) is final: the engine keeps the refusal for the answer's key, so the answer is never sent again. The server logs it, closes the request on its side, and closes the card as `refused`, which tells the operator that the bot could not take the answer and to stop the bot and ask again in a message. The turn waits until it is stopped or ends. Each answer is sent on its own: one that fails never holds up the next. When the turn ends, every request still open on it expires, and its card shows that it expired. An engine question is shown whole, with each question's header and each option's preview as the harness gives them, however many questions and options it has. A card a client older than 0.11.0 can decode holds at most four questions of six options each, so a larger one carries its questions in `whole`, and in `questions` a single placeholder that asks an older client's operator to update: an older client shows that placeholder and ignores `whole`, so it never shows part of a question nor turns a question into an answer it did not ask for. Its answer goes through the card like any other, keyed by the whole question's ids.

**Why an approval came.** When the bot's AI falls back to asking before acting, although the bot works in Auto or Full access, the engine says so in the turn, and the adapter reports it as an `engine-notice` ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)). The server shows it as an `asks-before-acting` notice in the bot's own conversation, where the approvals it explains will be, once per kind (Auto, Full access) for each model the bot has (`engine_notices`), whichever of the bot's sessions the engine says it in, a routine's side sessions included, and however often it repeats it. A notice of a permission mode the bot no longer has is over, and does not show. Its `details` are the engine's words, scrubbed, for the notice's Details. Every other notice the engine records stays in the server's log ([decision 0083](../decisions/0083-only-the-engine-notices-a-person-can-act-on-show.md)).

**A secret request** comes from a bot calling the engine's `request_secret` tool. Its card shows the label, the reason, an optional link, and either one masked value with its placeholder or the request's fields (`fields`, up to eight, each with its label, placeholder and whether it is masked), and where the values go (`delivery`: `reference`, which the bot uses without seeing, or `file`, private files its commands read for 24 hours), never a value. The operator's answer travels in its own request (`secret.provide`), which hands the values straight to the engine: `provide` for one value, `provide-fields` with a value for each field, keyed by the field's id. An answer that does not fit the card's fields, as a client before 0.12.0 sends to a card with several, fails with `Invalid("answer")` before anything reaches the engine and leaves the card open; so does a value of spaces alone, which the contract refuses. No value enters an entry, a change, a log, a notification or a command receipt. The card records only that the values were provided, or declined, or that the request expired, from the request's resolution. `secret.provide` succeeds only when this call's own answer was the one the engine took. It has no receipt, so after a lost reply a retry may find the request closed: the server synchronizes the session and fails with `Conflict("card-closed")`, because the closing answer may have been another device's, or the request expired. A closed card is never reported as success; the card itself shows how it closed. The server takes one submission per card at a time, and so does the adapter per request, so a second device's submission waits for the first and then finds the card closed. Before calling the engine, the server writes a durable marker on the card that a submission is in flight, naming that submission and holding no value, and clears it on a definite answer; a cleanup removes only the marker of its own submission, so it never erases a newer one. A marker left by `EngineIndeterminate`, or found at start after a crash, means the outcome is unknown: the server synchronizes the session at start, and before it sends the next submission for that card. A request that is closed then shows how it closed; one still open takes a value again, and a submission that lands late only makes the next one find the card closed.

## Secrets

Secrets keep what the operator types for bots to use without reading it: logins for websites and single values such as an API token (**secrets**), and what a connector needs to reach its service (**connector secrets**). They are a SQLite database of their own, `<J3_HOME>/vault/vault.sqlite`, apart from `j3.sqlite`, sealed under a data key the server keeps in a key file beside it, `<J3_HOME>/vault/key`. Nothing unlocks them: they can be used from the server's start ([decision 0046](../decisions/0046-secrets-the-operator-types-a-bot-uses-a-reference.md)).

**Keys.** The data key is 256 random bits. The key file holds it in base64 under a first line, `j3-bots secrets key v1`, written private to the server's user (`0600`, its folder `0700`) and replaced whole. Every value is sealed with AES-256-GCM, a random 96-bit nonce, and the value's record id and field name as associated data, so a sealed value copied to another record or field does not open. A key check, a fixed text sealed under the data key, tells the right key from another one. Every buffer that holds a key or an opened value is zeroed once it is used, also when the request fails or is interrupted.

**States.** `secrets.status` streams the state to every client, with a revision that changes with every change to the secrets:

- `ready`: secrets can be added and used.
- `unreadable`: secrets were sealed, and the key file is missing or holds another key. Nothing is changed: the server reads them again once the file is put back, or the operator starts over ([Operating](../operations/operating.md#secrets)).
- `newer`: the file records a migration this release does not know, because a newer release wrote it. The server leaves it as it is.

At start, with nothing sealed yet, the server uses the key file's key or makes one, and writes the key check. While the state is not `ready`, every request that reads or changes secrets fails with `Conflict("secrets-unavailable")`, and the state says why. A value that does not open under the key, or opens but does not decode as what its field holds, means the file was changed or damaged: the request fails with `Conflict("secrets-damaged")`, and the server logs an error naming the record and the field, never the value or the decoder's message, which could quote it.

**A vault from before secrets.** Until 0.9.0 the data key was wrapped under a key scrypt derived from a master password ([decision 0029](../decisions/0029-the-vault-key-comes-from-a-master-password-held-in-memory.md)), in a keyring beside the records. Releases from 0.9.0 to 0.12 asked for that password once and moved the key to the key file; this release does not take it. A keyring with no record beside it is deleted at start, and the key file is made. One with records refuses the server's start, as a configuration it may not run on does (`serve` ends with 78): its one line says to open the vault with 0.12 first, and nothing is changed ([decision 0086](../decisions/0086-code-stays-only-for-the-current-release-a-rollback-and-protocol-11.md)). An update from 0.12 to such a home therefore never becomes ready, and rolls back to 0.12, where the vault opens. An export of it stops with the same line.

**A secret** has a name, unique among the operator's secrets, by which a bot names it: lower-case letters, digits, `-` and `_`, starting with a letter or a digit, at most 64 characters, so it reads the same in a header, an argument or an environment value. It is one of two kinds:

- a **login**: the sites it is for (1 to 10 exact origins, scheme, host and port as a browser writes them), a user name, a password and, optionally, a one-time-code key. That is TOTP (RFC 6238) over HOTP (RFC 4226), read from a base32 key or an `otpauth://` address, with SHA-1, SHA-256 or SHA-512, 6 to 8 digits and a period of 15 to 120 seconds, computed with `node:crypto`;
- a **value**: one token or key.

Its values and a login's origins are sealed. In the clear, the file holds the record's id, kind and name, its creation and change times, which bots may use it, when a bot last used it (which bot, and where: a site's origin or a connector's name; a fill counts once the browser took what was typed), the bot's ask it was saved from, and when a value was last shown, which field and on which device. **Which bots may use it** is every bot, those the operator makes later included, or the bots named; a bot that is deleted drops out of every list.

**Requests.** `secrets.list` returns every secret by name, with none of its values. `secrets.add` takes an id the client chose, so a retry after a lost reply adds nothing, the name, the values and which bots may use it; a name another secret has fails with `Conflict("secret-name-taken")`, and a one-time-code key that is not one with `Invalid("totp")`. `secrets.addFromAsk` answers a bot's ask for several values ([Asking for one](#secrets)): an id the client chose for each, the value of each of the card's inputs, and which bots may use them. `secrets.update` changes the name or the values, a field left out keeping its value; a login's field on a value, or the other way round, fails with `Invalid("secret-kind")`. `secrets.setAccess` replaces which bots may use it. `secrets.delete` deletes it. `secrets.reveal` returns one value to the client that asked and records the time, the device and the field. `secrets.totpPreview` returns the current code and until when it holds. Any paired client may make these requests; no bot tool reaches them.

**Using a secret.** A bot never gets a value. It is handed a reference, `secret:<name>`, and the server puts the value where it is used, for a bot the secret allows:

- **In its browser**, a login's user name, password or one-time code, typed by the server into the bot's tab ([Filling a login](#filling-a-login)).
- **In a connector's settings**, `${secret:<name>}` in a header, an argument or an environment value, or `${secret:<name>.username}` for a login's user name; a login's bare reference is its password. A connector is one set of settings for every bot it goes to, so its references are resolved for the connector, not for each bot ([Secrets in a connector's settings](#secrets-in-a-connectors-settings)). `Secrets.referenceValues` gives each reference's value for the bots that will use it, or fails with `SecretUnavailable`, naming the secret and why (`missing`, `not-allowed`, `no-value`, or `unavailable` while the secrets are not `ready`), never a value.

Each use records the bot and where, once a minute at most for the same bot and place, since a connector may send it with every request. A connector's use is recorded when a bot's call to it is answered, for that bot.

**Asking for one.** A bot that needs a secret it does not have calls `ask_for_secret` with a name, the kind, for a login the address of the page that asks for it, and a note in its own words. A secret of that name the bot may use already, of the kind asked for and, for a login, for the page's site, shows no card: the answer is `exists`, with the reference. One the bot may use that is something else is refused, saying what it is, and so is a name another secret has, and so is a login without an `http:` or `https:` address, or a value with one, and work the operator stopped. Otherwise the call puts a `secret-ask` card in the bot's own conversation, also during a room turn, since only the operator types into it, and answers `shown` with the reference the secret will have; a second call for the same name and the same secret while that card waits shows it again, and one for something else is refused. A bot that needs a few values that go together, such as a service's client ID and its client secret, asks for them on one card (`kind: values`, two to four, [decision 0090](../decisions/0090-a-connector-signs-in-as-a-service-with-credentials-the-operator-gives.md)): each with a name and a label of the bot's, masked unless the bot says it is not secret, and no name another secret's; the answer is `shown` with every reference. Any ask may name the `https:` page where the service issues what it asks for (`setup`), which the card links to. The operator types the values into the card, which says the bot will not see them, and saves it with `secrets.add` and the card's id. The card's name and kind must be the secret's. A card for several values is saved with `secrets.addFromAsk` instead, which adds each as a single value under its own name, for the same bots, and refuses an answer that leaves one out; such a card answered with `secrets.add`, as by a client that shows one input, is refused (`Invalid("answer")`), so it is never saved in part. The bot that asked may use them whatever the operator chose, and the operator can widen that to more bots or every bot. Secrets are another database, so the secret is added first, with the card it answers, and the card closed after; after a crash between them, a device that sends the answer again, under any id, closes the card, and so does the next sweep, a minute later at most, for a card for several values once every one was added; a card already closed another way stays as it is, and the secret is added all the same. Declining is `card.answer` with `dismiss`. An ask nobody answered for 24 hours expires. Each close wakes the bot, for the audience of the turn that asked, with a system event that names the outcome (`saved`, `declined` or `expired`) and the reference, or each reference. A save or a decline is the operator's word, which no Stop cancels ahead of time; an expiry continues the work that asked, so a Stop of that work leaves the bot asleep.

The engine's own `request_secret`, which hands one value to one command and keeps nothing ([Questions, approvals and secrets](#questions-approvals-and-secrets)), is unchanged and stays for a value a single command needs.

**Connector secrets.** A connector's secret is one record, `connector:<id>`, with seven sealed fields:

- `address`: the full URL of a remote connector, as the operator typed it or a gallery entry's form filled it in, which may carry a key in its path or query; empty for a command;
- `oauth`: the OAuth client (issuer, whether it was registered dynamically or by the operator, client id, optional client secret, how it authenticates, redirect URIs) and its tokens (access token, optional refresh token, expiry, scopes, resource);
- `headers`: header values;
- `command`: a started server's program and its arguments, which can carry a key; empty for a remote connector. What runs is read from here, never from the program the connector's row shows;
- `env`: environment values;
- `formSecrets`: the value of each secret field of a gallery entry's form, by the field's key. The address and headers hold them rendered (the key in `Bearer {key}`, or in a segment of the path); these are never sent as they are, only taken out of what the connector's server says and kept out of a sign-in's address.
- `formValues`: the value of each plain field of that form, by the field's key, so a header made of several fields renders again when one of them is given anew ([New values](#connectors)). A record from an earlier release has none and reads as empty: its header takes every one of its fields again, once.

In the server these are typed records: every token, secret and value is a redacted type. Connectors read and write them through `Secrets.connectorSecrets` (`get`, `put` of the whole secret, `remove`). That service is not reachable over RPC, a connector's secret is not in the operator's list, and nothing it does changes the revision. While the secrets are not `ready`, every one of these calls fails with `Conflict("secrets-unavailable")`, and a connector reports it as its own state, "secrets unavailable".

**What carries a value.** Values travel as redacted types from the client to the server and back, in the contract and in the server, and are decoded as redacted types at the RPC boundary. A client gets a value only from `secrets.reveal` and `secrets.totpPreview`; a bot, never. No value reaches an entry, a change, a command receipt, a log line, an error or a tool result. None of the secrets' requests takes a command id, so no receipt keeps one: `secrets.add` is made safe to repeat by the id it carries instead. A test drives the secrets' requests over the socket and checks that no value appears in any log, error or answer except the one that reveals it.

**Backups.** No update snapshot copies the secrets or their key file, and no rollback puts an older one back ([decision 0016](../decisions/0016-an-update-keeps-a-snapshot-to-roll-back-to.md)). A release that finds them `newer` leaves them alone. The secrets' file is useless without the key file, and the key file opens it for whoever has both: a backup that keeps them apart keeps them safe ([Operating](../operations/operating.md#secrets)). An export carries them to another server sealed again under a passphrase of the export's own, and never the key file ([Moving to another server](#moving-to-another-server)).

What the key file means for a bot with a shell is in [Security: secrets](security.md#the-operators-secrets).

## Filling a login

A bot signs in to a site with a login from the operator's secrets without being handed its values: it asks, and the server types them into the bot's browser tab while it holds the tab ([decision 0037](../decisions/0037-the-server-types-a-saved-login-into-the-bots-tab.md)). There is no confirmation for each fill: the operator decides ahead of time, by which bots may use each login ([decision 0046](../decisions/0046-secrets-the-operator-types-a-bot-uses-a-reference.md)).

**Who may.** A bot may fill a login only when both hold:

- its **browser** is on (`bots.browser`), since the fill uses the bot's tab ([The bot's browser](#the-bots-browser)); off gives `no-access`;
- the login is one the bot may use ([Secrets](#secrets)); else `unknown-login`.

Both are read at each call, so a change takes effect at once.

**What the bot sees.** Two `j3` tools, whose answers are fixed shapes that carry no value:

- `list_secrets` answers with the reference of every secret the bot may use, its kind and, for a login, its origins, or `unavailable` while the secrets are not `ready`.
- `fill_credential` takes the login's reference or name, the field (`username`, `password` or `totp`) and, optionally, `submit`. It answers `filled` with whether Enter was pressed, or `refused` with a reason. The reasons are `FillRefusal` in the contract.

**The fill.** The bot first clicks the field, with the engine's browser tools, so that the page's focus is in it. Then:

1. **The call.** The request is matched to the engine's record of a `fill_credential` call, open in the caller's running turn with the same arguments, and pinned to it in `tool_calls`, as a turn tool's call is ([Bot tools](#bot-tools), "Matching a call to its turn"), but in a transaction of its own: the fill holds a tab for seconds and runs after it. A request no call of the turn matches, from a client that is not the bot's harness or after the turn ended, is refused by the bridge before anything is read, as for a turn tool. The pin carries the turn the refusals below count in, and later the fill's outcome, which a forward retried under the same request id gets back. Past this point, one fill runs at a time on the whole server.
2. **The bot and the login.** The bot's browser switch and the login of that name, if the bot may use it, are read. None gives `unknown-login`.
3. **The tab.** The fill acts on the bot's front tab: the tab of the calling session that most recently opened or navigated ([The bot's browser](#the-bots-browser)). A session with no tab gives `no-tab`, and a tab a paired device holds gives `held`.
4. **Take it.** The server opens an engine viewer of its own on the tab and takes it, as a device would. From then until it closes the viewer, the bot's browser actions on the tab fail with the engine's `humanControl` ([Engine: the bot's browser](engine.md#the-bots-browser)). The engine must say the server holds it within 5 s; when another viewer holds it, `held`.
5. **Read where the tab is.** Only now, so the bot can no longer move it, the server reads the tab's committed address from the engine (`BrowserBridge.readTab`), not from anything the bot said. A page still loading gives `loading`; a blank or failed one, or an address that is not `http:` or `https:` gives `origin`. That includes a `blob:` page, whose origin is the address inside it but whose content is whatever made it. Plain `http:` on a host other than `localhost`, `127.0.0.0/8` or `[::1]` gives `insecure`.
6. **Match the origin.** The bot's browser switch and the logins it may use are read again, as they are now: the operator may have changed either while the take waited. The page's origin, as the URL parser writes it (scheme, ASCII host, so a lookalike name stays in punycode, and a port other than the default), must equal one of the login's origins exactly. No domain or suffix match: `https://example.com` does not cover `https://accounts.example.com`. No match gives `origin`.
7. **Open the value.** `Secrets.fillValue` opens the user name or password, or computes the current one-time code from the login's TOTP key (RFC 6238), and answers it with the login as it was when it opened it, read together. The fill refuses unless the bot may still use that login and is for the page's origin, so a change to the login made meanwhile cannot pair a new value with the origins it had before. It records the use, on the page's origin, and no reveal, since no device saw it. A login without that value gives `no-value`.
8. **Read again, then type.** The server reads the tab once more and refuses with `navigated` unless it is still loaded at the same address. It reads the bot's browser switch and the login's bots once more, and checks that the pinned call is still open in the session's running admitted turn: a turn the operator stopped while this fill waited for another gives `stopped`. It refuses unless the server still holds the tab. Then it types the value with the engine's `text` input (Chromium's `Input.insertText`), where the page's focus is.
9. **Submit.** With `submit`, it presses Enter. After a password it always does, `submit` or not, so the bot does not get the tab back with a filled password field on the page (step 11 says when it still can).
10. **Wait for the engine to type.** The engine runs a holder's input one action at a time, in order, and a release drops what is still waiting ([Engine: the bot's browser](engine.md#the-bots-browser)). So the server sends a `probe` after its input and waits up to 5 s for the answer, which the engine gives from the same line once the input before it ran. No answer gives `unavailable`, with a warning in the log: the value may then be in the field.
11. **Clear a password on a page that stays.** After a password, the server reads the tab every 100 ms for up to 6 s. The page counts as gone only when the tab closes, goes blank or fails, or loads another address after it was seen loading. Anything else counts as staying: an address that changes while the page stays loaded (a page that shows an error with `history.pushState`), loading the same address again (a sign-in that failed and shows the form again, or a submission answered with no new document), and a sign-in still loading when the wait ends. If it stays, the server deletes what it typed with one Backspace per character where the focus is, then waits for the engine again as in step 10.
12. **Give it back.** Closing the viewer gives the tab back to the bot, whose call then returns.

The viewer is released when the fill ends however it ends, refused, failed or interrupted, so a tab is never left held. A fill runs in the server's scope, not the call's: a bridge that gives up on a forward does not cut it short between typing and giving the tab back, and the forward's retry under the same request id gets the same answer.

**A refusal is final for the turn.** Once a fill was refused on a tab, every other fill the same session asks for on that tab in the same turn, the one its call was pinned to, is refused with `refused-before`, without taking the tab. This keeps a bot that a page talked into the wrong fill from trying again until something works. Secrets that cannot be read, a browser that did not answer, a person holding the tab and a stopped turn are not refusals of the page, and do not count.

**What is kept.** Each fill, typed or not, is a step of the bot's running turn in its work fold: tool `login`, the login's name and id, the field, the page's origin as checked, and the outcome (`CredentialFill` in the contract). A fill outside a running turn is not recorded there. The server's log gets one line per fill with the bot, the login's id, the field and the outcome. No value reaches a step, an entry, a log line, a tool result or an error. A test drives `list_secrets` and three fills through the bot tools and checks every step, answer, conversation entry and captured log line for the values and the one-time code.

**Reading the value back.** The fill keeps the value from the bot's tools; it does not keep it from the page, and every bot keeps the engine's `preview_evaluate`, so a bot can read a value it filled back with a script. The owner accepted that to keep page scripts. What the server cannot rule out, and what stands against it, is in [Security: filling a login](security.md#filling-a-login).

## Attachments

Files cross the server in both directions as attachments. Bytes and names are stored apart: a **blob** is the bytes, stored once under `<J3_HOME>/attachments` by their SHA-256; an **attachment** is a name and a media type over a blob. The same bytes sent twice, or under two names, are one blob and two attachments.

- A client uploads a file with `POST /attachments` before sending the message that carries it, and gets its id. The server puts its name and media type in the form the engine's upload takes (`normalizeAttachmentMetadata`: the name trimmed, the media type lowercased without parameters, at most 100 characters) and stores that, so a staging never fails on metadata. An empty file, a blank name or an unusable media type is refused; each file is limited to 25 MiB. `message.send` names up to ten, and the server checks them against the engine's limits before it writes anything: inline images (GIF, JPEG, PNG, WebP) at most 10 MiB each and 80 MiB together. It checks the message's length as the bot would read it the same way. `message.send` may also say where the operator placed each file in the text (`attachmentOffsets`: one UTF-16 offset per file, never decreasing, none inside a character); offsets that do not fit the text and the files are refused with `Invalid("attachment")`, and the message entry keeps them for every client to draw. The wake envelope writes each file there ([Wake envelopes](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). Coalesced wakes are checked again together ([coalescing](#2-the-server-holds-waiting-wakes)).
- A screen recording ([decision 0098](../decisions/0098-the-desktop-app-records-the-screen-for-a-bot.md)), a zip of up to 8 GiB (`MAX_RECORDING_SIZE`), is too large for one request and for memory, so it uploads in pieces that resume, under `/attachments/recordings`, to a paired device only. `POST` with its name and size begins an upload and answers it, with its id (`rec_…`) and the bytes the server holds, none yet. Each `PUT /attachments/recordings/:id?offset=n` appends one piece of at most 8 MiB (`RECORDING_CHUNK_SIZE`, 413 above it, keeping what fit) at `n`, which must be where the upload stands: a piece sent at another offset is answered 409 with the bytes the server holds (`RecordingOffset`), so a client whose answer was lost goes on from there. `GET` says where an upload stands and `DELETE` gives it up; an unknown upload, or one past its lifetime, answers 404. The bytes are appended in place to one file, `attachments/uploads/<id>.part`, opened so that a link at its name is refused, so a piece cut off keeps what arrived of it; one request writes an upload at a time. The piece that completes it, or an empty one once every byte is there, checks that the file ends as a zip does, hashes it as a stream and moves it into the blob store by a rename, under the store's lock as an upload does, so it is never read whole. The upload then answers with its attachment for as long as it lives, so a client that lost the last answer still gets it. A piece past the size the upload began with, or a whole file that is not a zip, removes the upload (400). Before it begins, and before each piece, the server checks the disk: what is still to come, a second copy of the whole for the bot's inbox, and 1 GiB kept free (`SPACE_RESERVE`); short of that it answers 507 with `Conflict("no-space")`, as it does when a write runs out of space. A disk whose free space cannot be read is not refused. An upload untouched for 24 hours is removed, when it is next asked for and by a sweep each hour, which also removes what an interrupted write left. A recording past what the engine takes with a message, 50 MiB ([dispatch](#3-a-dispatch-is-written-before-the-engine-is-called)), is copied into the bot's `files/inbox/` alone, and the envelope names it there with no number ([Wake envelopes](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). A server that takes recordings says so on the roster snapshot (`screenRecordings`, [Clients](clients.md#protocol-compatibility)).
- The dispatch lists its files in order. Before its first send, each the engine carries is staged in the engine, which mints its own id for it; the server keeps that id with the dispatch, so a retry sends the same staged file. A file is staged again whenever its `usableUntil` has passed before a send, including a recovery, because the engine claims the files before it looks at the key; a copy a recovery does not use is removed by the engine.
- A file a bot attaches to `say` is copied from its workspace into the same store, so the operator's devices can open it after the bot has changed or removed its copy. Paths outside the workspace, links that lead outside it and URLs are refused: the server never fetches a file for a bot. The workspace is its real path as the server recorded it when it first provisioned the folder, before the bot's first turn (`workspace_root`), never resolved again, so a link the bot puts in the folder's place leads outside it. The path is resolved and checked, then opened without following a link at its name and without waiting on a FIFO, and the check that counts is made on the open descriptor, since the bot can swap a folder on the way for a link between the check and the open: on Linux by the name the kernel gives the descriptor's file (`/proc/self/fd`), elsewhere by matching the descriptor's file to what the path resolves to again, which narrows the swap to one made and undone between two reads. This containment is a guardrail, not a security boundary: the bot runs as the same user ([Security](security.md)). Only a regular file is read, and at most `MAX_ATTACHMENT_SIZE` bytes of it, the limit of an upload: a larger file is refused once the read passes the limit. The files of one `say` together take at most 25 MiB (`MAX_SAY_ATTACHMENTS_SIZE`): the operator's upload path has no total per message, so a `say` takes one upload's worth, and each file is read only up to what is left of it, so a `say` past the total is refused while it is read and never buffers more. Every file is checked, its name included, before any is stored. The files are stored in the transaction that admits the `say` and publishes its message, so a refused `say` stores none of them; bytes written for a transaction that rolled back are removed before the store's lock is released. Writing a blob's file and counting it for that removal are one step an interruption waits out, so a write the file system finishes after an admission's deadline is still removed.
- The store has one lock over its files, so writing a blob and removing one never interleave. It is always taken before a transaction and never inside one: an upload, a `say` with files and a blob's removal take them in that order, so they never wait on each other in turn.
- `GET /attachments/:id` serves a file to a paired device only, with the device token as a bearer, streaming it from the disk.
- `bot.files` lists the files a bot has produced, newest first, for the bot's details view.

## Images a bot looked at

A step of a turn's work can show an image the bot looked at while it worked ([decision 0075](../decisions/0075-a-bot-sends-a-picture-as-a-photo-and-shows-what-it-looked-at-in-its-work.md)). The engine names, with a step, the path of an image file the harness read (`dynamic_tool.viewedImagePath`: Claude Code's `Read` of an image, Codex's `imageView`, the ACP and Pi read tools, for the extensions its clients preview; **Read**, T3 Code `bc0f04d`). `engine-t3` passes it on with the step as `viewedImage`, to the server only.

- **Kept by id.** When it observes the step, the server records the image (`WorkImages`, `work_images`), in the transaction that writes the step: when the path ends in `.gif`, `.jpeg`, `.jpg`, `.png` or `.webp` and its real path, resolved now, lies inside the bot's workspace (`workspace_root`, as for attachments), it keeps that path relative to the workspace under a new random id, `wimg_…`, and the step goes to clients with `image: {id}` (`WorkStep.image`). The path never leaves the server. Anything else gives the step no image. A step seen again keeps its id. The row goes with the step, its fold and its bot.
- **Served by id.** `GET /work-images/:id` serves the image to a paired device only, with the device token as a bearer. The server reads it from the workspace each time, by the same containment as a file a `say` attaches, at most `MAX_INLINE_IMAGE_SIZE` (10 MiB, of which no more is read), and serves it only when its first bytes make it a GIF, JPEG, PNG or WebP, with that type, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; sandbox` and `Cache-Control: private, no-store`. An unknown id, a file gone, out of the workspace, too large or of another type, whatever its name, is a 404.
- **What it shows.** The file as it is when asked, not as it was when the bot read it: one the bot changed shows its new content, one it removed nothing. An image only in a tool's output, with no file, shows none.

## Visuals

A `say` may show a page above its text ([decision 0030](../decisions/0030-a-visual-is-part-of-say.md)): `visual: {path, title, height?}` names an HTML file in the bot's workspace, which the server reads as it reads an attachment, by the same containment, and stores with the message as `MessageEntry.visual`, `{title, html, height}`. The page itself is never sent to the bridge inline, since the engine summarizes a tool input past 16 KB and J3 Bots matches a `say` to the engine's call by its input ([Bot tools](#bot-tools)).

- **What is refused.** A blank title or one over 200 characters, a URL, a path that does not end in `.html` or `.htm`, a path outside the workspace or that is not a regular file, a file past `MAX_VISUAL_BYTES` (512 KB, of which no more is read), one that is not UTF-8 text, an empty one, and one that embeds another page or holds a declarative shadow root: an `<iframe>`, `<frame>`, `<object>` or `<embed>` start tag, or a `shadowrootmode` attribute, anywhere in its text, which a client would not show ([Security: visuals](security.md#visuals)). Each refusal sends nothing, and says what to change. A `height` outside 80 to 2000 is refused by the tool's schema; without one, the client measures the page.
- **What is done with it.** Nothing: the server neither runs nor parses the page, which only a client's sandboxed frame renders ([Security: visuals](security.md#visuals)). It is stored as text in the entry and never written to a log, a trace, a notification or a push, which say the message's text. The roster's preview of the message is its text.
- **How it is served.** The web client's visual page, `VISUAL_FRAME_PATH`, is served with `VISUAL_FRAME_POLICY` and no `X-Frame-Options`, so the client can frame it; every other page of the client is served with `frame-ancestors 'none'; frame-src 'self'`.

## Offers

A bot's `say` may offer the operator one thing to do from its message ([decision 0098](../decisions/0098-the-desktop-app-records-the-screen-for-a-bot.md)): `offer: "record-screen"` puts a Record your screen button under it. The server checks nothing else and stores it with the message as `MessageEntry.offer`, `{kind: "record-screen"}`, in the conversation or room the `say` goes to; a `say` without one carries none, and no other message has one. The kind is an open literal (`MESSAGE_OFFER_KINDS`): a client shows nothing for a kind it does not know, and passes over fields added to an offer later. What pressing it does is the client's ([Clients](clients.md)): the desktop app opens its screen recorder, and every other client says to open J3 Bots on the computer. No migration: the offer lives in the message's stored body.

## Sessions

Each bot has one **main session**: an engine thread in the bot's project, where operator turns, room turns and teammate messages all land. One working context means a bot in a room can use what the operator told it privately, which is what makes it the same teammate everywhere.

**Side sessions** are short-lived engine threads in the same project, used for routine runs: each attempt of a run, and of each bot step of a routine in steps, in that step's bot, gets one of its own, never reused by another. They start with fresh context, see the same system prompt and memory files, and can deliver messages with `say` like the main session; what they say goes to the bot's conversation. They cannot use `ask`: its answer would wake the main session, which never saw the question, so a routine's turn puts its question in what it says. Of `set_routine` they may only pause the routine they run ([Bot tools](#bot-tools)). Their work is out of the operator's sight: a side session's turn shows no work fold, and a failed attempt no notice. A side session counts toward the concurrency budget like any other, and its turns count in the bot's usage (`usage.turns`), each naming its routine, or saying it was deleted since: deleting a routine unlinks its wakes but not its side sessions.

A side session is never aligned (**Settings alignment**, below): it is replaced. Its row names the bot's provider, read in the transaction that writes it, and it is opened on that provider with the bot's model, effort, permission mode and browser switch as they stand then, its project's browser access written first. Every write of a project's browser access, for an opening, an alignment or a side session, reads the bot's switch just before it, one write at a time and outside every session's gate: the last one written has the latest switch, and a write never waits for a turn to end. While the bot counts a browser it lost and not yet withdrawn (`bots.browser_withdrawals`, one more in the transaction that turns the switch off), a write that finds the switch still off withdraws it from the whole project instead, and clears the count it read only if no other was added since ([Engine: the bot's browser](engine.md#the-bots-browser)). The bot counts the changes of any of them (`settings_revision`), one more in the transaction that saves each, and a side session's row records the bot's count when it is written, before it is opened. The sender's claim refuses every first send into a side session whose bot has moved past its count, also one claimed before the change and given back unsent because the engine was unreachable. The routine runner then releases the attempt's dispatch, if one waits unsent, retires the session and opens a fresh one for the same attempt with the new settings, which does not count as another attempt; a send given back so nudges it. So no routine turn starts with a permission mode the operator lowered, also when its session opened before the change and waited for a turn slot. A side session whose first send was claimed and not given back keeps the settings it opened with, as a running turn does: the claim is where its turn starts. A retried opening returns the thread with the settings of its first attempt, which are never newer than the count its row records, so a change committed after the row was written has it replaced as well. One not opened yet whose bot moved to another provider is retired unopened and a fresh one written for the same attempt, as for a main session. The rotator never rotates, aligns or opens a side session: each of those reads main sessions only.

Once its attempt has ended, and the engine shows no turn it can still run and no background work in it, the routine runner retires it, checking again in that transaction that the server knows of no turn, dispatch, continuation or unsettled Stop there. Background work the run left running keeps it until that work ends, unless a newer attempt supersedes it: a retry of its failed attempt is due, or a newer run of the routine has started. The retry waits for the failed attempt's session to be out of use, so once the retry's delay has passed, the session is retired as soon as no turn can still run there. A newer run has a session of its own, so a run that succeeded and left work running keeps its session only until the routine runs again. Either way the archive ends that work, as it does for a main session replaced on recovered context. The rotator then archives its engine thread and stops following its events, by the same path as a replaced main session (**Rotation**, below). A side session the engine answers it no longer has, or archived from the engine's console, is recorded lost, without a notice, and then retired: the archive ends its event stream (**A lost session**, below), also while its attempt's turn is still starting or running, so its attempt fails and its run tries again. A side session lost before its attempt reached the engine is replaced by a fresh one for the same attempt.

A session's id is J3 Bots' own, written before the engine is asked for a thread and used as the key of that request, so a retried creation cannot make a second thread. The engine keeps a refusal for the key it was sent under, so a key it refused is never sent again: a main session the engine refused to open is opened later under its id with the count of refusals, a key that names a thread of its own (**Every bot has a main session**, below), and a side session it refused to open fails its attempt ([Routines](#routines)). A refusal of the bot's permission mode is the exception: the engine keeps no receipt for it, so it spends no key. A main session it refused for its mode is opened later under the same key, and a side session's attempt still fails, its retry opening a fresh side session under a key of its own, as every new attempt does. The engine's thread id is stored next to it once known, unique across sessions. Before each request the row records that the engine was asked (`open_requested_at`), and forgets it when the engine refuses the key, which leaves no thread; the refusal is read from the key's receipt before anything else is checked, under the engine's dispatch lock (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `Orchestrator.ts`, `dispatchWithReceiptEffect`). A model the engine no longer offers is refused before anything is sent, so the record is forgotten then too, unless an earlier request under the same key may have reached the engine. A session retired with that record and no thread id, after an answer that was lost or a crash, may still have a thread on the engine: before archiving it, the rotator asks the engine once more under the same key, in the lowest permission mode, which no policy refuses, records the thread id it answers with, and archives that thread (**Rotation**, below). An existing thread is answered as it was made; one the engine never made is made then, idle, and archived at once, which is the only way to be sure none is left. The prompt version the session was created with is recorded too, so a session started on an older prompt can be found and rotated.

Because the main session does not see what a side session did, the server keeps a **catch-up note** per bot, in the database. When the bot next wakes in its main session, the envelope begins with what happened since: "Routine 'Morning repo check' ran at 08:03 and told the user: …". Each message a routine run says adds one note, in the transaction that publishes the message, with the message's place in the conversation: everything said in a side session is its attempt's, also in a turn the engine started there itself, as when background work the run started wakes it. A silent run adds none. A fresh start drops every such note from before it, and one an unsent dispatch carried is dropped when that dispatch gives it back, so nothing said before a fresh start reaches the bot's working context; the conversation itself still shows it. A routine's own run history is not the bot's working context: it stays, and a routine's next run still reads what its recent runs said, also those before the fresh start, to tell what is new ([Routines](#routines)). The note is consumed in the same transaction that writes that dispatch, so it is neither lost nor shown twice.

**Rotation.** The server replaces a bot's main session when any of these is true:

- it ran 200 turns, a provisional threshold ([open question 6](../open-questions.md#6-when-should-a-main-session-rotate-and-what-does-the-digest-contain));
- the engine ran a turn on recovered context because the provider could not resume its own conversation (the turn ends with `contextRecovered`, [Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface));
- the operator moved the bot to another provider (another model on the same provider stays in the session, aligned as **Settings alignment** below describes);
- the operator asked for a fresh start (`bot.freshStart`).

Each records the reason on the session in the transaction of what caused it. A stronger reason replaces a weaker one (the run limit, then recovered context, then a provider change, then a fresh start); a weaker one never replaces a stronger. How the rotator waits depends on the reason, and quiet is the engine's word: before it retires a session it takes a synchronization marker ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)) once the session's events are known, and then checks again, in the transaction that retires the session, what the server itself knows: no admitted or open turn, no continuation pending, no dispatch on its way and no Stop unsettled.

- **The run limit** waits for the session to be quiet: no turn the engine can still run, also one whose provider has not started, and no background work pending, counting work the adapter could not judge yet and work the engine's stop does not reach ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)). Meanwhile the session still takes new work, so long background work, a dev server or a watcher, never holds the bot's wakes. Background work that may end with no event is looked at again every 30 seconds.
- **Recovered context** waits only until no turn runs or can run. Nothing new is planned into the session. Background work it still has is ended by the retirement, as for a lost session, and the server logs that it was when the engine showed background work pending.
- **A provider change and a fresh start** are the operator's own actions. Each first stops the bot's work through the Stop machinery ([Stop](#stop)), in the transaction that records it, and the session is replaced once that Stop settles and the engine shows no turn it can still run and no background work pending that its stop ends. Background work it does not reach, which a provider session the engine kept from before a fresh-context start still runs, does not hold the session: the retirement ends it, as for recovered context; a client says so wherever it offers the action ("Stops any work {name} has running."). Nothing new is planned into the session, and wakes written after the action wait for the new one. A fresh start clears the bot's context, not its inbox: a teammate's message the bot has not read is new work, so a held one waits for the new session, and an urgent one the fresh start's Stop cancels waits on as held ([Teammate messages](#teammate-messages)). A provider change's Stop leaves a new bot's unsent opening waiting (1. A wake is recorded). A bot moved back to its session's provider before the session was replaced keeps it: the reason is cleared and the held wakes go to it. A weaker reason the move replaced is not restored; the rotator asks for the run limit again on its next pass. A fresh start also takes effect when the bot has no opened main session: the one written and not yet opened is retired at once.

In one transaction the rotator retires the session and writes a catch-up note with a digest of the conversation, unless the operator asked for a fresh start, which carries none; memory files stay either way. The bot has at most one digest waiting: a later one replaces one no envelope carried yet, and a fresh start drops it. A fresh start also records where the conversation stood, and no later digest reaches back past that point, so a session lost after a fresh start brings only what was said since. A digest belongs to the session it was written for: when a dispatch that carried it is returned unsent because its session was retired or lost, the digest goes with it, and the session that replaces that one gets a digest of its own. The bot's new main session is then opened as any is, and its first envelope brings the note in `since-last-turn`, ahead of whatever woke it, so a rotation costs no turn of its own. A digest is far below the engine's input limit; only a message that alone comes near the limit leaves it no room, and it then comes with the next envelope (**Coalescing**, above). The old engine thread is archived (`retireSession`) and never deleted; a retired session whose opening was asked and never answered has its thread asked for first, under its key ([Sessions](#sessions)), and one whose key the engine refused has none to archive. A model the engine no longer offers to ask with waits as a refused archive does. Each retirement sends its Stop round and its archive under keys of its own. An archive whose answer was lost is asked again on the next pass. One the engine refused is tried again from 5 seconds, doubling up to 5 minutes: the engine refuses an archive while delegated tasks run in the thread, and the retirement's Stop only queues their end, so an archive sent right after it can be refused once (**Verified** on `0.1.11`, in the conformance suite; the code it rests on is unchanged at `bc0f04d`, `Orchestrator.ts` L4609-4621). Either way only that session waits, and the pass goes on with the others; a retired session holds no turn slot meanwhile. The server closes its event stream and never follows it again. Since no event of it is applied once the archive is recorded, also one the stream had already delivered, the transaction that records the archive settles what is still open there as a loss does (**A lost session**, below), without a notice: a turn the engine started there meanwhile, as finished background work can, ends interrupted. The conversation itself is untouched.

A dispatch planned into a session is written only if, in the writing transaction, the session is still live and still takes new work; otherwise the bot's wakes are planned again, into whichever session now takes them. So no envelope reaches a session the rotator has retired or reserved for its replacement.

**Every bot has a main session.** On each pass the rotator opens the main session of any live bot that has none opened: under the key of the one already written, so a retried creation makes no second thread, or a new one. A failed opening is tried again from 5 seconds, doubling up to 5 minutes, with no engine restart needed, and one the engine refused under a fresh key ([Sessions](#sessions)); an engine that takes no commands waits until it is ready. Once three openings or alignments in a row failed, about 15 seconds in, the bot's conversation shows a `session-not-ready` notice, once until an alignment succeeds: the bot keeps trying, new messages wait, and if it does not clear the operator checks the bot's model, since a model the engine no longer offers fails every attempt. A permission mode the engine refuses to open the session in, as the ceiling of an administrator's policy on its machine does until the mode is lowered or the engine starts again under another policy ([Engine: protocol facts](engine.md#protocol-facts-the-adapter-depends-on)), is no failure and is not tried on the backoff: in one transaction the bot records the count of its settings changes it was refused at, read with the settings it sent, and its conversation shows a `permission-mode-refused` notice naming the mode and the highest one the policy allows, when the engine reports it, and the two ways out: choose a lower permission mode, or raise the policy's limit and restart J3 Bots, which restarts the engine. Each active room it is a member of shows a `member-mode-refused` notice naming it, once for each refusal its conversation is told of: a room is told unless it already has such a line newer than the conversation's last `permission-mode-refused` notice. So a room that takes the bot in while the refusal stands is told then, and one that took it in while no refusal stood, as after an engine restart, is told at its next refusal, even one its conversation was told of before. The line says what happened at its time: the bot took no part there until the operator acted, and the rest of the room went on without it ([Rooms](#2-the-server-holds-waiting-wakes)). A turn the engine refuses to run in a session already in such a mode, as one opened before the policy was installed is, puts the bot in the same state ([The wake pipeline](#3-a-dispatch-is-written-before-the-engine-is-called), Refusals). The rotator then neither opens nor aligns its main session while that count stands, the dispatcher plans nothing into it, and new messages wait. Any change of the bot's model or permission mode moves the count past it and nudges the rotator, which opens the session under the same key, since the engine kept nothing for it, or aligns it, which nudges the dispatcher. A refusal is recorded with the engine start that gave it: the adapter numbers the engine's starts, one more each time it becomes ready, and the refusal names the start the call was sent to, read before it was sent, so it is never newer than the start that refused it ([Engine: the adapter's interface](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)). Each time the engine becomes ready, J3 Bots' own start included, the refusals of earlier starts are cleared before the workers are nudged, since the engine may have started under another policy, so each is asked once more; one the new start gave already stands, as when a send went to the new start before the server's startup caught up with it. A send under way across the restart names the start before, so its refusal is cleared or set aside and costs one more ask. The clearing runs once the turn machinery is released for the new start, and a refusal from an older start than that one, from an engine that has stopped since, is not recorded, so it can never block the bot under an engine that would allow its mode: the start released for is read in the transaction that records it, so such a refusal is either cleared or never written. The adapter counts afresh in each run of the server, so the server forgets the starts it recorded when it starts, and its first engine start clears what an earlier run recorded. The bot also records the refusal its conversation was told of, its count of changes and the mode the engine allowed: a refusal the engine repeats after a restart is not told again, and one at a later count, or naming another allowed mode, is told once. An opening or alignment that succeeds, or a turn the engine takes in the main session, forgets it, so a later refusal is told again. The provider of a session is chosen in the transaction that writes its row, from the bot's provider then, and a session is opened only on that provider: a bot moved to another provider before its session opened gets a new session on the new one, never one on the provider it left. Once the engine answers an opening, the server records the session's engine id and aligns it before it takes any work, since a retried opening returns the thread with the settings of its first attempt. A crash between the engine creating the thread and the server recording its id, or an answer that was lost, leaves that thread on the engine: a session opened again later gets it under the same key, and one retired first has it found under that key and archived ([Sessions](#sessions)).

**Settings alignment.** The bot's saved model with its effort, permission mode and browser switch are what its session runs with, and a session takes no work until the engine has them. Each session row counts the changes of its bot's settings it has not been brought to (`settings_pending`): a new row starts at one, and `bot.update` adds one in the transaction that saves a new model, effort, permission mode or browser switch, then answers with success. That transaction runs in the gate of the bot's main session, and checks that the session is still the bot's main one, or runs again in the gate of the one that is: a send claimed before the change reaches the engine before the change commits, and a send after it is refused. So `bot.update` waits behind a send in flight on that session; its receipt makes a client's retry safe. Nothing is planned into a session above zero, the transaction that writes a dispatch checks it again, the sender's claim refuses every send into it, a resend included, since the engine starts a turn for a key it never had, and a held engine queue there is not resumed.
