A turn can still start on an older model: the engine starts a queued run, a requeued steer's included, on the model it was queued or sent with, and moves the session to it, while a run for a delegated task's completion takes the session's model as it starts (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `Orchestrator.ts`, `queuedRunModelSelection` and `followsCurrentConversation`, L1058-1085). So each turn tells the model it started on (`turn-started`), and a turn of a bot's main session that started on another model of the bot's provider than the bot's adds one to `settings_pending` as it starts: the session is aligned again before its next send. A turn that started and ended while the server was away is not told; the replacement after the gap carries the model the engine holds the session on now, which is the model of the last turn it started, and the observer compares it with the bot's in the same way.

The rotator aligns such a session on its next pass, which the change nudges and an engine coming back to ready starts: in the session's gate, so no send and no other alignment crosses it, it sends the bot's settings as they stand (`configureSession`), with a browser switch that differs from the one the row records as a reload of the session's tools after its project's browser access is written ([Engine: the bot's browser](engine.md#the-bots-browser)), and sets the count back to zero, and the row's browser to the one it sent, only if no change committed meanwhile, which wakes the dispatcher; otherwise it aligns again. A session already opened is only aligned; its workspace and project are not provisioned again. A failed alignment is tried again with the same backoff as an opening. One whose permission mode the engine refuses waits for the bot's settings to change, or the engine to be ready again, as an opening does (**Every bot has a main session**, above): the session keeps the settings it had and takes no work meanwhile. A session on a provider the bot left, or one lost, is replaced instead. An alignment the engine answers with `SessionNotFound`, as for a thread archived from the engine's console, records the session lost (**A lost session**, below) and stops following its events, and the next pass replaces it.

- **Turning the browser off does not wait.** Its transaction also records the bot's Stop, as a change of provider does, keeping the bot's opening; and its alignment withdraws the browser from every thread of the bot's project before it checks for a running turn. So a turn running with the browser is stopped, and nothing the engine starts there afterwards has it.
- **A running turn keeps the other settings it started with.** On Claude Code a new permission mode replaces the provider session, which would end a turn running there, and close a request it waits on (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `Orchestrator.ts` L4871-4913, `ClaudeAdapterV2.ts` L660). So the rotator aligns only while the server knows of no admitted or open turn in the session, and the adapter refuses the change with `SessionBusy`, sending nothing, when it sees a turn the engine is preparing, starting or running. The turn's end nudges the rotator, which aligns then, with no backoff. The operator stops the turn to end it sooner.
- **Background work holds it.** The engine refuses a change that would replace a provider session still running background work, a delegated agent or a command left running (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `Orchestrator.ts` L4900-4913); the adapter reports `BackgroundWorkRunning`. The rotator looks again every 30 seconds, as for a rotation held by background work, which may end with no event. Meanwhile the bot takes no work, and its conversation shows a `settings-held` notice once per change: the new settings apply once the background work ends, and a Stop, which ends it, applies them now. The bot's `settingsHeld` says so to clients, which offer the Stop while it is true, also with no turn running; it clears once the alignment succeeds, or with the next change, until the engine refuses that one too, and once the session is replaced, since the next one opens with the bot's settings. A bot Stop reaches every live session of the bot, and the engine's stop, seeing the background work pending, interrupts the latest run with a restart of the provider's runtime, which ends a Claude Code process and the commands and agents it runs (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `dispatchThreadStop`, `ProviderTurnControlService.ts` L312-317, `ClaudeAdapterV2.interruptTurn`). Its settling nudges the rotator, which aligns then. A wakeup Claude Code scheduled for itself is not work the stop reaches, and holds the change until it fires (**Read**, `ClaudeAdapterV2.ts`, `hasPendingBackgroundWork`).
- **What the engine starts on its own is not held.** The engine can start a turn without J3 Bots: a usage-limit continuation at its reset (`autoResumeLimitedThreads`, `UsageLimitRecoveryWorker.ts`), or a wake from background work that ended. Between a change and its alignment such a turn runs with the settings the session still has, and the alignment waits for its end. The pinned engine has no fence that holds those starts and nothing else: a settled thread loses usage-limit resume altogether, and a snooze holds only that resume (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `UsageLimitRecoveryWorker.ts`, `limitRecoveryCommand`). The window is short unless the engine is unreachable to J3 Bots while it runs, or background work holds the change.

The digest is the delivered messages of the bot's conversation, the operator's and the bot's own, newest kept: at most the last 60 such messages, each cut to 1,000 characters, 6,000 characters in all with the names of their files, oldest first. Each message is kept apart with its author, so the envelope renders it as its own block ([Prompts](../prompts/architecture.md)). Notices and messages that never reached the bot neither appear nor count toward the 60. Memory, not the digest, is what a bot relies on across rotations ([decision 0003](../decisions/0003-conversation-is-not-session.md)).

**A lost session.** When the engine answers that it has no such session (`SessionNotFound`), to a send, a Stop round, a resume, an answer or the event stream, the session is recorded **lost**, once. The event stream ends so when the engine deletes the session's thread or archives it, from its console say: the archive cancels the turns still queued or preparing there, and the stream ends after those cancels, but it gives a turn already starting or running no end of its own ([Engine: protocol facts](engine.md#protocol-facts-the-adapter-depends-on)), so the archive is the end of all of them. A session J3 Bots had retired is recorded archived instead, as when the rotator's own archive ends its stream. Everything open in a lost session is settled in the transaction that records the loss, since nothing runs there any more: its Stop targets settle, each round keeping what the engine answered for it, a refusal included; a dispatch the engine had accepted, also one it queued, fails as `unresolved` and keeps the engine's first answer, its wakes failed and retryable, or cancelled when a Stop covers them ([What a Stop covers](#stop)); a delivered one, every open turn and its work fold end interrupted, each where the turn last showed activity, since the session may have been gone long before the loss was known, as across an outage; its open requests close as expired. A dispatch whose answer is not known is due at once and the sender settles it in the gate, as `unresolved` when it was sent. Nothing is planned into the session or watched in it again, and it counts toward no budget. Its loss only holds back its own work: every other bot goes on. A lost main session shows a `session-lost` notice in the bot's conversation, unless the server had already retired it, so its disappearing was intended; when the loss cut short a new bot's opening turn that no Stop covers, a retryable `turn-failed` notice follows it ([1. A wake is recorded](#1-a-wake-is-recorded-before-anything-happens)); the bot's wakes wait until it has a new main session, which the rotator opens at once, with the same digest a rotation brings ([Rotation](#sessions)).

### Effort

A bot's effort is how much its AI thinks before it acts: one of the levels the engine reports for the bot's model, or none, the default, which leaves it to the harness and is what every bot starts with ([decision 0082](../decisions/0082-a-bots-effort-sits-beside-its-model-and-the-default-is-the-harnesss-own.md)). Only the operator chooses it, in the bot's settings, as only the operator chooses a model: no bot tool changes either. A bot that another bot's `create_bot` makes takes its maker's model, but starts at the default effort.

- **Saved against the model.** `bot.update` refuses a level the engine does not report now for the bot's model, the new one when the same change moves it, with `Invalid("model")`. A new model that does not take the bot's level, sent without an effort of its own, takes the bot back to the default in the same transaction, whoever moves it: the operator, or the server moving a new bot to the default model. A model that takes the level keeps it.
- **Never a failed turn.** A level the model stops taking later, after an update of its harness, stays saved, and the engine adapter drops it before each turn while the engine does not report it: the turn runs at the default, and at the level again once the engine reports it again, unless J3 Bots has started again since ([Engine: effort](engine.md#effort)). The bot's settings say so in plain words, and the operator's next change of model or effort saves the default.
- **Applied like a model.** A new effort is a settings change: it counts in the session's pending changes and reaches the main session before its next turn (**Settings alignment** above), on the same provider session, without a rotation. Back to the default on Claude Code is held while the session's background work runs, as a reload of its tools is, since Claude Code clears a level in a new process. Side sessions open with the bot's effort as it stands.

### Another engine

Projects, sessions and the keys they were opened under are one engine's. A fresh data directory starts another engine, which has none of them, as when the engine's directory is deleted or a home is moved with `j3-bots import` ([Moving to another server](#moving-to-another-server)). So the server records the engine its sessions belong to (`engine_instance`), by the id the adapter answers for the engine that is ready (`instance`, [Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)), and compares it each time the engine becomes ready, before it follows any session or sends anything:

- the same engine: nothing changes;
- none recorded, as in a database from before the record: the sessions are taken to be this engine's, and it is recorded;
- another engine, or the record says the sessions belong to an engine this home does not have, as an import writes: in one transaction every session the engine was given, or is still to open, and has not taken out of use is lost (**A lost session**, above), since a session still to open is opened under its row's key, which the engine before may have opened already; each bot told once, and every bot's project is forgotten, then the new engine is recorded. The rotator opens each bot a new main session in a new project, with the digest a loss brings, and nothing is sent to a session of the engine before.

Without the record, the server would learn of each loss only when the new engine answered a read of the session, and never for one whose stream it still followed on the engine before.

### An older engine

The engine migrates its own data when a newer release first starts on it, and an older release started on that data runs until it meets what it cannot read ([Engine: upgrading the engine](engine.md#upgrading-the-engine)). So the database also records the newest engine release that ran on the home (`engine_release`). Before the engine starts, the server compares the release it pins with the record: an older one is refused, and the server exits naming both releases and `j3-bots update --rollback` (`EngineDataNewer`); the same one starts; a newer one, or none recorded, is recorded and starts. The record is in `j3.sqlite`, so it goes back with a rollback's snapshot together with the engine's data it describes ([decision 0077](../decisions/0077-the-engine-moves-to-t3-code-0-1-13-and-no-older-engine-starts-on-its-data.md)). An import keeps the home's own record in place of the export's: an export carries no engine data, so the home keeps the data its engine migrated, and the record that guards it. With `J3_ENGINE_BINARY` the release that runs is not the pinned one, so nothing is checked or recorded.

## Bot tools

Bots act on J3 Bots through a small MCP server named `j3`. The harness starts it as a local stdio process, one per loaded session; that process forwards each call to the J3 Bots server's tool endpoint, an HTTP listener of its own on `127.0.0.1:J3_TOOL_PORT`, which stays on loopback whatever `host` is and takes a request only as the bridge makes it ([Security](security.md#j3-bots-own-surface)). It holds no state and does no work of its own, because a host with many recently active bots runs many of them (see [Engine](engine.md#footprint)).

The bridge identifies its caller from `T3CODE_THREAD_ID`, an environment variable the engine sets for each stdio MCP server the harness starts, with the session's thread id. The bridge reads it with `sessionOfToolCaller` from `packages/engine-t3` and never names the variable itself. The server maps that id to a session and a bot. A call from an unknown thread is refused. The id alone is not enough: the bridge signs every request with HMAC-SHA256, over its path and its body, with a key the server makes each time it starts and the engine gives every bridge in `J3_BRIDGE_KEY` (`ToolBridge.environment`), and the server signs its answer over the request's tag and the answer's body. The endpoint refuses an unsigned or wrongly signed request with 403 before it decodes it, and the bridge treats an answer without the server's tag for its request as no answer, so the bot reads that J3 Bots could not be reached ([Security](security.md#j3-bots-own-surface)). A bridge started with no key, or one that is not one, has every call refused. **Verified** on Claude Code on releases `4941` and `4969`, and on Codex from `4969`, the pinned release ([Engine](engine.md#version-pinning)).

**Matching a call to its turn.** A request carries a session, not a turn, and the server's view of the session can trail the engine by a few events: a call made at the end of one turn could otherwise act for the next. So the server matches each request to the engine's own record of the call. The engine reports every `j3` tool call as a `tool-call` event in its turn, with the bare tool name and a digest of its input ([Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)).

1. It picks a token, starts waiting for it, and calls `synchronize` for the session. When its event applier has applied the `synchronized` event with that token, every event the engine had when the request arrived has been applied.
2. It looks, in the session's running admitted turn, for an open call to the same tool whose input digest equals `toolInputDigest` of the request's arguments and that no other request has claimed. It takes the oldest that no other request in flight has matched: a request reserves the call it matched, in the server's memory, until it ends, by when it has pinned it or nothing. Two identical calls a harness runs in parallel would otherwise both match the oldest, and the second's pin would fail though its own call is still open. The reading and the reservation are one step at a time for the session, so two requests never see the same call free.
3. The engine records the call apart from the request, so the call may not be there yet. Without a match the server synchronizes again, up to five times within two seconds.
4. With a match, the request is **pinned**: stored under the bridge's id for the request with the turn, the turn's audience and the engine's id for the call, which no other request can claim. The pin is written in the transaction that applies the request, publishing what it says, messaging the teammate or changing the routine, and that transaction checks again, from what is committed, that the call is still open and unclaimed in the session's admitted turn, with the input digest it was matched by (the engine records an open, unclaimed call again when an ACP agent reports its arguments late, so a request matched against the earlier input does not claim the call as recorded since), that the turn has not finished, and that the room the turn speaks for is still live, also for a `say` to the operator from a room turn. A retry of a request that already committed returns its stored result there; a result is stored for the request's id, session and tool together, so the same id from another session or for another tool gets none and is refused. The files a `say` attaches are read before and stored only once the pin holds. Everything the request does acts for that turn and audience, even if the session admits another turn meanwhile.
5. Without one, unless a forward of the same request committed meanwhile and claimed the call, whose stored result it then returns, the request fails with a typed `TurnEnded` result whose text the bot reads (`NO_TURN_REFUSAL` in `packages/prompts`), and nothing is published or recorded: the call belongs to a turn that has ended, or to no turn of this session.

**Subagents cannot speak.** A Claude Code subagent's tool call is recorded on the subagent's own thread, outside the bot's turn (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `ClaudeAdapterV2.ts`, `startSubagentToolCall`; Codex is **Assumed** to do the same), so it never matches and is refused by step 5. The tool descriptions and the base prompt tell a subagent to report in its final reply instead.

**Duplicates.** Several requests may match one call: the bridge forwarding the same request twice under different ids, or a harness repeating an identical call. The oldest unclaimed request claims the call, and every other one is refused at step 5, so one call acts once. A later identical call is a call of its own, with its own engine record: the model made it twice, so it is accepted and acted on once more. That fails safe, because each call still acts exactly once: a repeated `say` shows twice, visibly, and a repeated `remember` is deduplicated.

**Size.** The engine shows a tool input over 16,384 bytes only as a summary, which no digest can match, so the tool schemas refuse arguments over 12,288 bytes with a message telling the bot to split them ([Engine](engine.md#behaviors-j3-bots-designs-around)).

A call whose audience is a room that is now archived fails with `Conflict("room-archived")`, and nothing is published.

The bridge gives each request one id and reuses it when it retries the forward, so a retry finds the stored request, acts for the same turn, and returns the stored result instead of acting twice. If the engine cannot be reached, the server retries the match until the request's deadline and then fails it as retryable. A request has fifteen seconds end to end; past that nothing was committed and it fails as retryable.

J3 Bots' own `j3` tools are pre-approved by name in every permission mode, so they never raise an approval card, but those that change something ([What the bots manage](#what-the-bots-manage)) and `use_connector_skill`, which brings a connector's instructions in ([Connectors](#connectors)); a connector's tools are not either, and follow the bot's permission mode. How each harness is told so is in [Engine](engine.md#provisioning).

**Connector tools.** The bridge lists, after J3 Bots' own tools, the connector tools its session's bot is offered ([Connectors](#connectors)), each named `<connector>__<tool>`; none of J3 Bots' own tools has a `__` in its name. The bridge asks the server for the list (`/tools/list`) each time the harness lists, and falls back to J3 Bots' own tools when the server cannot be reached or the session is no bot's. Each bot's list has a version, which changes whenever the connectors say its offered tools may have, and which a server process never gives twice across restarts. Once the harness has listed, the bridge waits on the server for a version other than the one it listed (`/tools/changes`, answered at a change or after four minutes, and refused at once for a session that is no bot's, so only a session's own bridge can hold such a wait open) and then sends `notifications/tools/list_changed`, so a grant reaches a bot without a new session: a Codex bot in the same turn, a Claude Code bot from its next turn ([Engine](engine.md#behaviors-j3-bots-designs-around)). It sends each wait once, and stops waiting at the first the server does not answer: the server is stopping, and its harnesses with it, or the session is no bot's. So a bridge with nothing to forward never sends its session's id to a port the server has let go ([Security](security.md#known-gaps)).

A call to a connector tool:

1. is refused when its arguments are not an object or are over the same 12,288 bytes as J3 Bots' own tools take;
2. is matched to the engine's record of the call in the session's running admitted turn, as above, and claimed for its request in a transaction of its own, which commits before the connector is called, so the call is made once, and only for a running turn. Unlike J3 Bots' own tools, the record may have closed already: Codex starts a call before it asks for its approval (**Read** in Codex `rust-v0.171.0`, `core/src/mcp_tool_call.rs`), and the engine closes the record when Codex's code-mode `exec` yields, about 30 seconds in (**Verified** for a call that runs; **Assumed** for one that waits on its approval), so an approval that takes longer reaches the server with the record closed. The call must still be the running turn's and no other request's, and not one the operator declined: the server keeps which call an approval holds back when the engine names it beside the request, which it does on Claude Code and not on Codex ([Security: connectors](security.md#connectors)), so a decline ends the call for every claim from the moment it is written, before the engine hears it;
3. goes to the connector's client, which refuses a tool the bot is not offered and answers the rest as text the bot reads as untrusted, or J3 Bots' own line when the connector could not answer. The answer goes back to the bot and is not stored: the operator's data stays at the connector. So a forward of a request claimed before, after a lost reply or a server restart, is answered that the call may or may not have taken effect, and that the bot should check before making it again.

A connector call may take up to eleven minutes at the bridge, past the connector client's own deadline, and each harness's timeout for `j3` is twelve. So may `search_mcp_servers`, `add_mcp_server`, `update_mcp_server`, `remove_mcp_server` and `install_skill`, which ask the registry, list a server's tools, revoke a sign-in or fetch a repository; one with no answer is answered as a connector call is, with the bot told to check `list_mcp_servers` or `list_skills` first. A harness that gives up on a call sends `notifications/cancelled`; the bridge then abandons its forward without an answer, which interrupts the call at the server and, through the connector's client, at the connector's server. A harness that closes the bridge's input, as it does when it ends, has every call still running abandoned the same way. When the bridge gets no answer from the server for a connector call, it tells the bot that the call may or may not have taken effect and to check before making it again, never to try again: the server may have made the call before its answer was lost, and a new call is a new request. No progress is relayed: neither harness extends its timeout for it ([Engine](engine.md#behaviors-j3-bots-designs-around)). A connector call shows in the bot's work fold as a `connector` step, named by the connector and the tool, never by its arguments.

| Tool | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| `say` | Deliver a message to the audience of the turn that made the call: the operator, or the room during a room turn. Optional attachments, an optional visual ([Visuals](#visuals)), an optional `reply_to`, a message there it answers ([Quotes](#quotes)), and an optional `offer` ([Offers](#offers)). `done` marks the delivery complete. |
| `ask` | Put one question to the operator as a card with options. Ends the turn. Refused in a routine's side session, and from a turn of a room of bots only, whose operator answers nothing there ([Rooms](#rooms)). |
| `react` | React with one emoji to a message of the conversation or room the turn is for, by the id its envelope gives it, never one of the bot's own; a new emoji replaces the bot's reaction there. Notifies nobody. `done` makes it the whole reply, as `say`'s does. See [Reactions](#reactions). |
| `message_bot` | Send a message to one teammate, by bot id: `urgent` wakes them now; otherwise it waits for their next turn. Or post in a room the bot is a member of, by room id, from any turn, with an optional `mention` routed as a room `say`'s and an optional `reply_to` as `say`'s. Returns only an acknowledgement. See [Teammate messages](#teammate-messages). |
| `remember`, `forget`, `recall` | Write, remove and search memory, in the bot's own scope or the shared one. What was said in a room is not memory: `read_room` reads it. |
| `set_routine` | Create, change, pause, resume or delete one of the bot's own routines, by the same commands, checks and routine-change entries as the operator's, with the bot as the author and no approval card. A schedule and a time without an offset, also in an update of a routine whose schedule kept another zone, are read in the operator's zone as it is set at the call: the operator speaks in their own zone. Returns the routine as saved. A webhook routine's address is a credential. It lives in the routine itself, which the operator is shown, also through their live subscription and an operator edit's command receipt; in the stored tool result, which answers a replayed request; and wherever the bot then puts it: the calling system, a private `say` (`to: "dm"` in a room), or a command it runs, whose text shows in that command's work step like any command. The server never adds it to entries, notices or logs on its own. In a routine's side session the only call taken is `pause` of the routine that session runs, so a routine can stop itself once its job is done; every other call is refused, and in a step of a routine with several steps every call is. With `steps` instead of `instruction`, it saves a routine in steps ([Routines in steps](#routines-in-steps)); a bot step names a teammate by id, or no one for the bot's own step, and the routine belongs to the calling bot. |
| `finish_step` | Reports the end of the bot's step of a routine with several steps: its `result` line, its `handoff` for the steps after (at most 6,000 characters, and refused when a later step's envelope, rendered with the run's own trigger data, briefs and reports, would pass the engine's input), `goOn` when a condition follows, `ask` (the question and its two labels) when a yes follows, or `blocked`. Taken only in a step's side session, once per attempt; a report that leaves out what the following condition or yes needs, or gives what nothing follows, is refused with what it lacks. See [Routines in steps](#routines-in-steps). |
| `request_browser_help` | Ask the operator to do one step in the bot's browser that only they can, on its front tab, as a card. Ends the turn: the server stops it. Refused in a routine's side session, when the bot has no tab open, and on a tab whose last card the operator declined or let run out until they write. See [Help in the browser](#help-in-the-browser). |
| `ask_for_secret` | Ask the operator for a login, a single value, or a few values that go together, each under a name, with a note, as a card in the bot's own conversation. Answers the references, `secret:<name>`, never a value; the bot is woken when the operator saves it, declines, or it expires. See [Secrets](#secrets). |
| `list_secrets` | The references of the secrets the bot may use, each one's kind and a login's origins, never a value. See [Secrets](#secrets). |
| `fill_credential` | Have the server type one value of a login from the operator's secrets (user name, password or one-time code) into the field the bot focused in its front tab, after checking the page's origin. Answers only whether it was filled. See [Filling a login](#filling-a-login). |
| `search_mcp_servers` | Search J3 Bots' curated gallery, then the MCP Registry, in English: each match with the `id` `add_mcp_server` takes, how it connects, and the fields its form asks for. See [MCP servers and skills](#what-the-bots-manage). |
| `list_mcp_servers` | The MCP servers the bot uses, or, for the primary bot, every one: id, name, tool prefix, status, bots, whether the caller may change it, and who changed it last. |
| `add_mcp_server` | Asks. Add a server from the gallery, by its remote address with headers, or as a command with arguments and variables, each value a `${secret:<name>}` reference where it is a key. For every bot when the primary bot adds it, for the caller otherwise. |
| `update_mcp_server` | Asks. Change a server the caller may change: its name, its form's or headers' values, or a command's whole command line. |
| `remove_mcp_server` | Asks. Remove a server the caller may change. |
| `set_mcp_server_bots` | Asks. Primary bot only: every bot, or the bots named. |
| `list_skills` | The skills the bot has, its own and the shared ones, and for the primary bot every bot's: name, description, source and who changed it last. |
| `save_skill` | Asks. Save a skill from its `SKILL.md` text or from a folder of the bot's workspace, in the bot's own skills, or, for the primary bot, the shared ones or a teammate's. |
| `install_skill` | Asks. Install a skill from a folder of a public git repository at one commit, which is recorded, in the same places. |
| `remove_skill` | Asks. Remove a skill the caller may change, and its folder. |
| `list_routines` | The bot's own routines with their ids, instructions, triggers, whether each is paused, and their last runs. |
| `list_teammates` | Names, titles and jobs of the operator's other bots and the rooms this bot is in, each with whether it is hidden, and each room with whether the operator takes part in it (`withUser`, [Rooms](#rooms)). The primary bot also gets the rooms it is not in, as `otherRooms`, with their names and members only, so it can show or hide them ([Visibility](#visibility)). |
| `read_room` | Read a room the bot is a member of, archived or not, from any turn: its newest messages, or those whose text or author's name holds a search, oldest first. Answers framed text. See [What a bot has seen of a room](#what-a-bot-has-seen-of-a-room). |
| `create_bot` | Primary bot only: create a teammate with a name and a job. Taken only in a turn the operator spoke in, as `set_operator_name` is, so a routine, a teammate's message or a room wake only bots caused never creates a bot; and at most five for one request of the operator's, a room's six members less the bot that creates them. The request is the message or card answer that opened the turn, in the bot's conversation or in a room: the bots made by every turn of the bot that answered it count, whatever kind of wake carried it, so a turn that failed after making some and was retried goes on from them instead of making a second set. The call is pinned like the turn's other tools, and checked, pinned and counted in the transaction that saves the bot, so a retried forward returns the same bot and two calls at once cannot pass the cap together. That check includes the Stops that cover the turn ([What a Stop covers](#stop)): a Stop recorded before the bot is saved refuses it with nothing written, whatever the engine has done with the turn yet. The new bot runs on the caller's model while that model is offered and its provider ready, and on the default model otherwise, recorded as the server's choice either way. Its hello waits for that turn to end ([1. A wake is recorded](#1-a-wake-is-recorded-before-anything-happens)). `hidden` creates it hidden, as the caller's choice ([Visibility](#visibility)). Returns its id. |
| `update_bot` | Primary bot only: refine a teammate's name, title or job. |
| `set_visibility` | Show or hide a bot or a room in the operator's list, by id: what the caller made, or anything but itself for the primary bot, and never what the operator chose. Not tied to a turn: a routine's run may tidy what it made. Returns whether it is hidden now. See [Visibility](#visibility). |
| `start_room` | Start a room with teammates, in a turn the operator spoke in: the caller joins as its first member, and its kickoff message wakes the members. Any bot may call it, not only the primary one, on purpose: the operator's word is the gate, and a bot they asked for a group can only gather teammates that exist. `with_user`, required, says whether the operator takes part; `false` makes a room of bots only. `hidden` starts it hidden, as the caller's choice, and a room of bots only starts hidden without it ([Visibility](#visibility)). See [Rooms](#rooms). Returns the room's id. |
| `update_profile` | Change this bot's own name, title or avatar when the operator asks. |
| `set_operator_name` | Save the name the operator asked to be called by, for every bot. Taken only in a turn the operator spoke in: what opened the turn was their message, sent again by a Retry or not, or their answer to an `ask` card, or, in a room, an entry of theirs woke it. A turn they did not start (the opening, a routine, a teammate's message, a room wake only bots caused, a card they dismissed, which says nothing) is refused. So is their message steered into a turn that was already running: the engine tells whether the agent took a steer only once the turn has ended, so the refusal tells the bot to ask them to say it again once the turn is over. A steer the engine runs as a turn of its own opened that turn, and counts there. This way a name a bot only read cannot reach every bot's instructions; whether a name in the operator's own message is theirs or quoted is the prompt's to tell ([Base prompt](../prompts/base-prompt.md)). The name is trimmed and must be one line of a label's length, with no line break, tab or other control character, since every bot's instructions open with it; Settings takes the same name (`OperatorName`), and a refusal tells the bot why. It is saved by the same settings write as the operator's, in the transaction that pins the call, with an `operator-named` notice where the turn speaks: the conversation, or the room. After it commits, the base prompt and every bot's instructions are rendered again and every device following the settings gets them. A name equal to the one saved changes nothing and shows no notice. Returns the name as saved. |

The complete parameter schemas and the wording of each description are part of the prompt package. See [Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#tools).

### First contact

A bot's first contact with the operator is its hello, two or three short messages ([Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)), or, for a bot made for a room, whose hello `start_room` cancelled, its turns there until it first speaks. The server paces those messages as a person's would arrive in a chat: a `say` that follows another of the same turn waits before it is posted for as long as typing it would take, 0.5 seconds plus 14 ms a character, between 0.9 and 2.6 seconds (`typingPause`), less the time since the one before, so a model that took longer to write it waits no more. While a message waits, the bot's state, `thinking` or `working`, says `typing`, and every client shows "Typing" where it shows what the bot is doing, so web and Android show the same, and a notification of the next message comes only once it is posted.

A turn is part of a first contact when its dispatch delivers the bot's `first-run` wake, or when the bot's conversation holds a `made-for-room` notice and the bot had posted no message when the turn started. The bot's first message, in its conversation or a room, from `say`, `message_bot` or its engine's own text, is dated on the bot (`first_spoke_at`) as it is written, so neither a change of the room's members nor the room's deletion makes a bot that has met the operator new to them again. The same newcomer test, without the turn, sets a room envelope's `newcomer`, which asks the bot to open with who it is.

A bot's `say`s are posted one at a time, each from reading its files to its post, so each message of a first contact is paced after the one before. The base prompt asks for each `say` of a hello once the one before has returned, which is what keeps them in order: a model that sends them together breaks that rule, and its calls, each answered in its own request, take the lock in whatever order they are matched. A Stop that has come since the message before drops the next one, whether it waited or not: the `say` is refused, "Nothing was sent: your user stopped this work", and the bot's turn is already ending. The pause and the time of each bot's last message are kept in memory: a restart ends the turn, and with it the first contact's pacing. Turns outside a first contact are not paced, so a turn that speaks twice while it works posts each message as it comes.

## Rooms

A room is J3 Bots data: members in order, entries, and a status. An active room has two to six members, all equal: none answers for the room. A message wakes the members it is for, and every member reads every message posted in it, the operator's and the other members', in its next envelope ([0039](../decisions/0039-a-room-has-no-host.md)). Their order is the one the room lists them in. An archived room is read-only: it takes no messages and no changes but `pinned`, `hidden` and unarchiving, and it may have fewer than two members. There is no engine object behind it.

**A room of bots only** ([0103](../decisions/0103-a-room-of-bots-only-is-read-and-never-written-by-the-operator.md)) is one a bot started with `start_room` and `with_user: false` (`rooms.bots_only`, added by migration 66, false for every room before it; `Room.botsOnly` in the roster). The operator reads it and takes no part: the server refuses, with `Conflict("room-bots-only")` and nothing written, their message there (also a reply, a quote or `@everyone`), their reaction to one of its messages, and a `room.update` that changes its name or members. `pinned`, `hidden`, archiving and unarchiving are theirs as in any room (a room of bots only that deleting bots left too small stays archived, since its members are not the operator's to give), and so are `room.stop`, `room.forceStop`, deleting it and the Retry of a failed turn. The operator's word never reaches it, so its rounds count from its kickoff and it pauses at the limit ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)). A bot's `ask` from a turn of the room is refused with a reason that sends the bot to its own conversation with `say` and `to: "dm"`, since nobody would answer the card. A mention of the room in a bot's conversation points and writes nothing to it, as every such mention does ([Mentions in a bot's conversation](#mentions-in-a-bots-conversation)). The operator's `room.create` makes only rooms they take part in.

**Archiving** a room, by the operator (`room.update` with `archived: true`) or because deleting bots left it too small, is one transaction that cancels every pending wake addressed to the room, expires the room's open `ask` cards (an answer can no longer reach it), and records a Stop for every member session whose turn, waiting turn or dispatch has the room as its audience, selected as every room Stop selects its targets ([Record](#stop)). That Stop has the `room` scope and then runs as any other ([Stop](#stop)). A bot tool call whose audience is the archived room fails with `Conflict("room-archived")`, and host speech from a turn whose audience is the archived room is not published there, including speech a replacement replays after the archival. Unarchiving needs two to six members, which the same update may give. **Removing a member** records, in the same transaction, a `room` Stop of its work for the room, so its running room turn ends; it cancels its wakes still held for the room, releases its room dispatches never sent with their wakes cancelled, expires the `ask` cards it raised there, and takes the Retry off the room's notices of its failed turns, so it reads nothing said there after it left. A `say` its turn makes for the room after that is refused with a tool error telling it its work there is over; the dispatcher cancels any wake it meets later the same way, and a room start's send refuses a bot that is no longer a member, also between two of its files. The operator's room messages it never received count it no more, as for a deleted bot ([Deleting a bot](#deleting-a-bot)): every wake of its for the room that did not finish, pending, in a dispatch never sent, or failed, stops counting, so each message shows its delivery to the other members it woke, and `cancelled` only when it had no other. When the removal released a room dispatch of the member's never sent, the dispatcher is nudged once it commits: its other wakes may have waited behind it. **Stopping** the room's work (`room.stop`) records the same Stop for an active room and cancels its waiting wakes without archiving it: its cards stay open and it takes messages as before. In an archived room it fails with `Conflict("room-archived")`: the archival stopped its work. **Deleting** a room records the same Stop before the room's row goes. The Stop, the dispatches and the turns keep the room's id as plain text, with no reference to the row, so once the room is gone the one rule of [What a Stop covers](#stop) still tells its work from another room's: a dispatch of the deleted room that settles later, also one whose key the engine applies after the deletion, is covered by the room's Stop, and its wakes are cancelled.

**Background work belongs to the turn that started it** ([0013](../decisions/0013-background-work-belongs-to-the-turn-that-started-it.md)). A turn records the turn of its session whose work it continues: the one a usage limit's continuation resumes, or the one whose delegated task woke the session, when the engine names it ([Engine](engine.md#behaviors-j3-bots-designs-around)). It speaks to that turn's audience, so a room turn's background work reports in the room, and nowhere once the room is archived or the bot left it; its hops and its Stops count with that turn ([Budgets](#budgets), [What a Stop covers](#stop)). Each turn also records whether background work it started may still be pending: set when it ends handing off to such work, or when a report of the session's pending work lists the turn, then kept or cleared by each report taken while nothing runs. While turns of a session have it set, each turn's end makes a report due, which the Stop runner asks the engine for once nothing runs there, so a flag lasts little longer than its work. A room turn's end also leaves the turn unreported until the next such check, and makes one due, also when it told of no such work: the engine can count work a turn's end did not tell of, as a monitor the agent did not mark awaited, which is no hand-off but which the engine's stop still counts as the turn's ([Engine](engine.md#behaviors-j3-bots-designs-around)), and only its report attributes it to the turn. It waits while a Stop of the session is unsettled, whose rounds ask for reports of their own, and while the engine holds the session's queue, whose waiting turns keep any report from being the check. A report taken while nothing runs or waits makes it due no more and leaves no turn unreported, and the end of a turn that ran or waited makes it due again. A room's Stop targets a session while one of the room's turns there has it set or is unreported, and its round counts either as work of the room's that may still run: it is judged on a report ([Stop](#stop)), which flags the turn whose work it lists, and settles the target with no round when it lists none of the room's; while the report cannot tell, because a turn runs or may run unseen, the round waits for one that can, as for a flag. It never uses a flag kept for the whole session: the engine's rule for pending work does not ask which run started it, so such a flag would reach private work, or outlive the room's.

**A delegated task's report goes back to the turn that delegated it.** The engine steers a delegated task's completion, and a notice of it such as its question, into whatever turn of the session runs when it arrives, whichever turn delegated the task (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `Orchestrator.ts`, L7890-7936); only when none runs, or the running turn declines it, does it start a turn that names the delegating one (`EffectWorker.ts`, L366-423), which goes on with its work as above. So a room's result can reach the bot in a turn of its own conversation, and the reverse. The adapter tells each report steered into a turn other than the delegating one, once that turn has ended with it (`delegated-result`, [Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)); one whose hand-over failed there, the engine runs as a turn of its own that names the delegating turn, and needs no wake. When that turn is for another audience than the delegating turn's, the observer records a `system` wake, `delegated-result`, for the delegating turn's audience, with the report's text, once per report (`delegated_results`, also when the engine lists it again after a gap). Its cause is the work of the delegating turn's chain, so a Stop that covers that work drops it, and a Stop of its audience cancels it as any of that audience's wakes; a chain no dispatch began is covered by no Stop ahead of time, as a turn the engine starts on its own is not ([What a Stop covers](#stop)). The turn it starts records the delegating turn as the one whose work it continues: it speaks to that audience, and its hops and Stops count with that turn. Its dispatch, which carries only the report and the held messages it delivers, does not begin a chain of its own: a chain the engine began counts by what it borrowed, as before the report landed elsewhere, raised by those held messages ([Budgets](#budgets)). The base prompt tells the bot that a delegated task's result is handled in the conversation it was delegated from, and the wake's envelope tells it the report belongs there. A report that lands in another turn for the same audience reaches it where it should, and needs no wake. The report still reaches the unrelated turn, which cannot unread it: the bot is told to leave it for its own conversation.

**An active room says whether its work is under way** (`Room.work`), so a client offers its Stop without guessing from its members' states, which do not say whom a bot works for. It is derived when the room is read, from what the turn machinery records: `stopping` while a room Stop of it has a target not settled; else `running` while a wake for the room is pending, a dispatch for it is not settled, a session has a turn for it admitted, or one of its turns runs or still has background work flagged in a session neither lost nor retired by the engine; else `none`. A member's work for another audience never counts. The roster pushes the room again through its usual path: with every change of one of its members, when the room's work as the subscriber last received it differs (a turn's start and end, a background report, a Stop settling), and with the room itself when a wake for it is written or cancelled, a dispatch for it settles without a turn, a Stop of it settles without a round, or the operator's `room.stop` is recorded. A client of a server that does not send it reads `none`, and offers no Stop of the room.

**An active room says who is answering there** (`Room.answering`), so a client can show it at the room's end without guessing from its members' states, which do not say whom a bot works for nor who waits for a turn. It is every member at work on the room, several at once as members run in parallel, in member order: each one whose turn of the room has not finished, in a session neither lost nor retired by the engine, or whose dispatch for the room is live (`intended`, `accepted`, or `delivered` with no continuation pending), and each one with a pending room wake that is not a routine's, waiting for a turn slot or for a turn of its own to end, the room's or another. Member order holds while a member goes from waiting to working, so the list changes only when a member starts or ends, and each such change comes with a change the roster already follows. A member whose permission mode the engine refuses waits out of the room and is left out, as is a bot no longer a member; recording or clearing a refusal touches the bot, so its rooms are read again. It is empty unless the room's work is `running`, so a room whose Stop is settling shows nobody answering. It is read with the room's work and members in one transaction, so the three agree. The roster pushes the room again when what the subscriber last received of its work or of who answers differs, through the path above. A client of a server that does not send it reads nobody.

**The room is checked wherever its wakes are written.** `Wakes.record` writes no room wake, whatever caused it, for a room that is not active or a bot that is not its member, nor a wake that work for such a room causes, so no new wake carries a deleted room's id. A path that returns a room's wake to `pending` asks `covers` first (`wakesAfter`). In an archived room, retrying a message or notice fails with `Conflict("room-archived")`, and answering a card with `Conflict("card-closed")`, since the archival expired it; a failed wake survives the archival, so the Retry works again once the room is unarchived. Deleting a room deletes its entries, so each of these then fails with `NotFound` ([Client API](#client-api)).

When an entry is added to a room, the server decides who wakes:

| The entry | Who wakes |
| --- | --- |
| Mentions members | Only those members |
| Mentions nobody, and replies to a member's message | Only that member, as if the message mentioned it ([Quotes](#quotes)) |
| Mentions nobody | Every member but its author |
| Mentions everyone (`@everyone`, the operator's only) | Every member, each asked to answer |

The rule is the same for the operator's message and a bot's: a `say`, a `message_bot` to the room, the kickoff of a room it started, or what it said in a room turn through the engine's own `send_to_user`, which mentions nobody. A bot's message never wakes the bot itself, and once the room is quiet it wakes nobody ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)). A member the message does not wake still reads it, in the envelope of whatever wakes it next.

Every one of these is a `room` wake for the room's audience, written in the transaction that adds the entry, with why the member is woken: `kickoff` for a kickoff, `addressed` when the message mentions it, `everyone` for `@everyone`, and, for a message that mentions nobody, `user` when the operator wrote it and `member` when another member did. A member's waiting wakes for the room share one envelope, which takes the reason that asks the most of it: `kickoff`, then `addressed`, then `everyone`, then `user`, then `member`. Room wakes carry 0 hops: a room counts its bots' rounds instead, and a teammate message a member sends from a room turn starts a chain of its own ([Budgets](#budgets)). The operator's carry the operator's priority ([2. The server holds waiting wakes](#2-the-server-holds-waiting-wakes)); a bot's are held to the Stops of the turn that spoke like any wake work causes, for the audience that turn spoke for and for the room the wake goes to ([1. A wake is recorded](#1-a-wake-is-recorded-before-anything-happens)). A Stop of the speaker after it spoke does not take them back: what it said was said. A Stop of the room its turn spoke for does, as it cancels that room's own waiting wakes: when a turn of one room posts in another, that room's Stop, its archival, its deletion or the removal of the member cancels the wakes the post wrote while they wait ([Record](#stop)).

**A mention says whom a message wakes.** The operator's message carries the members it addresses, up to six, or `@everyone`; a bot's `say` carries `mention`, a list of at most six member ids, which the server honours only in a room turn. A message that mentions members wakes only them, and tells each that it is addressed, in the wake's reason; every envelope that shows the message names whom it mentions, so the others read whom it was for. No fan-out limit applies to a room message's mentions. The server refuses, with a tool error that says why and nothing written, a `mention` outside a room turn (also with `to: "dm"`), and one that lists the bot itself or a bot that is not a member; the operator's message fails with `Invalid("mentions")` for a bot that is not a member. The server never parses mentions out of the text.

**The members of a room run in parallel.** A message that wakes several members starts each one's turn at once, as far as the turn slots allow ([Rooms](#2-the-server-holds-waiting-wakes)), and each answers without seeing what the others are answering: replies may overlap, as in a group of people, and the closing line for a message to the whole room tells a member to read what is already said and to end its turn without a word when it has nothing to add. A member runs one turn at a time: a reply posted while its room turn runs waits, with whatever else reaches it for the room, and its next turn carries them in one envelope, unless the operator's word for the room steers the running turn first, which carries them then. A member busy elsewhere, in its conversation or another room, takes its room wakes when that turn ends; the other members do not wait for it. A member whose permission mode the engine refuses takes no part until the operator lowers it or raises the policy's limit, and the room shows one `member-mode-refused` line naming it; what was said to it then reaches it once. When the operator's room message wakes a member whose turn for that room is running, it steers that turn, so they can redirect it. It never steers a member's turn for another audience, which takes the message as its next turn. Wakes for two rooms never share an envelope.

**The envelope carries what the member has not seen.** A room wake is delivered into the bot's **main session**, in an envelope that names the room and its members, in member order, and that ends with a line chosen by why the bot was woken ([Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). Each room dispatch records the room's newest entry when its envelope was written, and the session has seen the room through the newest such entry among its dispatches the engine may have applied, one released, dropped or refused leaving its entries unseen for the next envelope, or that a read or a refused post of the session showed it ([What a bot has seen of a room](#what-a-bot-has-seen-of-a-room)). The envelope holds the entries the bot's wakes are for, then the newest of the other unseen messages that fit the engine's limits for one message, in room order, never the bot's own. Earlier ones are left out for good, and one line says how many. The entry that woke the bot always fits alone, so a room wake never fails for its size: the operator's room message is accepted only if it fits beside the room at its widest, with the longest name a room can have, as many members as it can have, and the longest names, titles and ids they can have, since the room may change before the wake goes; another wake whose entry does not fit beside it waits for the next envelope. Its audience is the room: during that turn `say` delivers to the room, and `say` with `to: "dm"` delivers privately to the operator.

A bot that has nothing to add ends its turn without calling `say`. That is the normal way a room goes quiet, and how a message to the whole room gets one answer from the member it is for instead of one from each. Each envelope's closing line asks less of a member that another member's message woke than of one the operator's did, so the members do not talk on among themselves once the operator's question is answered. A member posts in its room from any other turn with `message_bot` and the room's id ([Teammate messages](#teammate-messages)), and reads it from any turn with `read_room` ([What a bot has seen of a room](#what-a-bot-has-seen-of-a-room)).

### What a bot has seen of a room

A bot learns what a room said from its room envelopes, which land in its main session, the one its own conversation and teammate messages use too. So it usually answers about a room from memory. When it needs more, it reads the room, and a post it makes there from outside that room's own turn goes out only on what it has seen.

**Seen** is one notion, kept per session. A session has seen a room through a place when it was shown the room's newest entries up to there, or, by an envelope or a refused post, told how many older ones it was not shown, as an envelope tells it what it left out. Three things show a room:

- a room envelope the engine took into the session, through the room's newest entry when it was written (`dispatches.room_through`);
- a `read_room` of the room's newest messages that showed every message others wrote there after what the session had seen, through the room's newest entry (`room_views`); a read that showed fewer, for its `count` or its size, leaves them unseen, since the bot chose not to see them;
- a refused post (below), through the room's newest entry (`room_views`).

A view counts only for calls the bot wrote after it had it: each read or refusal records, with how far it showed the room, the newest of the session's tool calls the engine had recorded by then (`room_views.calls_known`, from `engine_tool_calls`' order), and counts only for later ones. A post written in the same model message as a read, recorded before the read ran, was written without what the read showed, and is checked as if the read had not happened. That the engine records every call of a model message before it runs the first is **Assumed**; a call it records only after the read's view counts that view. A session's view is only what its own envelopes, reads and refusals showed it: a session that replaced another after a rotation or a fresh start, and a routine's side session, start with none, so what one session was shown never lets another post. A read with a search shows a selection, so it moves no view.

What showed the session a room is one record (`roomShown`), which the room's rounds go by too ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)), read with the certainty each use needs. The check of a post and the rounds count only what surely showed the room: envelopes the engine took, reads and refusals. Room envelopes go by the same view, the session's they are written for: they leave out what its envelopes the engine may have applied showed (a dispatch written but not yet applied is applied before the next one), and what its reads and refusals showed. A session that replaced another, after a rotation or a fresh start, is shown again in its first room envelope what others wrote there that only the old one was shown, the newest that fit, as any envelope carries them: a rotation's digest carries the conversation, not a room's entries.

**Reading** (`read_room`). The bot names a room it is a member of, an archived one included while it is listed as a member; any other id is refused with nothing recorded, pointing to `list_teammates`. Without `search` it shows the room's newest messages, at most `count` (20 by default, 100 at most); with one, the newest whose text or author's name holds the words, case and accents set aside. Each message is framed as its envelope would frame it: the operator's words in `<message from="user">` with the name they asked to be called, every bot's in a nonce-tagged `data-` block, the bot's own marked `you`, each with its time in the operator's zone, whom it mentioned, and the names of its files, which the read does not carry. It says how many earlier messages (matching ones, for a search) are not shown. The result is held to 50,000 characters (`ROOM_READ_LIMIT`, well under an envelope's limit, since it is one tool result among many): the newest messages that fit go, at least the newest, and it says when fewer than asked were shown. A read is a turn tool, pinned to its turn as `say` is ([Bot tools](#bot-tools)), so a delegated worker cannot read, and a forward retried under its id gets the stored text again. A read of the room's newest that showed all the session had not seen spares the session's next envelope those entries, which the session already holds; the entries that woke it are always carried. A single message longer than 50,000 characters still goes whole, as the newest, as it would in an envelope.

**The check of a post.** A `message_bot` to a room from any turn but one of that room ([Teammate messages](#teammate-messages)) is checked after the room's membership, its status and the Stops: when the room holds messages by others after what the call's session had seen when the bot wrote it, nothing is posted. The refusal shows them, the newest of at most 200 that fit in 50,000 characters, at least the newest, with how many earlier ones it left out, and asks the bot to decide again: post as it meant to, adapt, or not post and tell the operator what changed, such as work already done. The refusal is stored as the call's result with the view it showed, so the bot's next post there, a new call, goes out unless something newer arrived meanwhile, which is refused the same way. Nothing else of the post happens on a refusal: the operator's word is relayed only by a post that went out, so the next post of the turn is still its first there. A room turn's own posts, its `say` and its `message_bot` to that room, are never checked: members answer in parallel, and a reply beside another's is accepted ([0040](../decisions/0040-the-members-of-a-room-run-in-parallel.md)). A read and a refusal show the room to their turn as an envelope does, so the turn's next message there answers what they showed ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)). See [0043](../decisions/0043-a-bot-reads-its-rooms-from-anywhere-and-posts-there-only-on-what-it-has-seen.md).

### The room's rounds

**A room counts rounds of its bots answering each other since the operator's word last reached it**, and that bounds how long its members talk among themselves, the same in a room of two as in a room of six ([0042](../decisions/0042-a-room-counts-rounds.md)). Every room message takes a round, kept with its entry (`entries.room_round`) and written by routing in the transaction that posts it. Three things carry the operator's word and are round 0:

- any message of the operator's in the room;
- a kickoff: a bot starts a room only in a turn the operator spoke in;
- a request of theirs relayed: the first `message_bot` to the room from a turn the operator spoke in, as `set_operator_name` takes it ([Bot tools](#bot-tools)), other than a turn of that room. Their word reaches a room once per turn, as one message of theirs would: the turn is recorded with the room (`room_relays`), and its later posts there are the bot's own. So the operator can ask a bot in its own conversation, or in another room, to take work to a room, also one that went quiet, as their own message there would. A turn of that room is left out because the operator's message there already was their word; what its bots then say is their own. Members woken by a relayed post get the wake any bot's message gives (`addressed` when it mentions them, `member` otherwise), whose closing lines have them answer what the post asks of them.

In a room of bots only, the kickoff is the one round-0 message: the operator writes nothing there, and a post from a turn they spoke in is the bot's own, which takes the round past the room's newest, and records no relay. So the room counts from its kickoff for good.

The room's newest round-0 message is the operator's latest word there; nothing else keeps it. Every other bot message answers what came before it:

- **From a turn the room was shown to**, by its room envelopes, a room turn and any steer that rode it, by a read or a refused post of its own ([What a bot has seen of a room](#what-a-bot-has-seen-of-a-room)), or a turn that goes on with such a turn's work (the turn a continuation resumes, or the one whose background work woke the session: `turns.origin_turn_id`), it takes one round past the newest round among the messages they showed since that word, round 1 when they showed none of it. Each room dispatch records the round its reply takes (`dispatches.room_round`) as its envelope is written, and each read or refusal as its view is (`room_views.room_round`): one past the newest round among the messages it shows. A steer counts once the engine has it: whether the agent read it is told only when the turn ends, so it is taken as shown. A turn's message takes the highest such round among the dispatches the engine took for it, its reads and refusals, and those of the turns it goes on with, that showed the room since the operator's latest word. So waiting wakes that share an envelope count from the newest round among them, a turn steered with the operator's message answers that message, and the messages one such turn posts take the same round until the operator's word reaches the room again.
- **From a turn shown nothing of the room**: a teammate's or a routine's turn, or the bot's own conversation, that neither read it nor was refused a post there, or a turn the engine started on its own for no room turn's work, it takes one round past the room's newest round since that word, each time it posts there.

A message of work the operator stopped takes its round too, though it wakes nobody. Two fixed numbers, not settings, act on the rounds:

- **Closing, from round 3** (`ROOM_CLOSING_ROUND`). A room envelope whose reply would take round 3 or later adds to its closing line that the bots have answered each other for several rounds since the operator last wrote, so the conversation is closing: a member speaks only if it is essential ([Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). In a room of bots only it says since the room started, that the room pauses soon, and that a member who speaks says what is settled and what is left.
- **Quiet, past round 4** (`ROOM_QUIET_ROUND`). A bot message of round 5 or later is posted but wakes nobody. Its tool result says so (`status: "recorded"`), and that nobody will be woken for it, so the bot does not try again. The first such message since the operator's latest word shows one `chain-stopped` notice in the room (`limit: "room"`). The room stays quiet that way until the operator's word reaches it again: from then on no bot's message there wakes anyone, also a late answer to an earlier round from a member whose turn was slower. In a room of bots only, which their word never reaches, that first message pauses the room instead: the room and the conversation of the bot that started it show one `room-paused` notice, naming that bot, in place of `chain-stopped`; when that bot was deleted before, only the room shows it, naming nobody (`botId: null`), and its tool result says the room paused and its starter is told. In the same transaction a `system` wake, `room-paused`, wakes the starter in its own conversation; its envelope names the room and asks the bot to tell the operator where it got to and what is left, and that more work takes a new room on their word ([Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). The wake is caused by the work that spoke, held to its Stops as the message's wakes would have been, so a Stop of that work drops it, and work the operator already stopped writes none. When that work was a turn of the room, the wake also records the room, so the room's Stop drops it while it waits, as it drops a post of that work in another room ([Stop](#stop)). A starter since deleted is told nothing, and a room archived or deleted before the wake is taken leaves it nothing to tell: the wake is cancelled and no turn runs.

So after the operator writes, two rounds of replies run as usual, the third and fourth only where they are essential, and then the room waits for them. Rounds replace counting hops in a room: every member's answer to a kickoff would extend one chain, so a large room would reach a hop limit while still answering the operator's own message ([0039](../decisions/0039-a-room-has-no-host.md)). They replace counting messages too, which made a room's size decide how many exchanges it got: a room of six, whose members answer at once, was closing on its first replies, while a pair took twelve ([0042](../decisions/0042-a-room-counts-rounds.md)).

**A bot starts a room on the operator's word.** `start_room` takes a name, up to five teammates as `members`, a `kickoff` message, a required `with_user`, whether the operator takes part, and an optional `mention`, a subset of the members the kickoff wakes; without it, the kickoff wakes them all. It is taken only in a turn the operator spoke in, as `set_operator_name` is, from any bot: the primary one usually makes the teammates first, but any bot the operator asks for a group may gather the teammates that exist. It is refused, with nothing written, when a member is the caller itself, repeated, or no bot, and, read in the transaction that pins the call, when a member was deleted meanwhile or a Stop covers the turn ([What a Stop covers](#stop)). In that transaction, it:

1. creates an active room of the caller, as its first member, and the members in the order given, as the operator's `room.create` does, but with no command receipt: the call's stored result answers a retried forward. It is of bots only when the call says `with_user: false`. It is hidden when the call says `hidden: true`, and, without `hidden`, when it is of bots only; its visibility is the caller's choice, not the operator's ([Visibility](#visibility));
2. shows a `room-started` notice in the room and in the caller's conversation, so the operator sees what the bot set up where they asked for it;
3. posts the kickoff as the caller's message with its mentions, routed as a bot's room message that carries the operator's word: it is round 0, and wakes, with a `kickoff` wake, the members it mentions, or every other member when it mentions none;
4. for each member made for the same request of the operator's, by a `create_bot` call of this turn or of one that answered the request before a Retry, cancels its pending `first-run` wake and shows a `made-for-room` notice in its own conversation, naming the caller and the room. Such a bot opens in the room: its first turn is the kickoff's.

A room may already exist with the same members; `start_room` makes another, as the operator can. The members then work in it as in any room.


**Waiting on the operator.** Approvals, engine questions, a bot's `ask` and credential requests raised during a room turn appear as cards in the bot's own conversation, and the room shows one `waiting-in-conversation` notice per turn naming the bot, so the operator knows where to look. A room turn that fails shows `turn-failed` in the room, with a Retry that sends the member's wakes again while the room is active and the bot its member. So does a member's room turn that never started because its dispatch failed, when no message of the operator's woke it: a bot's message, also once the operator retried it, has no delivery state of the operator's to show the failure on. When the dispatch carried the operator's message, that message shows the failure, with its Retry, and the room shows nothing. What decides is the entry that woke the member, never the wake's priority. A `say` whose files the engine would refuse in one message, such as an image above the inline limit, is refused with nothing written, as the operator's message is.

## Mentions in a bot's conversation

The operator's message in a bot's conversation may mention, in `conversationMentions`, the operator's other bots and the rooms that bot is a member of, up to six ([decision 0092](../decisions/0092-a-mention-in-a-bots-conversation-points-and-delivers-nothing.md)). A mention there points and delivers nothing: the message wakes the conversation's bot, as any message there does, and nothing is written to, or wakes, the bot or room it names. `Pipeline.sendMessage` checks each one before anything is written, and refuses the whole message with `Invalid("mentions")` for a mention of the bot itself, of a bot that does not exist, of a room that is archived, gone or does not have the bot as a member, for one listed twice, and for any `conversationMentions` on a room's message; an empty list mentions nothing, as an empty room mention does in a conversation. More than six is a malformed request, which the contract refuses before the server reads it, as it does a room's mentions. A conversation's message carries no room `mentions`, as before. The entry keeps the mentions as the operator sent them, and the envelope resolves them each time it is written, from the bots and rooms as they are then: a bot by its name and title, or "A former teammate"; a room by its name and whether the bot is still an active member, or "A former room" ([Prompts: wake envelopes](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). The message's size check counts each mention at its widest name, as a room's does. The bot acts on a mention with `message_bot` and `read_room`, which keep their own rules ([Bot tools](#bot-tools)).

The roster snapshot says the server takes them (`conversationMentions: true`), and a client offers them only then ([Clients: protocol compatibility](clients.md#protocol-compatibility)). They live in the message's stored body, so they needed no migration.

## Quotes

A message can quote up to three messages of its conversation or room: at most one whole, which it replies to, and passages of their text ([decision 0094](../decisions/0094-a-message-quotes-messages-a-reply-whole-a-quote-a-passage.md)). J3 Bots owns them, as it owns the messages: the engine sees a quote only as the envelope writes it.

**The operator** sends quotes with `message.send`, each the id of a message and, for a passage, its text: up to 2,000 characters, trimmed. More than three is a malformed request, which the contract refuses before the server reads it. `Pipeline.sendMessage` resolves them before anything is written (`entries/quotes.ts`), and refuses the whole message with `Invalid("quotes")` for a message that is not one of the same conversation or room, J3 Bots' own message, a passage that is not in its message's text exactly as written, two whole messages, and one quote given twice. A message in an archived room is refused before, as any message there.

**What the entry keeps** (`MessageEntry.quotes`), in the order sent, the reply first as clients send it: each quote's message id, its author (the operator or a bot), when it was said, and either the passage or the start of the message, up to 300 characters, never half a character, with whether the text went on. It lives in the message's stored body, so it needed no migration. A quote keeps what it read: a source deleted, or a bot renamed, changes nothing in it, and a client draws it without reading the source. The size check counts each quote beside the message, a bot's name at its widest, so the envelope that carries it fits.

**What a bot reads.** The envelope writes each quote after the message, as a data block of its own: its id, who said it (`user`, `you` for the bot itself, or the teammate), when, and the passage or the start of the message; then one line on what they are ([Prompts: wake envelopes](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). A quote is always of a message of the same conversation or room, which the bot reads anyway, so a quote shows it nothing new. `read_room` and a session's digest carry the messages' text without their quotes.

**A reply in a room addresses.** A room message that mentions nobody and quotes whole a message of a member other than its author wakes that member alone, as `addressed`, as a mention of it would; one that mentions members or `@everyone` wakes whom those name ([Rooms](#rooms)). A passage addresses nobody. The rule is the same for the operator's messages and the bots'.

**A bot** replies with `reply_to` on `say`, or on `message_bot` to a room: the id of a message its envelope gave, of the conversation or room the message goes to. The server keeps it as the operator's reply is kept. It refuses, with a tool error and nothing sent, an id that is not a message there or is J3 Bots' own, and `reply_to` on a message to a teammate. A bot quotes no passages. Its tool says to reply only where an answer would be unclear otherwise.

The roster snapshot says the server takes them (`quotes: true`), and a client offers Reply and Quote only then ([Clients: protocol compatibility](clients.md#protocol-compatibility)).

## Reactions

The operator and the bots react to messages with an emoji, as people do in a chat ([decision 0084](../decisions/0084-reactions-are-a-quiet-signal.md)). J3 Bots owns them, as it owns the messages: the engine never sees a reaction, only the turns one may start.

**One each.** A message has at most one reaction by the operator and one by each bot (`reactions`, keyed by the message and the reactor). Another emoji replaces it, keeping its place among the others, which are in the order each reactor first reacted; taking it off deletes it. A message carries its reactions in `MessageEntry.reactions`, read from that table whenever the entry is, so a later write of the message (its delivery, say) never loses them, and a change to them records a change of the message, which every client following it receives. J3 Bots' own messages take none.

**The operator** reacts with `message.react`: a message's id and one emoji, or `null` to take theirs off. The server takes exactly one emoji as Unicode defines one (`\p{RGI_Emoji}`, a flag, a keycap or a family included) and refuses anything else with `Invalid("reaction")`; a message that does not exist fails with `NotFound("entry")`, one in an archived room with `Conflict("room-archived")`, and one in a room of bots only with `Conflict("room-bots-only")` ([Rooms](#rooms)). It keeps no receipt (above).

**What a change tells, and to whom.** Each change is told to the bots of the conversation or room the message is in, never to the one that made it:

- The operator's reaction on a bot's message is for that bot. It waits four seconds for more changes (`reaction_signals`, `REACTION_SETTLE`), each change starting the wait again and cancelling a wake an earlier wait left that is not sent yet, so a change of mind reaches the bot once, as it ended, and a reaction taken off within it reaches it not at all.
