Then the reaction worker, which runs whether or not the engine is ready, wakes the bot with a `system` wake (`reaction`) for the audience the message is in, unless the operator's reaction is gone by then. A bot that is busy, with a turn running or sent in its main session or with other wakes waiting, gets no wake: the reaction rides its next turn instead.
- Every other change, a bot's reaction or the operator's on their own message or on another bot's, wakes no one: each bot there finds it in its next turn.

What rides a turn is a catch-up note (`reactions`), one per bot and message while it waits, which the envelope tells as the reactions stand when it is written, in `since-last-turn`; a note whose reactions are all gone, or only the bot's own, or whose room the bot has left, tells nothing. A reaction's wake that waits while another of the bot's wakes is dispatched becomes such a note, so it rides that turn rather than start another just after; one whose reaction was taken off by the time its envelope is written is cancelled. A reaction's wake is never coalesced, comes after the operator's messages, the rooms' and the teammates', and waits for a usage limit's reset like any automated wake.

**A quiet turn.** A turn whose dispatch carries only reactions' wakes is quiet (`turns.quiet`, `quietTurns`): its envelope tells the bot that a reaction is a light signal to answer only when it accepts something the bot offered and its instructions or memory say that emoji means yes ([Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). Until it calls `say`, `ask` or `react`, or speaks through its engine, it writes no work fold and shows no plan, a room does not count the bot among those answering, and when it ends it leaves no notice of a failure or of a reply it never gave, so one that ends without a word leaves no trace in the conversation. A continuation of it is quiet while it is. The operator's message never steers into a quiet turn: it starts a turn of its own once the quiet one ends. The steps it took before it spoke are not shown on the fold it writes once it does.

**A bot** reacts with `react`, on a message of the conversation or room its turn is for, which it names by the `id` its envelope gives the operator's messages and a room's entries (`read_room` gives none). It is refused, with nothing recorded, for an emoji that is not one, a message not there, and one of its own. A new emoji replaces the bot's reaction; the bot does not take one off. A bot's reaction notifies nobody and wakes no one, and pushes nothing. With `done: true` it closes the turn's delivery, so a lone reaction answers a thanks without a reminder that the bot said nothing.

## Visibility

**Every bot and every room is visible or hidden** ([0050](../decisions/0050-bots-and-rooms-are-visible-or-hidden-and-the-operators-choice-wins.md)). Hidden is a flag the roster carries (`Bot.hidden`, `Room.hidden`) and clients draw: nothing else on the server reads it. A hidden bot's turns, routines, rooms and teammate messages run as any bot's, a hidden room's wakes as any room's, and what they say notifies as anything does ([Notifications](#notifications)).

**Who may change it.** The operator, through `bot.create`, `bot.createFromTemplate`, `room.create`, `bot.update` and `room.update` (also on an archived room): each records the visibility as the operator's choice (`visibility_by_operator`), what they create as well as what they show or hide later. An edit form sends `hidden` whenever the operator moved its switch, also when a bot set the same meanwhile, so the choice is recorded as theirs. A bot, through `create_bot` and `start_room` (`hidden`; a room of bots only starts hidden without it), which record it as the caller's choice and the caller as the maker (`made_by_bot_id`, cleared when the maker is deleted; `create_bot` claims the maker's turn before it writes the bot, so a maker deleted meanwhile, whose turn went with it, ends the call as any ended turn does), and through `set_visibility`. That one is decided in the transaction that saves it, in this order:

1. the operator's primary bot is refused: only the operator shows or hides it, so the primary bot never hides itself either;
2. anything whose visibility the operator chose is refused, shown or hidden alike;
3. the primary bot may change the rest;
4. any other bot only what it made.

A refusal changes nothing, records nothing, and tells the bot why. The call carries a command receipt keyed by its request, the same for a bot and a room, so a retried forward returns the first answer, also when the bot it changed was deleted meanwhile. A bot or room from before migration 41 counts as the operator's choice.

## Deleting a bot

Deleting a bot removes its engine project first, so nothing keeps running for a bot that no longer exists, and waits for a session opening in progress to finish, so the rotator opens no session for a bot being deleted; while the engine cannot take that, the deletion fails with `Conflict("engine-unavailable")` and changes nothing. Then one transaction removes its conversation, sessions, wakes, routines and memory. What it said elsewhere stays:

- Entries it wrote in rooms keep their author id. Nothing refers to the bot row, so the entries outlive it, and clients render the author as a deleted bot.
- It leaves every room it was in, in the same transaction; the others keep their order.
- A room left with fewer than two members is archived, in the same transaction, as [Rooms](#rooms) describes: it stays readable and takes no new messages.
- Files it produced stay in the attachment store while an entry refers to them.
- The bots and rooms it made stay as they are, visible or hidden, and no longer name a maker: from then on only the primary bot or the operator changes them ([Visibility](#visibility)).
- Messages it sent teammates stay theirs: a held one still reaches its recipient, and one a Stop of it ended stays ended ([Teammate messages](#teammate-messages)).
- Messages teammates sent it that it never read go with its conversation and inbox; each sender's card that still showed `sent` shows `not-delivered`.
- The operator's room messages to it show their delivery to the other members they woke; one that only it was to receive, and never did, shows `cancelled`.

## Teammate messages

`message_bot` sends one teammate a message, or posts in a room. Its `to` is a bot id or a room id. The call is pinned to its turn as `say` is ([Bot tools](#bot-tools)), and the transaction that pins it does the rest.

**To a room.** The bot must be a member of the room and the room active; otherwise the call is refused with nothing written, and so is `urgent: true`, which is a teammate's (`false` is the default and is taken). From any turn but one of that room, it is also refused while others wrote there what the turn's session has not seen, with what it missed ([What a bot has seen of a room](#what-a-bot-has-seen-of-a-room)). The post is the bot's room message, from any turn, whatever audience the turn speaks for: its `mention` is checked as a room `say`'s is (members only, never the bot itself), and it is routed as one, taking the round past what it answers, unless it is the first post there of a turn the operator spoke in, which relays their request and is round 0, in any room but one of bots only ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)). A Stop that covers the turn refuses it, as for a teammate, and so does a Stop of that room recorded since the turn's work began, by a dispatch or by the engine on its own: a room Stop is recorded for every member of the room, also one with no work there, so a turn of the member's own conversation that was already running posts nothing more to it. Begun is the earliest start of the turn and the turns it goes on with, compared with the Stop's time, since a turn the engine started on its own has no dispatch to order it by. Nothing shows in either bot's own conversation or in a teammate conversation. The acknowledgement is `posted`, or, when the room was quiet and the post woke nobody, `recorded`, saying so ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)); from a turn of another audience that the operator did not speak in, it adds that a message they sent while the turn ran does not count, as for `set_operator_name`, so the bot asks them to ask again once it has finished; not in a room of bots only, which no word of theirs starts again.

**To a teammate.** `mention` is refused: it goes with a room.

1. **Refuse what is not a teammate's to get.** A recipient that is the sender itself, or no bot (never created, or deleted), is refused, and so is work a Stop covers: a Stop of the sender, or of the room its turn speaks for, recorded while the turn ran or after the dispatch that began its work was written ([What a Stop covers](#stop)). A turn the engine started on its own has no such dispatch of its own: a Stop covers it as it covers the turn it continues, when recorded while it ran, or when a Stop of the sender was still unsettled in its session as it started. Nothing is written.
2. **Apply the budgets** ([Budgets](#budgets)). Past the fan-out limit the call is refused with nothing written. Past the hop limit it is refused, and the sender's conversation shows one `chain-stopped` notice for the turn, however often the turn tries.
3. **Show it in both conversations.** A handoff card goes into each: `sent` in the sender's conversation and `received` in the recipient's, each with the text and whether it is urgent. The message's row (`handoffs`) records the call that sent it, the recipient and both cards, and lives as long as the recipient's card: deleting the sender takes its call, never the row or the mark a Stop left on it. Clients show each card as one line naming the other bot, "Message to Wren" or "Message from Scout", which opens the message in full with a way to that bot's conversation while it exists ([Design language](../design/design-language.md), "Event line"). Both cards show where the message stands, the same on each: `sent` (on its way or read, which they do not tell apart), `not-delivered`, `stopped` or `unconfirmed`. The same transaction lists the message in its pair's teammate conversation ([Teammate conversations](#teammate-conversations)). Neither card calls for the operator: it never notifies, urgent or not ([Notifications](#notifications)), never makes its conversation unread, and is never the bot's roster preview or its latest activity ([Client API](#client-api)). A bot that needs the operator says so with `say`.
4. **Urgent:** a `bot` wake for the recipient's conversation, carrying its hop count and the recipient's card, and caused by the sender's work, so it is held to the sender's Stops like any wake work causes ([1. A wake is recorded](#1-a-wake-is-recorded-before-anything-happens)). The dispatcher takes it as any `bot` wake: a turn once the recipient's main session is idle, after the operator's and the rooms' wakes, and wakes that wait together share one envelope, one data block per message.
5. **Not urgent:** a held message, which wakes nobody. The recipient's next main-session dispatch, of any kind, carries it at the top of its envelope (`since-last-turn`), held messages in the order they were sent, and consumes it in the transaction that writes that dispatch. It goes back for the next envelope only when the engine certainly never applied that one ([Catch-up notes and held messages](#3-a-dispatch-is-written-before-the-engine-is-called)), so it reaches one envelope the bot may have read, never two. When that envelope's dispatch ends with nothing to tell whether the engine took it (its recovery gave up, or its session was lost before its turn started), the message stays with it, and both cards show `unconfirmed`; they show `sent` once the engine turns out to have taken it. A routine's side session never takes it.

**A failed urgent message never looks delivered.** When the dispatch carrying its wake fails (the engine refused it, or whether it took it could not be learned) or the engine dropped it before its turn started, the wake fails as an answer's does ([What a Stop covers](#stop)): both cards show it `not-delivered`, and the recipient's conversation shows one retryable `handoff-failed` notice per dispatch, naming each sender whose message it carried. `notice.retry` sends again the wakes that failed, never one a Stop cancelled, as they were: a `bot` wake with its hop count, so a retry does not start the chain again. The Retry itself is the operator's action, so no Stop of the sender cancels it, whenever that Stop is recorded, nor removes the held copy a Stop of the recipient makes of it; if its dispatch fails too, the cards and a new notice show that again. The cards show `sent` again once it goes; so does a late delivery, when the engine had taken it after all. A message that reached the recipient once, by whichever wake or held copy, stays `sent` and goes no more: the delivery that shows it read cancels a Retry still waiting and removes a held copy no envelope took, the dispatcher checks again before it writes either into an envelope and once more when it claims the first send of an envelope written before (which it releases, so whatever else it carried is planned again without the message), and no notice of it, the original's or a Retry's, offers a Retry. A Retry already sent when the original lands cannot be called back; a failure of it shows nothing and offers no Retry. The sender's tool call keeps the result it was given: the failure is the operator's to see and retry, not the bot's.

**A Stop of the sender.** A message belongs to the turn that sent it. A Stop covers it only when it reaches that turn while the turn runs: recording the Stop marks the running turns in its scope ([Record](#stop)), and with them each message they sent that no earlier Stop marked. A message from a turn that had already ended is that work's finished result: a later Stop of the sender leaves it as it is, an urgent one waiting for its turn and a held one for an envelope, also when the engine started that turn on its own. For a message it covers, recording the Stop cancels the urgent message's wake while it waits and removes a held message no envelope has taken, in the Stop's own transaction, and both cards show `stopped`. One already in a dispatch is left to it: if that dispatch does not run to its end, the wake is cancelled, or the held message removed, instead of waiting for the next one, and the cards show `stopped`; if the engine turns out to have started its turn after all, also after its recovery gave up, the cards show `sent`. Whether its wakes then count as delivered depends on the dispatch: one no Stop fenced runs its turn to the end, so wakes cancelled meanwhile count as delivered again; one a Stop fenced has its turn ended by that Stop, and its wakes stay cancelled, though a teammate's message among them was read all the same. The operator's Retry of an urgent message is the operator's own word: no Stop of the sender cancels it, also when the Retry is already in a dispatch that then fails or is released, or held after a Stop of the recipient.

**A Stop of the recipient.** The operator stopping the recipient stops the recipient's work, not what its teammates told it. An urgent message whose wake that Stop cancels before the engine had it (while it waits, in a dispatch the Stop releases, or in one the engine drops before its turn started) becomes a held message: the recipient's next envelope carries it, once, where it was sent among the held messages, and both cards stay `sent`. So does the operator's Retry of one, also after a Stop of the sender. One in a dispatch whose recovery gave up may have been read, so it is not carried again, and both cards show `unconfirmed`. A held message stays held, as any does. A fresh start, which stops the bot's work first, does the same: it clears the bot's context, not its inbox.

The sender gets an acknowledgement (`sent` or `held`), never a reply. A reply is a separate `message_bot` call from the other side and arrives as its own wake or held message. A refusal reaches the bot as the tool's error, with what to do instead. One that wrote its notice is stored as the request's result, so a retried forward refuses the same way and adds no second notice.

The prompt tells bots not to answer an acknowledgement, a thanks or an FYI. The budgets only bound how far an exchange goes when one answers anyway; they do not keep a bot silent.

## Teammate conversations

The messages two bots sent each other make their **teammate conversation**, which the operator reads and never writes into ([decision 0038](../decisions/0038-a-teammate-conversation-is-a-view-of-teammate-messages.md)). It is a view: a message stays its two handoff cards and its `handoffs` row, which delivery works from, and nothing about it moves.

- **One per pair of bots**, addressed by the two bot ids, sorted. It has no id of its own, and no read mark, pin or section. A pair with no messages is not one.
- **Its messages** are listed from `pair_messages`, one row per message, which `message_bot` writes with the cards (migration 31 filled it from every message sent before). A message is read from the recipient's card, or from the sender's once the recipient is deleted: they say the same, its state included. Each has `seq`, its place among all teammate messages, given in the order they were sent and never given again, which a client sorts and upserts by and pages back with. It also names both cards (`sentEntryId`, `receivedEntryId`), so a client maps a handoff card in a bot's conversation to the message.
- **A deleted bot.** Deleting a bot takes its conversation, so its cards: the row names the other card only, and the pair stays readable from the other bot's side, its messages that will never reach the deleted bot marked `not-delivered` by the deletion ([Deleting a bot](#deleting-a-bot)). Once neither card is left, which takes deleting both bots, the row goes (a trigger on the cards' deletion) and the pair with it. Hiding a bot changes nothing here: clients decide where a pair with a hidden or deleted bot shows.
- **`pairs.subscribe`** sends every pair, latest message first, each with its latest message (its text cut to `PREVIEW_LENGTH`) and how many messages it holds, then each pair whose row changes (a new message, a change of its latest one's state, a card gone with its bot) and each pair that is gone. It follows the two bots' conversations' changes and reads a pair again only when a changed entry is one of its cards, or when a bot itself changed (its deletion). It does not resume.
- **`pairs.messages.subscribe`** sends one pair's latest messages, then each message whose card changed, as whole messages, once per change: both bots' conversations are read in one step, so a message whose two cards change together, as when it is sent or its state changes, comes once. A frame is bounded as a conversation's is (8 MB of text, at least one message); a change too large to replay, or a gap in the change history, sends a fresh snapshot, and so does the deletion of either bot, whose cards go without a change being recorded. **`pairs.history`** pages back by `seq`. It does not resume either.

## Routines

A routine is a row with a trigger and either an instruction or, since 0.12.0, a list of steps ([Routines in steps](#routines-in-steps)). The server's scheduler owns it; the engine's scheduled tasks are not used ([decision 0005](../decisions/0005-j3-bots-owns-wakes.md)).

**Triggers.**

- **Schedule triggers** are five-field cron expressions read as wall-clock time in the time zone saved with the trigger. A bot's `set_routine` saves the operator's zone as set at the call, also when it gives a new schedule to a routine whose schedule kept another zone; a schedule nobody changes keeps its zone. A time the clocks repeat when they go back runs once, at its first occurrence. A time the clocks skip runs once that day, as much later as the clocks moved: 02:30 on the night the clocks skip 02:00 to 03:00 runs at 03:30. Two times that land on one instant run once. A schedule is refused with `Invalid("schedule")` when it does not parse, has a seconds field, names no date that exists (`0 0 31 2 *`), or runs twice within five minutes anywhere in the next eight days, and with `Invalid("time-zone")` when it names a zone the server does not know.
- **One-time triggers** run once at a set instant, in UTC, and then pause the routine. They are how "remind me tomorrow at nine" is kept. A one-time trigger must lie ahead when it is saved.

A new trigger is checked when it is saved, also on a paused routine, so a resume never meets one that could not have been saved. An update that sends the saved trigger again, as a form sending the whole draft does, gives no new trigger: it is not checked again, the next run does not move, and nothing starts. A one-time routine whose time has passed, whether it ran or was paused before its time, starts again when given a new trigger, from the operator or the bot, unless the same update pauses it. Any other paused routine keeps its pause through an update.
- **Webhook triggers** are an HTTP endpoint on the J3 Bots server; see [Webhooks](#webhooks). The request body reaches the bot as data inside a nonce-tagged block.
- **Event triggers** are an event a connector's server offers (MCP's Events extension): the routine runs when that server says it happened, such as a new email from one sender. Only a bot sets one, with `set_routine`, from what the connector lists; see [Event routines](#event-routines).

**The scheduler.** Each routine stores its next run. The scheduler wakes at the earliest of them, at least once a minute, and when a command changes a routine, each counted from when it last looked: a routine that comes due while it is looking is looked at as soon as it is done. For each routine that came due it records the run and the next run in one transaction, so a run is never recorded twice, also across a restart. Each routine is read again in that transaction, so an edit committed meanwhile wins. A routine whose run cannot be recorded is logged and tried again 30 seconds later, unless it was edited meanwhile; the other routines carry on. A schedule that no longer has a next run, such as one in a zone the server no longer knows, runs that once and pauses its routine, with a warning in the log. A routine whose stored trigger the server cannot read, such as one written by another version, runs nothing and is paused, with one warning. Either pause also shows a `routine-paused` notice in the bot's conversation, which names the routine and says to edit it to turn it back on. Anything else that goes wrong in the scheduler is logged, and it looks again 30 seconds later.

- **Runs missed while the server was not running** (shut down, crashed or asleep, which the server cannot tell apart) are not replayed. Of the runs that came due, only the latest can run: it runs once the server is back if the server is at least as near to it as to the schedule's next run, and the routine goes on from there. Past that half of the gap, the routine runs nothing now, waits for its next run, and counts every run that came due as **missed** ([decision 0021](../decisions/0021-a-missed-routine-runs-only-while-it-is-nearer-than-the-next.md)). So a daily 08:00 routine back at 10:00 runs, and one back at 07:00 the next day waits an hour for 08:00; an hourly one back at :40 waits for the hour; a weekly one runs up to three and a half days late; and a weekday 09:00 one that missed Friday's run still runs it on Saturday up to 21:00, halfway to Monday's. After a downtime of several runs, only the latest runs, and the earlier ones are not counted as missed. The rule has no constant of its own: half of the gap is at least two and a half minutes, since runs are at least five minutes apart, so a run a few seconds late always runs, and a night the clocks shorten or lengthen moves its half. A one-time trigger always runs, however late. The count of a routine's missed runs, with the first and the last, is kept on the routine until its next run starts, and is cleared when the routine gets a new trigger; a routine that misses runs again before that adds them up. It counts up to 1,000: a routine at that count missed at least as many. The cost lies in the transaction that records a run. Finding the latest run and the next one each walks about two days of the schedule's runs, since a run's wall-clock time can lie up to a day from its instant: some 580 steps each for a schedule every five minutes, a handful for a daily one. Counting the missed runs, only when the routine skips, reads at most 1,000 more, so a long stretch of a frequent schedule costs no more than that. Runs, and missed ones, are told by the schedule's own times: a run postponed 30 seconds after it could not be recorded is still the 08:00 run.
- A clock set forward makes the runs it jumps over come due, and the rule above limits what runs. A clock set back moves nothing: the next run is still ahead, and nothing runs twice. A schedule keeps the zone it was saved with ([Triggers](#routines)) when the computer moves to another zone, so an 08:00 routine saved in São Paulo still runs at 08:00 there.
- A scheduled run keeps when it was due. Clients are told that time for a run that started more than a minute after it (`dueAt`), and the routine envelope gives it to the bot, so it reads the dates in the instruction against the day the run was due ([Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). An attempt tried again more than a minute after its run started gives that time too. The envelope also gives it when the run was due on another day in the operator's zone, however little before: a run due at 23:59:45 that starts at 00:00:15 is not late for clients, but its "today" is the day before.
- A run that comes due while the previous run of the same routine is still going is recorded as `skipped` and does not count as a run.
- `routine.run` runs a routine on demand, paused or not, without resuming it or moving its next run. A retried command is the same run.

**Running.** A run records a `routine` wake for the routine's bot through the wake pipeline, and the run keeps that wake. The main session's dispatcher never takes a routine wake, and the main session is never woken for one. The **routine runner**, a worker like the others ([The workers](#the-workers)), takes it instead:

1. Once the run's next attempt may start, the attempt it retries has its side session archived in the engine or lost (a send of it whose answer was lost may land there yet), and the bot's provider is not at its usage limit, it writes a side session for the attempt's wake, on the bot's provider, which starts the attempt. Then it asks the engine for the thread with the session's id as the key, so a retried request cannot make a second one. An engine that does not offer the bot's model, or that refuses the opening or the bot's permission mode, fails the attempt unsent, as an `unresolved` dispatch that was never sent, and the run tries again after its delay ([Retries](#routines)), in a side session of its own, under a fresh key: the engine keeps a refusal for its key. A refused permission mode is recorded on the run, with the mode the policy allows when the engine reports it, in the transaction that fails the attempt; its retry clears it, so a run that fails for good keeps why its last attempt failed, and its history says so. The same holds for a side session opened before a policy was installed whose turn the engine then refuses to run in its mode: the dispatcher fails that attempt `unresolved`, naming the refusal on the run, rather than as refused, which would fail the run at once.
2. Once the session's events are known and a turn slot is free, it writes the attempt's dispatch into that session as a start, in one transaction that finds the wake still pending, and the dispatcher sends it as it sends every start, unless the provider of the side session reached its usage limit meanwhile, which holds it until the reset. The envelope names the routine, its trigger and instruction, the run, by an id every attempt of it shares, the trigger data, and the routine's five newest settled runs with what each said, as many of them as fit the engine's input limit, saying so when some were left out. Runs from before the bot's latest fresh start count too: a fresh start clears the bot's working context and its catch-up notes, while each routine keeps what its recent runs said, so a run can tell what is new and not report the same thing again. An envelope that does not fit with none of them fails as refused, unsent.
3. A message the bot says in the side session is tagged with the attempt's wake, and adds a catch-up note ([Sessions](#sessions)).
4. Once the attempt's turn has ended, the run settles, and the side session is retired, then archived by the rotator.

A Stop of the bot reaches side sessions too: a wake still pending is cancelled, which settles its run, and a running attempt is stopped, which fails its run without another attempt. A provider change and a fresh start stop the bot's work first ([Rotation](#sessions)), so a run in progress ends this way, and its side session is retired once that Stop settles; the routine's next run opens on the bot's provider and settings then. Deleting the routine cancels a wake still pending, and one whose dispatch was written and not sent yet, held by a provider's usage limit say: that dispatch is released in the same transaction, so nothing of the routine starts after it is gone, and its side session is retired.

**Run history.** Each run has its start, its end, a status and, when it started more than a minute after its schedule's time, when it was due (`dueAt`):

| Status | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `running` | Its latest attempt has not ended, or the next one waits |
| `delivered` | The bot said something with `say` in one of its attempts |
| `silent` | Its attempt's turn completed without a message from the bot. It becomes `delivered` if the bot speaks later in the same side session |
| `failed` | Its last attempt failed, the engine refused its envelope, or a Stop of the bot or the routine's deletion ended it |
| `skipped` | It came due while the previous run was still going |

`skipped` keeps that one meaning: a run missed while the server was not running leaves no row, since a new status would change the protocol. It is counted on the routine instead (`missed`, above).

**Retries.** An attempt that fails before the bot says anything (the engine could not open its session or lost it, its turn failed, also before it started, or was interrupted by something other than a Stop) is tried again with a new wake, in a fresh side session: two minutes after the first attempt failed, four after the second, three attempts in all. One whose envelope the engine refused, or that could never fit its input limit, fails the run at once: the next would be refused too. A failed attempt's leftover background work ends when its retry is due ([Sessions](#sessions)). The run's history shows how many attempts started, none for a skipped run. A provider at its usage limit holds the next attempt until the reset, as it holds every automated wake. The last attempt's failure fails the run. Nothing about a failed attempt shows in the conversation; the run's history is where it shows. What the operator stopped is not tried again.

A run settles from its latest attempt, when the runner, the scheduler or the routine next looks. A run whose row cannot be read is logged and left running; the others settle. A silent run keeps no `note` yet: the engine adapter does not surface a turn's final words outside `say`, so there is nothing to keep. A routine keeps its 100 newest settled runs and the run going, counted again as each run settles; its run count counts the runs that started.

### Routines in steps

A routine may be a list of steps ([decision 0080](../decisions/0080-a-routines-steps-are-each-bots-own-turns-driven-by-j3-bots.md)): bot steps, each optionally followed by a **condition**, a **yes** of the operator, or a condition then a yes, before the next bot step; it starts and ends with a bot step, at most six of them. The routine keeps them structured (`routines.steps_json`), and its `instruction` is its first step's. A routine whose steps would be one bot step by its own bot is saved as a one-step routine, which runs exactly as above. Every routine saved before 0.12.0 is one: migration `0060_routine_steps` adds the columns and no row changes.

- **A run copies the steps** (`routine_runs.steps_json`) and writes one row per step (`routine_run_steps`): its state, result line, handoff, the question a yes asks, the answer, its deadline and its failure. The run's `step` is where it stands. Everything in the run follows that copy, so a change to the routine applies from its next run. Before the first step the run checks that every step's bot still exists, and fails naming the one that does not.
- **A bot step** is a `routine` wake of that step's bot, run by the routine runner as above, in a fresh side session of that bot. Its envelope carries the step's brief, what each earlier step reported, the operator's yes and note, and what its report must decide ([Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). The step ends with `finish_step` ([Bot tools](#bot-tools)), whose first call of an attempt is stored on the step; the run moves on only once the step's turn has ended. A step's bot may still `say`, which goes to its own conversation.
- **A condition** is judged by the bot of the step before, as `goOn`. When it does not hold, the condition's row is `stopped` with that step's result, every later step `skipped`, and the run settles `silent`.
- **A yes** opens with the question that step's report wrote, or is `not-needed` when the yes asks only if something holds and the report left the question out. Its deadline is fixed as it opens: the hours the step allows, at most the routine's next scheduled run. `routine.answer` closes it, in the transaction that finds it still waiting, and, when the answer names the yes's place (`step`, which every client of this release sends), still at that place: a yes starts the next bot step with the note in its envelope; a no ends the run `silent`. The scheduler applies a passed deadline (`timed-out`) on its pass, before it admits a new run, and on its first pass after a restart. While a yes waits, the routine's owner shows as waiting on the operator in the roster and the conversation's header, the run's card being the one it waits on, as for an `ask` it ended a turn on, and the run counts as going, so a run that comes due is `skipped`.
- **Retries.** A step's attempt is tried again by itself only when its turn never started, as a one-step attempt is. A turn that started and ended without a report, or failed, fails the run at that step, marked `partial`; a report with `blocked` fails it with the bot's words, also marked `partial`, since its turn ran. A report is kept once taken: a turn that fails after it still goes on from it. `routine.retry` resumes a failed run from that step while it is its routine's latest run and nothing else of the routine is going, keeping the earlier reports and the yes; it is refused for a run a Stop ended.
- **The run card.** A run posts one `routine-run` entry in its owner's conversation (`routine_runs.entry_id`), or an `event-routine-run` for a routine on a connector's event ([Event routines](#event-routines)), updated in the transaction that moves the run, so a client sees each step's state change on the conversation's stream. It notifies when the run waits for a yes or fails at a step other than by a Stop ([Notifications](#notifications)).
- **Stop.** A Stop of the owner, or `routine.stopRun`, ends its runs that are going in its own transaction: the step at work, or the yes waiting, is `failed` as `stopped`, the rest `skipped`, the run `failed` and `stopped`; the step's wake is cancelled or its unsent dispatch fenced, and a turn the step may have in the engine is ended by a Stop of that turn alone (`Stops.endTurn`), in whichever bot's side session it runs. A turn the engine took but had not shown is ended the same way by the routine runner once it shows. A Stop of a step's bot ends that step like the rest of its work, and the run with it, also after the step reported. Deleting the routine or its owner ends a run that is going first.
- **Settling.** A run with steps settles from its steps, not from its latest wake, into the statuses above: `delivered` when it ended without failing and one of its steps spoke, `silent` when it ended without failing or a message, every step run or a condition, a no or a deadline ending it, `failed` when a step was blocked, ended without a report, ran out of attempts or lost its bot, or a Stop or a deletion ended it. The steps travel with the run (`RoutineRun.steps`), so a client tells a quiet end from a no.
- **Background work.** A step's side session is retired once its turn has ended and nothing of it can still run; background work it left keeps it until that work ends, the next step or a newer run of the routine starts, or a Stop ends its run, which supersedes it, as a retry supersedes a failed one-step attempt.
- **Templates.** A routine with several steps is not written into a template: its steps name other bots.

### Webhooks

A webhook routine's address is `/hooks/<routine id>/<secret>` on the server's HTTP listener, given to the operator as the routine's `webhookUrl`, built when it is given: a repeated create or update names the listener as bound now. The secret is 32 random bytes, base64url-encoded, drawn when the routine gets its webhook trigger; an update that keeps the trigger keeps it. With `publicOrigin` set ([Configuration](#configuration)), the `webhookUrl` names that origin, for a server reached through a proxy or a tunnel. Without it, the `webhookUrl` names the first address `j3-bots pair` would offer, at the port the listener bound (the one the system chose, when the port set is `0`), written as a browser writes an origin, so `https` on port 443 names no port: with TLS, the first DNS name on the certificate; with `lan`, the first private address of a real interface (not loopback, a container or a virtual network), read when the URL is built; otherwise the address set. With none, it says what `pair` says: with TLS, `<a name the server's certificate covers>` in place of the host; with `lan`, `127.0.0.1`.

- **Authentication.** The secret in the path is the credential, checked before the body or a header the route acts on (`Idempotency-Key`, `Content-Length`) is read. The server compares SHA-256 digests of the given and the stored secret with Node's `timingSafeEqual`, also when there is no such routine, and answers a wrong secret, an unknown routine and a routine with another trigger alike: `404`, and it closes the connection after answering. The route never reads the body of such a call; bytes the caller already sent are discarded as the connection closes, and a caller that waits for `100 Continue` is never sent one, so it sends no body. A proxy in front of the server may wait for or buffer the body before it forwards the request. Refused calls are not limited ([Security](security.md#j3-bots-own-surface)). The server keeps the secret out of its logs: the request log records any path under `/hooks/` as `/hooks/…`, with the method and the status and nothing else, whether or not a webhook answers it, and no trace span is made of such a request. A call that fails answers `500`, and what went wrong is logged on its own line, without the request. An HMAC signature over the body is not checked: the routine contract has no field to require one, and a signature that is optional adds nothing.
- **The body** of a call that named its webhook is read up to 64 KiB (`413` past that) and must be UTF-8 text (`400` otherwise). It is stored with a `running` run as its trigger data. A `skipped` run keeps its `Idempotency-Key` but not the body.
- **Replay.** A request with an `Idempotency-Key` header (at most 200 characters) is recorded once: the same key again answers the run it recorded, with `200`, for as long as that run is kept in the history. Without the header, every request is a new call.
- **Rate.** A webhook call is refused while the routine has recorded ten runs in the last minute, skipped ones included: it answers `429` with `Retry-After`, the time until fewer than ten remain, and records nothing. A run the operator asks for is not limited, so it can take the count past ten.
- A paused routine answers `409` and records nothing.
- An accepted call answers `202` with the run's id and status, `running` or `skipped`.

### Event routines

New in 0.13.0 ([decision 0088](../decisions/0088-a-connectors-events-wake-a-bot-through-a-routine.md)). A routine whose trigger is a connector's event (`EventTrigger`: the connector, the event's name, the arguments its `inputSchema` takes, and the bot's own words for it, `summary`) runs when the connector's server says the event happened. The bot finds what a connector offers with `list_connector_events` and sets the trigger with `set_routine`'s `event` ([Prompts: tools](../prompts/architecture.md)); the operator never sees an address, a mode or a subscription. A trigger is checked as it is saved: the connector must be one the bot may use, connected, offering the extension and that event, and the arguments must give what the event's schema requires and nothing it leaves out (`eventArgumentsProblem`); each failure answers the bot in words that say what to do. A connector's events wake nobody by themselves: only a routine asking for one subscribes to it. A template carries no event routine.

**The listener.** The server holds every subscription, never a bot (`RoutineEvents`, `apps/server/src/routines`). Each event routine that is on has a row in `routine_event_listeners` (migration 0062), made when its trigger is saved and made again, from now, when the trigger listens for something else (another connector, event or arguments), when the routine is resumed, and when it was stopped and is given its trigger again; new words for the same event in `summary` keep it. Made again, it forgets the event ids it had taken, which were the old subscription's. Pausing the routine drops the events waiting for it; deleting it, or giving it another kind of trigger, drops the row. One fiber per row listens, through the routine's own bot's session of the connector, so the connector's grant and sign-in are the bot's:

1. It lists the connector's events (`events/list`) and finds the routine's; one no longer offered stops the routine (`event-gone`).
2. It picks how to listen, from the ways the event offers: by **webhook** when the server has a `publicOrigin` that is `https:`, else by **push** (`events/stream`, a request it holds open with a 30-second heartbeat, opened again when it ends), else by **poll** (`events/poll`, at most 50 events a poll, at the pace the server suggests in `nextPollMs`, never more than once a second, every 30 seconds when it suggests nothing). A way that fails, such as a webhook whose verification challenge does not come back, or a stream that never opens when the event can also be polled (a proxy that holds a response until it ends keeps it from arriving), is left out from then on and the next one tried; an event offering none of them stops the routine (`refused`).
3. A connector that is not connected, does not answer or fails, or whose server has no room for another subscription (`-32025`), leaves the listener `waiting`, trying again after 5 seconds, then twice as long each time up to 5 minutes. One whose event changed under it (`-32026`), or whose subscription its server no longer knows, starts again from step 1, and after three such starts in a row stops the routine (`event-gone`).

**Webhooks.** The listener draws a token of 32 random bytes, which names the address (`/hooks/events/<token>` on `publicOrigin`), and a `whsec_` secret of 32 more, both kept on the listener's row, and subscribes (`events/subscribe`) for an hour; it renews the subscription five minutes before the server's `refreshBefore`, or before the hour, or halfway through what is left of a shorter one the server granted, and unsubscribes when the routine stops listening. The route checks the signature (Standard Webhooks: HMAC-SHA256 of `<id>.<timestamp>.<body>`, compared in constant time, the timestamp within five minutes) before it reads anything else of the body, answers the verification challenge, and reads up to 256 KiB (`413` past that). An unknown or retired token answers `410`, which refuses that delivery for good; a bad signature `401`, an unreadable body `400`, a delivery taken `204`. A `terminated` delivery stops the routine when it names why (access revoked, arguments refused), and otherwise has the listener start again from step 1. The request log shows the path as `/hooks/…`, as for any webhook.

**Delivery.** Every event, whichever way it came, goes through one step, in one transaction: its id is recorded (`routine_event_seen`, the newest 1,000 kept), so a repeated event is taken once; it waits in `routine_event_pending`, its payload as text cut at 16,000 characters; the listener's cursor moves past it. A restart resumes from the cursor, asking the server for nothing older than six hours (`maxAgeMs`); a server that lost the cursor, or answered `truncated`, marks the next run as having missed events, as does a 101st event waiting. A routine with events waiting starts one run with up to 20 of them, oldest first, and no more than fit 48,000 characters of payload together (always at least the first), once its previous run has ended and within the ten runs a minute a webhook routine has ([Webhooks](#webhooks)); the rest wait for the run after. The run's envelope carries each event, its name and time and its payload as data, and says when some may have been missed ([Prompt architecture](../prompts/architecture.md#wake-envelopes)). A webhook's payload is not scrubbed of the connector's secrets, as a tool's answer is: it is the server's own post, not an answer to a request J3 Bots signed, so it holds none of them.

**Stopping.** A listener its server ends (`terminated`, or a refusal such as `-32024` access revoked) or that can no longer use its connector (removed, or no longer granted to the bot: checked as soon as the operator changes it, and again before each event is taken, so a stream or a webhook made before wakes nobody) is stopped, with a reason: `access-revoked`, `event-gone`, `connector-gone` or `refused`. The routine keeps its trigger and stays on; it shows `stopped` with the reason (`Routine.listening`), its conversation records an `event-routine-change` entry, and the bot gets a catch-up note at its next turn, which tells it to say so to the operator and offer to set the event again. Resuming the routine, or giving it its trigger again, listens anew. MCP's `notifications/events/list_changed` reaches only a client listening on `subscriptions/listen`, which J3 Bots' stateless sessions do not hold; a removed event is noticed as the server ends the subscription, or as the listener lists the events each time it starts again.

**Protocol.** Within protocol 11. A routine with an event trigger reaches only a client that asks for it (`routines.subscribe`'s `withEvents`); a client of an earlier release, whose `RoutineTrigger` knows three triggers, never sees one and still loads every other routine. Its changes are `event-routine-change` entries, and the card of a run of one with several steps an `event-routine-run`, which such a client passes over as entries of a kind it does not know; a client that knows `routine-run` reads its trigger as one of the three. A card keeps the kind it was posted with while its run lasts. `Routine.listening` and `connectorName` are optional fields. `routine.create` and `routine.update` take the three triggers only: an event trigger is the bot's to set.

## Templates

A template is a bot as a file to share, what makes it do its job and nothing it learned ([decision 0023](../decisions/0023-a-bot-template-is-a-file.md)); its schema is `BotTemplate` in `packages/contracts`.

**`bot.template`** builds one from named fields, never from a copy of anything larger: the profile, the model and the permission mode from the bot's row; each routine's name, instruction and trigger, a schedule as its cron alone and a webhook as the fact that it is one, with no one-time routine; and the skills from the bot's workspace as it was first provisioned. A skill is a folder of `skills/` whose name is a skill's name (one lowercase segment), that is a real folder holding a `SKILL.md` that is a regular file within the limit; a link is never followed, and a folder that breaks any of this is passed over. Folders are taken in name order, and skills and routines past the limits (16 each) stay out. Memory, the conversation, files, the routines' time zones and webhook addresses never reach it; the job, the skills and the routines' instructions go as written. The template is then measured as its file is written (`templateFileText` in `packages/contracts`, the same text the clients save), and one over 1 MiB of UTF-8 is refused with `TemplateTooLarge`, which names its largest part: the limits count characters, so text of several bytes a character can fit them and still make a file no client would read.

**`bot.createFromTemplate`** makes a bot from one, as `bot.create` does, in the same transaction and under the same command receipt, then, before the transaction commits:

- each routine, paused, with no next run, authored by the operator, who approved it in the preview; a schedule takes the operator's time zone, and one that cannot run refuses the whole command with `Invalid("schedule")`;
- each skill's `SKILL.md` under the new workspace's `skills/<name>/`, written through the workspace layout, which refuses a location that would expose a private one (`Conflict("private-location")`).

A creation that fails at any step, before or after the skills were written, rolls the transaction back and removes the workspace it started, so no bot is left half made. That holds for `bot.create` too, which writes nothing there before it commits.

The model is the command's, else the template's when the engine offers it, else the server's default. The permission mode is the command's, which a client sends only when the operator picked one, else the less permissive of the template's and the default for the model as it stands then (supervised for a mode this build does not know). The avatar is the template's when the server has its tokens, else one no other bot wears, as for any new bot. The bot is never made primary, and its opening wake is any new bot's: its first turn works from its job as written. A field the template's schema does not name is dropped as the payload is decoded, so it never reaches the bot; a client refuses such a file before this. The command is not decoded strictly, since the RPC's parse options also govern how a client decodes the reply, which would make an older client refuse a newer server's added fields of `Bot`.

## Connectors

The contract is in `packages/contracts` (`connector.ts`, `ConnectorRpcs`). The server serves all of it: a connector added by address, from the gallery or by command. A connector is a service a bot uses through J3 Bots: an MCP server reached over Streamable HTTP, or one the server starts by command. The server is its MCP client and its OAuth client, and holds every secret it needs ([decision 0026](../decisions/0026-a-bot-uses-connected-services-through-j3-bots.md)). Bots call its tools through the `j3` bridge, under their permission mode ([decision 0027](../decisions/0027-connector-tools-reach-a-bot-through-the-j3-bridge.md)).

**What the server keeps.** For each connector, in the `connectors` table: its id (`conn_…`, chosen by the client that adds it, so adding again after a lost reply finds it), its name and glyph, a slug that names its tools, the gallery entry it came from, how it is reached as clients are shown it, how it signs in, the fields its form took without their values, whether the operator confirmed an address in their own network, its grant (`connector_bots`, or every bot), when its form was last given, the tools its server listed last with what it said of itself, and who last changed it and when (`lastChange`). The secrets are in [Secrets](#secrets), a record per connector ([decision 0028](../decisions/0028-one-secret-store-for-logins-and-connector-secrets.md)): its OAuth client, tokens, header values and environment, and the address the operator typed or the command's arguments, either of which can carry a key in a path, a query or a flag. Nothing in J3 Bots' database holds a value of them. A client is shown how a connector is reached only as the address's origin or the command's program (`displayTransport`), in what `connector.add` answers as on `connectors.subscribe`; a secret a command needs belongs in its environment, which its form masks. What the server saw of a connector since it started (its last listing, a failure, a refused key) is kept in memory, not in the table. A removed connector's row goes at once; `connector_removals` keeps, in the same transaction, what is left to do outside the database (revoking an OAuth sign-in, deleting its secret) until it is done.

**The gallery.** `connectors.list` gives J3 Bots' curated list, in the client's language; `connectors.search` adds the MCP Registry's matches after it, and says when the registry did not answer; `connectors.get` gives one entry's page and the form Connect shows. The server answers them from `apps/server/src/connectors/sources`, which `search_mcp_servers` and `add_mcp_server` read too. Since 0.10.0 no client shows the gallery: the operator asks a bot, and `connectors.list` and `connectors.search` stay only for clients on protocol 11 that still do ([decision 0068](../decisions/0068-settings-lists-what-the-bots-added.md)). `connectors.get` is still read, by the card that asks the operator for their own app's client ([The operator's yes](#the-operators-yes)).

**The curated list** is a file shipped with the server (`curated.json`), reviewed for each release. Its text is J3 Bots' own, in every language the interface has; a service's name is a trademark it only names. Each entry has one of J3 Bots' own glyphs, line drawings of what the service does that the clients ship (`CONNECTOR_GLYPHS` in `packages/design-tokens`), never a vendor's logo or an image fetched at run time. Each also carries its address, so the gallery works when the registry does not answer, and its address is never taken from the registry: a change there cannot move a curated connector. An entry connects one of three ways:

- **One tap** (`sign-in`): Notion, Linear, Atlassian, Stripe, Figma, Vercel and Supabase let any client register itself, so the operator only signs in.
- **A token in a header** (`token`): GitHub, with a fine-grained personal access token, and Linear, with an API key instead of a sign-in. The entry links to the page that makes the token and says which permissions to give it.
- **Bring your own app** (`own-app`): Google (Gmail, Calendar, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Slides, Chat and Contacts) and Slack accept only an OAuth client the operator registers. The entry's setup steps say how; every Google connector uses the same client. Google's servers are a Developer Preview that answers only accounts in Google's Workspace Developer Preview Program, which the entry says before anything else (**Read**, Google's documentation, 2026-11-23). Slack lets only its directory's apps and a workspace's own internal apps use its server, so the operator creates one in a workspace they administer (**Read**, Slack's documentation).

The file is decoded at start like any other input, and refused, with the server, when an entry lacks a language, an address is not `https:`, a glyph is not one J3 Bots draws, a key is listed twice, or an entry cannot be connected.

**The MCP Registry** (`registry.modelcontextprotocol.io`, API `v0.1`) is searched through the server; clients never reach it, and nothing mirrors it. It holds tens of thousands of entries, most mass-published, and its search matches names only (**Verified**, 2026-11-23). So the server budgets each search:

- at most three pages of 100, each answer read up to 1 MiB, each request given 5 seconds and the whole search 8; when a later page fails or the time is spent, what was read is used;
- two requests to the registry at once, across every search;
- results ranked and cut to 40 per query, then cached for an hour (32 queries), and an entry read by name for a day (128 entries). A search the registry did not answer is remembered for a minute, so each keystroke does not wait on it again. The caches are memory only: they are rebuilt after a restart and never reach a snapshot.

Within those, entries the registry marks deleted or deprecated are dropped, and so are the registry's copies of curated entries, by name or by address. The ranking puts first the entries whose namespace's domain owns their remote's host (`com.notion/mcp` at `mcp.notion.com`): the registry verifies a namespace when it is published, so such an entry was published by whoever runs the service. It shows as "from notion.com", never as verified: the registry reviews nothing else. Then come names closest to the query. Mass publishers and wrappers of other companies' services, which fill most searches, fall behind. Without `all`, only entries J3 Bots can connect are listed: a server reached only over the legacy SSE transport, or only by installing a package, shows as `unsupported`.

**The connect form** comes from the remote's `server.json`: its first Streamable HTTP remote at an `https:` address. Each URL variable becomes a plain field, masked when the publisher marks it secret; each header with no fixed value becomes a field, masked when the header is secret; a header whose fixed value names variables (`Bearer {api_key}`) asks for each of them, masked when the header or the variable is secret. A variable the publisher named but did not declare is still asked for, with the header's description and secrecy: sending its braces as written, which the schema allows, would make a request that cannot work. A field with a default is optional and shows the default as its placeholder; one with choices takes one of them. The values the operator gives are redacted from the moment they are decoded. Filling the form refuses a value with a control character, which could start another header, and a field the form does not have; a URL variable is percent-encoded, so it cannot leave the part of the address it fills; and a secret part of the address stays out of the address shown. A remote is not offered when it asks to set a header the transport owns (`Host`, `Content-Length`, `Mcp-Session-Id` and the like), declares a malformed header, or asks for more than 16 fields. Publisher text is shown as plain text: control and direction-changing characters taken out, cut to the contract's lengths.

**Not a source: `cursor/community-plugins`** (the directory behind cursor.directory). Its repository has no licence, so all rights are reserved, and its data lives in a hosted database outside it. J3 Bots neither imports nor fetches it, at build time or at run time.

**Adding.** Since 0.10.0 the clients add no connector: a bot adds one ([Servers](#what-the-bots-manage)), and `connector.add` stays for clients on protocol 11 ([decision 0068](../decisions/0068-settings-lists-what-the-bots-added.md)). It takes a gallery entry with its form's values, an address with headers and an optional OAuth client, or a command. Without a grant it goes to every bot ([decision 0047](../decisions/0047-j3-bots-is-a-complete-mcp-host-at-the-current-specification.md)). An address in the operator's own network is refused with `Conflict("private-network")` until the client sends it again saying the operator confirmed it. Without an OAuth client, the server lists the address's tools first, signed with the headers given. A connector its server refuses becomes one that signs in with OAuth when it was given no headers, and one whose form needs new values when it was; one that does not answer is added as `failing`, and its tools are listed once it answers. With an OAuth client, the sign-in follows. A gallery entry is added from its form: the server builds the form again from the entry, in the language the client names, which the connector keeps its name and its fields' labels in, fills it with the values given, and adds the address and headers that gives as it adds one typed, checked by the same rules, never as one in the operator's own network. An entry whose form cannot be filled is `Invalid("connector-fields")`. How an entry signs in is its own, and a client cannot change it: an `own-app` entry needs the operator's client and is the only one that takes one; a `token` entry signs in with its form; a `sign-in` entry signs in with OAuth unless its server answers without one. An entry that would sign in with OAuth while its form put a key in its address is `Invalid("connector-unsupported")`, since a sign-in sends that address to the authorization server and through the browser. The connector shows the address's origin only when it is the entry's, so no value the form put in the host is shown. Its slug is chosen in the transaction that stores it, so two connectors added at once with one name get two. A connector that needs a secret can be added only while the secrets are `ready`; if it cannot be stored, its secret is deleted again.

**New values.** `connector.update` replaces the values a connector's form took. For a gallery entry, the server builds its form again, from the curated list or from the registry's entry (`Conflict("registry-unavailable")` while the registry does not answer and has no copy cached), and renders again each header a value is given for, from every value that header takes, an optional header left out when the connector was added included. A header made of several fields (`{account}:{key}`) takes the fields not given as they were kept: the connector's secret keeps every value its form took, secret or not, so giving one field alone renders the header whole (one added by an earlier release kept only its secret fields, so its plain ones are given again once), and a field given empty clears its kept value, which leaves a required field missing. A header no value names keeps what it had, and a value for a field of the address is `Invalid("connector-fields")`, since another address is another connector. For a connector added by address, a value names its header. The values go to the connector's secret only, a secret field's own value with them.

**Connectors started by command.** A bot adds one; the operator changes one's command line from the web or desktop app, and the Android app only shows them, a convention of the apps, since every paired device holds the operator's authority. It is an MCP server the server starts on its own computer, as its own user, and speaks to over its standard input and output (`packages/connectors`, `command/`):

- **Consent.** A command a bot adds runs under the bot's permission mode, with no confirmation of J3 Bots' own ([decision 0049](../decisions/0049-the-bots-manage-mcp-servers-and-skills.md)). A command line the operator types is confirmed: before the server runs it, the client shows the operator the whole command line, the program and every argument, each variable that decides which code runs with its value, above the program (`codeVariable` in `packages/contracts`: `NODE_OPTIONS`, `NODE_PATH`, `PYTHONPATH`, `PYTHONHOME`, `LD_PRELOAD`, `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`, `DYLD_*`, `BASH_ENV`, `ENV`, `PERL5OPT`, `RUBYOPT`, `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS`, `CLASSPATH`, `DOTNET_STARTUP_HOOKS`, `CORECLR_PROFILER*`, the certificate variables and the like, in any case), and the name of each other environment variable, without its value, and sends it only once they confirm. Such a variable's value must be on the line itself: one holding a reference to a secret, whose value can change with no new confirmation, is refused (`Invalid("connector-fields")`, `codeFromSecret`), and a command saved with one before is not started, its status saying so (`command-refused`), until a new command line with the value itself is confirmed. A new command line is sent whole with `connector.update` (`command`): the server stops the running process, forgets what it saw of the old one, and starts the new one to list its tools, whose tools reach bots as for any connector. The form takes new values only for the variables the command already names (`values`); adding, removing or renaming one is a new command line, and so is a new value for a variable that decides which code runs, which the server refuses in `values` (`Invalid("connector-fields")`). Values sent with a new command line replace its variables as written, an empty one included, as the command line itself could have written them. A changed value stops the process too, and starts it again with the new one. One change of a connector runs at a time, so two clients changing it at once cannot lose either's values.
- **Exact versions.** A command that fetches a package as it starts, through `npx`, `npm exec`, `uvx` or `uv tool run`, must name every package it fetches at an exact version, such as `@scope/server@1.2.3` or `server==1.2.3`, so the code that runs is the version the operator saw. A version is exact as semver writes it, since npm takes anything else, such as `1.2.3-..`, for a tag. A tag such as `latest`, a range, no version, a URL or a path is refused as `Invalid("command-unpinned")`, when it is added and when it changes, and so is an option of the launcher's the server does not know, since it could name another package, one that names a configuration file (npm's `--userconfig`, uv's `--config-file`), which would be read in place of the server's, and `npx -c`, which runs a shell line. `npm exec` reads its own options after the package too, so through it the package must follow `--`. npm takes any prefix only one of its commands has for that command, so a word that could be `exec`, `init` or `create` (which run a package too) is refused, as is an option before npm's command, which may take the next word as its value; uv's own options are read wherever they come before its command. The program is named by the file its path leads to, `.` and `..` read and Windows' trailing dots and spaces dropped, and checked again on the file found at each start, where a refusal leaves the connector `failing` (`command-refused`). A launcher's settings in the environment can choose another package or registry, so a variable of the operator's named `npm_config_*` for npm, or `UV_*` for uv, is refused too. Configuration files can too, so the server starts such a launcher (`packageLauncher`) with none of the user's or of a project's: npm with `npm_config_userconfig` set to the system's empty device (`/dev/null`, or `\\.\nul` on Windows) and in `<J3_HOME>/state/npm-project`, a folder of the server's holding only an empty `package.json`, which it writes again before each start, so npm takes it for the project and reads its `.npmrc` there; uv with `UV_NO_CONFIG=1` ([Security](security.md#connectors)). So a command through `npx` or `npm exec` runs in that folder, and reads a relative path in its arguments from there, not from the home folder. Any other program runs as given. The check is `pinRefusal` in `packages/contracts`, with `reservedVariable` for the names below, so a client's form says what the server would refuse before it sends a command line; the server checks again.
- **Its environment.** Only the server's `PATH`, `HOME`, `LANG`, `LC_ALL`, `LC_CTYPE` and `TMPDIR` reach it on Linux and macOS, and on Windows what Windows' own programs need to start (`PATH`, `PATHEXT`, `SystemRoot`, `ComSpec`, the profile and program folders, `TEMP` and `TMP`, and the like), then the operator's variables, which replace any of the same name; nothing else of the server's environment, such as a provider's key or J3 Bots' settings. Windows reads names without case, so a command may not name a variable twice in any case (`Invalid("connector-fields")`). Nor may it name one the server reads to find and start the program, in any case: `PATH`, `PATHEXT`, `SystemRoot`, `windir`, `ComSpec` and `PSModulePath`, since a value changes without a new confirmation and these would choose which program runs. A command has at most 32 variables, each one of the connector's fields. Node gives its children a `NODE_V8_COVERAGE` of its own unless told otherwise, so the server leaves it out of the process's environment unless the operator gave it. The command line, program and arguments together, and the variables' values are kept in the connector's secret, redacted from the request that carries them to the process's start, and read from it at each start: the program the connector's row shows is never what runs, and a new command line replaces the old one in one write, so no start runs half of each.
- **How it starts.** With no shell. A program named without a folder is looked up on the command's own `PATH` (with `PATHEXT` on Windows), skipping a folder of it that is not absolute; a path is taken from the home folder of the server's user, where the command runs (an npm launcher runs in its own folder, above). One that is not found is `failing` (`command-not-found`). While the server's own `NODE_DEBUG` names `child_process`, Node writes what it starts a child with, values included, to the server's standard error, so no command starts, and the connector is `failing` (`command-exited`). On Windows, a batch file such as the `npx.cmd` npm installs runs through the command interpreter, which reads its own syntax in the arguments however they are quoted: one with a quote, `%`, `!`, `^`, `&`, `|`, `<`, `>`, a parenthesis or a control character is not started, and the connector is `failing` (`command-refused`).
- **Its life.** A process starts when the connector is first used, by a listing or a bot's call, never ahead: after a restart of the server, a connector whose tools it listed before waits for a bot to call one, offering the tools it listed last. One process serves every bot granted the connector; the grant is checked on each call before it reaches the process. It stops after ten minutes unused, when the secrets can no longer be read, when its command or values change, when the connector is removed, and with the server. Each of these but the idle one stops it at once, even in the middle of a call, which then fails: a process started with a value or command line the operator replaced does not run on. A start still waiting out its pause, or under way, when a stop comes is dropped and runs nothing. A change is written to the connector's secret and the old process stopped as one step that a client's disconnect does not interrupt. One that ends by itself, or fails to start, is `failing` (`command-exited`) and started again by itself after a pause of a second, then twice as long each time it ends or fails again, up to a minute, starting over after a run of a minute; after five in a row it is left stopped until a value or the command line changes, or the server restarts. Every start that fails counts, however long it took; only a process that opened its session and then ran a minute starts the count over. A bot that calls it during a pause waits for it. At most one process runs per gibibyte of the computer's memory, from 2 to 12, so a Raspberry Pi runs only a few: a start past it stops the one unused the longest, and when every one is in use, the call is answered as the server being busy.
- **How it stops.** Its input is closed, as MCP asks of a client, and it has two seconds to end. On Linux and macOS it leads a process group of its own, which then gets `SIGTERM` and, three seconds later, `SIGKILL`, so a process it started ends with it. On Windows, which has no process groups, it runs in a job object that ends every process in it once the launcher holding the job ends (`inJob` in `packages/processes`, as the engine's processes do, with its standard streams relayed); ending the launcher ends them all.
- **What it says.** A message is a line of JSON-RPC; a line that is not one, as a server that logs to its output writes, is skipped, and a message longer than 10 MiB ends the session, as does a process that would leave more than 10 MiB of what was sent it unread. Either way the process is stopped, whether or not a call was waiting, and started again after its pause. What it answers is scrubbed of the values of the variables the operator gave it, as given and as a JSON string holds them, as a remote server's answers are of its credentials; its arguments are not, since they are the command's own words, such as a folder it serves. Its standard error goes nowhere: a server may print what it was given there, so none of it is read, kept or logged.
- **Logs.** The connector's id when its process starts, stops or ends by itself. Never the command line, an argument or a variable.

**Removing.** `connector.remove` takes the connector out at once, whether or not the secrets can be read: bots lose its tools and its sessions close. Then its OAuth sign-in is revoked and its secret deleted, both of which need the secrets: while they cannot be read, as while the key file is missing, these are done once they can, from the removal recorded with the row's deletion.

**Signing in.** `connector.beginSignIn` starts a sign-in bound to the device that asked, for ten minutes, and answers the address that device opens in the system browser. The browser returns to the server's own address when it is `https:` or the server runs on the device's computer (`server`), to a listener of the desktop or Android app on loopback, which relays the address it caught (`relay`), or, from the web client, to a page nobody answers, whose address the operator pastes (`paste`). Each returns under `/oauth/callback`, at a path of the authorization server's own: its issuer's host, then its path exactly as written, trailing slash included, as `/oauth/callback/accounts.google.com`. The relayed or pasted address comes back with `connector.completeSignIn`, which only that device may send; the browser's return to the server's own callback finishes only a sign-in begun as `server`. The `state` travels in the address the device opens and comes back with the code; the PKCE verifier never leaves the server, so a code caught by anyone else is of no use.

**Finding where to sign in.** The server follows the MCP authorization specification, revision 2026-07-28, whose discovery is 2025-11-25's. It reads the connector's full address from its secret, since it can carry a key. It sends the connector's server an `initialize` without a token and reads the `Bearer` challenge of its `401`. The protected resource metadata (RFC 9728) is where the challenge's `resource_metadata` says, else at the path-inserted well-known address, then at the root one. Its `resource` must cover the connector's address (the same origin, and a path that is the address's or one above it, segment by segment), and its first authorization server is the one used. It is refused, as `sign-in-unsupported`, when it holds a secret value of the connector's form, as given or URL-encoded, since it goes in the browser's address. That server's metadata is looked for at the RFC 8414 address, then OpenID Connect's inserted and appended ones, in the specification's order. For both documents, an address that answers `4xx`, something that is not a metadata document (a page a catch-all route serves), a `5xx` or nothing is passed over for the next; when none has the document, discovery failed as unreachable if an address did, else for want of metadata. The document's `issuer` must be the one asked for, character for character, and every endpoint `https:`; a person's sign-in also needs an authorization endpoint and `S256` among its PKCE methods. A connector that fails any of this is `Conflict("sign-in-unsupported")`, and one whose server did not answer is `Conflict("sign-in-unreachable")`. The scopes asked for are the challenge's `scope`, else the resource's `scopes_supported`. Metadata is kept an hour.

**The OAuth client.** In the specification's order: a client the operator gave, then J3 Bots' client metadata document, then a registration. The **document** (OAuth Client ID Metadata Document, draft 00) is J3 Bots' own, served by the server at its `publicOrigin` under `/oauth/client/<issuer host and path>`, one per authorization server: a public client named J3 Bots, for the code grant with refresh, returning to the server's callback for that issuer and to the same path on loopback. Its address is the client id. A sign-in uses it when the authorization server says it takes such ids (`client_id_metadata_document_supported`) and the server has a `publicOrigin` whose document the internet can read. The server reads it first through the client sign-ins use, which reaches public addresses only, and checks that it names itself and the sign-in's redirect. A provider that cannot read a client id shows its error page and never sends the browser back, so an origin only the operator's network reaches, such as a private network's own `https` name, registers instead, and the log says so. That a provider reads the document as the draft says is **Assumed**: the tests use a fake authorization server. Otherwise J3 Bots registers itself (dynamic client registration, RFC 7591, deprecated by 2026-07-28 and kept for servers without documents) for the address the sign-in returns to, as a native app for loopback and a web app for the server's own `https` address (`application_type`), as a public client when the server allows that, else with a client secret it keeps in the connector's secret; a registration the server refuses is `Conflict("registration-refused")`. A client registered earlier is used again when the same issuer has the sign-in's address registered. A relayed sign-in returns to a new loopback port each time, so a loopback address registered with another port counts as the same: RFC 8252 §7.3 asks an authorization server to accept any port for one (**Assumed** that each service does: one that does not shows its error in the browser, and the sign-in never comes back). The new client is kept only once its sign-in finishes, so a sign-in abandoned halfway leaves nothing saved, and the next one registers again: a client is used again only once a sign-in with it has finished. For a service that registers nobody, such as Google or Slack, the operator registers an app of their own and gives its client id and secret when adding the connector, and its issuer when the gallery entry does not name it. The entry's page (`connectors.get`) gives the addresses to register, as the server would send them: back to the server itself, from `publicOrigin` or the loopback address it listens on, and to a device's loopback ([Google](../operations/connector-google.md), [Slack](../operations/connector-slack.md), drafts). The app is used only when discovery finds that issuer, so a connector that names another authorization server never gets its secret; it authenticates with `client_secret_basic` when the server offers it, else `client_secret_post`. That client is never replaced, a sign-in started with another ends when it is given, and the operator registers at the service the addresses their sign-ins return to.
