A curated `own-app` entry also names the scopes its sign-in asks for and any parameters the service needs to give a refresh token (Google's `access_type=offline` and `prompt=consent`); the list is refused at start if one of them is a parameter J3 Bots sets itself.

**The sign-in.** The address the device opens carries `response_type=code`, the client, the exact redirect, the PKCE challenge (S256 of a 32-byte random verifier), a 16-byte random `state`, the `resource` (RFC 8707) and the scopes. The returned address must be the redirect itself, query and fragment aside, and name the sign-in's `state`; when the authorization server sends `iss` (RFC 9207), or says it will, it must be its issuer. Since no two authorization servers share a redirect, an answer one of them sent cannot finish a sign-in at another, `iss` or not (mix-up, RFC 9700 §4.4). A sign-in ends when it is used, after ten minutes, when another starts for the same connector, and with the server. A sign-in, another client or a sign-out for the connector that comes while a sign-in is prepared (discovery, registration) ends it before its address is given, and one that comes while a code is being exchanged wins: the tokens that exchange gets are dropped. A connector removed meanwhile is never written back. Its code is exchanged with the verifier, the redirect and the `resource` again, authenticated as the client's registration says (none, `client_secret_post` or `client_secret_basic`).

**Tokens.** Every request to an authorization server, and the discovery request to the connector, has 15 seconds from connecting to the last byte of its answer, reads 64 KiB at most, and records no trace span, since the connector's address can hold a key. Only `Bearer` tokens are accepted. They are kept in the connector's secret with when they expire, the scopes granted, the resource and when the operator signed in, which a refresh keeps; the connector's page shows that date. OAuth does not say which account at the service signed in, so none is shown. A worker per connector refreshes the access token five minutes before it expires, or halfway through a shorter life. A refresh that fails for a reason that may pass (the service down, a malformed answer) is tried again after 30 seconds, then twice as long each time up to 15 minutes; once the token has expired with the refresh still failing, the connector is `failing`. A refresh token the service rotates replaces the old one, and the request and the save are one step that nothing interrupts. One refresh at a time runs per connector, and a call that finds the token within a minute of expiring waits for it. A token given without a refresh token is used until it expires, then the connector needs a sign-in; an `expires_in` of `0` is a token that has already expired. A refresh answered with such a token keeps the refresh token it may have rotated and counts as failed: it is tried again after the wait, and no call is signed with it. When the connector's server refuses a token, the refusal says which token the request carried: one a sign-in or a refresh has replaced since is left alone, and the request goes again with the current one. A refused refresh (`invalid_grant`) signs the connector out: it `needs-sign-in` (`expired`). A refused client (`invalid_client`) also drops a client J3 Bots registered, so the next sign-in registers again; the operator's own client stays. When the connector's server refuses a token with `insufficient_scope`, no refresh is tried, since a refresh cannot widen a grant: the call needs a new sign-in. A sign-in asks for the scopes the connector's server names, those already granted, and each scope a call was refused for that the grant still lacks, so no call loses its scope to another's. While the secrets cannot be read no worker runs; they start once the secrets can be read.

**Signing out.** Removing a connector revokes its refresh token, then its access token, each asked for whatever became of the other, where the authorization server offers revocation (RFC 7009), then deletes them, with a client J3 Bots registered. A revocation that fails is logged and not tried again: the tokens are gone from J3 Bots either way.

**Signing in as a service.** Some servers sign in no person ([decision 0090](../decisions/0090-a-connector-signs-in-as-a-service-with-credentials-the-operator-gives.md)): they take a client the operator registered at the service, with the client credentials grant (RFC 6749 §4.4), as MCP's OAuth client credentials extension (`io.modelcontextprotocol/oauth-client-credentials`) describes, which every session declares. The code is `apps/server/src/connectors/oauth/clientCredentials.ts`.

- **Giving them.** A bot gives them to a connector added by address, with `clientCredentials` on `add_mcp_server` or `update_mcp_server`: the client ID, and the client secret or the client's private key, never both, each a `${secret:<name>}` reference to what the operator saved from one `ask_for_secret` card of kind `values`; optionally the issuer the service names and the scopes. The secret or the key must be a whole reference, never typed. They are checked as any secret a connector's settings name ([Secrets in a connector's settings](#secrets-in-a-connectors-settings)) and kept in the connector's secret, references unresolved. A connector started by command, one with the operator's own OAuth client, or one from the gallery, whose address may carry a key its form took, takes none (`Invalid("connector-unsupported")`). They are checked, and their authorization server found, before anything else an update gives is written, so a refusal leaves the connector as it was.
- **The issuer, pinned.** When they are given, the server finds the authorization server as a sign-in does ([Finding where to sign in](#connectors)). It must offer the client credentials grant, when its metadata lists grants at all (`grant_types_supported`), and a way for the credential to prove itself: `client_secret_basic` or `client_secret_post` for a secret, `private_key_jwt` for a key. An issuer the bot named must be that one. Anything else is `Conflict("sign-in-unsupported")`, and nothing is kept. The issuer found is kept with the credentials and they go to no other: changing them keeps it, and when the connector's server names another later, calls fail `failing` (`issuer-changed`), no request reaches the new one, and the bot is told the operator can remove the connector and add it again.
- **The token.** Asked at the token endpoint with `grant_type=client_credentials`, the `resource` (RFC 8707), and the scopes given, else the challenge's, else the resource's. A secret goes in a Basic header where the server takes one, as every server must (RFC 6749 §2.3.1), else in the body. A key signs an assertion (RFC 7523 §3) naming the client ID as issuer and subject and the issuer alone as audience, with `iat`, an `exp` five minutes later and a random `jti`, sent as `client_assertion` with the JWT bearer type. The key may be PEM (PKCS #8, PKCS #1 or SEC 1), whole or pasted on one line, or a JWK; it signs with the first algorithm it takes that the server lists, else its first: RS256 or PS256 for RSA of 2,048 bits or more, ES256, ES384 or ES512 by its curve, EdDSA for Ed25519. The token is kept in memory only, never in the secret, and a call that finds it within a minute of expiry gets a new one; the grant gives no refresh token, so that is the refresh. One request for a token runs at a time per connector.
- **Refused.** A token endpoint that answers `invalid_client`, `unauthorized_client` or `invalid_grant`, or a key nothing can be signed with, makes the connector `needs-secret` (`refused`), naming the credential's secret, and the bot's call is told, in fixed words (`credentials-refused`), to ask the operator for new ones. Nothing more is asked of the service with those values, which a digest kept in memory tells apart, until they change. When the connector's server refuses a token, it is dropped and the next call gets another; a token got just now and refused again counts as refused credentials too. A refusal for a scope the token lacks (`403` with `insufficient_scope`) is not one of the credentials, on the first try or the second: that call fails at once, telling the bot in fixed words (`scope-refused`) that the client needs that permission at the service, other calls go on, and the token is dropped, so the next call's new one carries the permission once the operator grants it.
- **No sign-in card.** A server whose authorization server lists the client credentials grant and not the authorization code one signs in services only: `add_mcp_server` shows no `connector-auth` card for it, and its answer tells the bot to ask for the credentials (`MANAGEMENT_NOTES.serviceCredentialsNeeded`). Credentials given to a connector that signed in with OAuth sign it out, revoking its tokens, and close its sign-in cards as `signed-in`.

**The client.** `packages/connectors` is the server's MCP client, on the official TypeScript SDK's client package (`@modelcontextprotocol/client`, pinned at 2.4.0, Apache-2.0, which implements 2026-07-28), over Streamable HTTP and a process's standard streams, and brings few dependencies. It speaks the protocol's 2026-07-28 revision, stateless, and falls back to an older server:

- **Over HTTP** it asks `server/discover` first. A server that answers with 2026-07-28 is spoken to without a session, each request carrying its own envelope and the `Mcp-Method` and `Mcp-Name` headers. A JSON-RPC error (as a 2025-era server answers an unknown method), `-32022` naming only older versions, or any `4xx` but `401` and `403` makes it an older server, and `initialize` follows on the same address, offering every version the SDK knows down to 2024-11-05. A `401` or `403` is the sign-in's, and a `5xx`, a network error or silence is an outage, never an older server.
- **Over a process's streams** the probe is the same. A server silent at it for 5 seconds is an older one, and `initialize` follows; one that ends at the first request it does not know is started again and spoken to with `initialize` alone.
- **What it declares:** elicitation, in forms and by URL, the Tasks extension (`io.modelcontextprotocol/tasks`) and the Events extension (`io.modelcontextprotocol/events`) on 2026-07-28, and the OAuth client credentials extension (`io.modelcontextprotocol/oauth-client-credentials`), since it can sign in as a service. Never sampling or roots: a bot's model is the bot's, and a connector's server gets none of it.
- **Lists** of tools, resources, resource templates and prompts are read whole, page by page, and kept as long as the server's `ttlMs` says (2026-07-28); a server's word that one changed (`notifications/*/list_changed`, on 2026-07-28 over `subscriptions/listen`, before it over the session's stream) drops it and reads it again. Every session of a connector shares one cache: they are signed with the same credentials, so a `private` answer stays with the connector. J3 Bots supplies the transport's `fetch`, so every request goes through its own connection code (below), never the SDK's.

- **Sessions.** A bot's calls to a connector go through that bot's own session, since a session can hold state at its server, such as a page or a cursor. A connector started by command has one session, its process's, for every bot ([above](#connectors)). The server keeps one more per connector to list its tools. A session opens on the first request that needs it and closes after ten minutes unused. At most 32 are open at once, idle ones included (`MAX_SESSIONS`): one that would be the 33rd closes the idle session unused the longest and opens in its place, and only when every one is in the middle of a request is the call answered as the server being busy, with nothing recorded against the connector. A session counts as in use from the moment a request asks for it until that request ends, so one being opened is never the one closed. A session closed while a request still uses it, as a new key closes it, keeps its place until that request ends: the one that replaces it waits for room as any other does. A call that breaks its session closes that one only, never one opened in its place since. A session that fails to open is not kept, and one that ends in a network failure, a reply that is not MCP or an error from the server is closed: the next call opens another. A connector's sessions close when the secrets become readable, when its sign-in changes, and a bot's when the bot loses the connector.
- **Signing.** Every request reads the connector's headers and, for OAuth, its access token at the moment it goes out, and adds them then; the session keeps no copy. A stored header the transport sets itself, or one that would change how the request is framed (`Host`, `Content-Length`, `Mcp-Session-Id` and the like), is refused, as is a value with a line break: no request is sent, and the connector needs its form again. A bot's session first checks that the bot is still granted the connector, and a request it no longer may make is not sent: the call is answered as no tool the bot is offered. A `401`, or a `403` whose `Bearer` challenge says the token lacks a scope (`insufficient_scope`), asks the sign-in for new credentials once, naming the token that was refused so one renewed meanwhile is not renewed again, such as an OAuth refresh or a sign-in with that scope, and sends the request again. A connector with only headers does the same when they were replaced since the request was signed, by new values or a secret they name changing, so a refusal of the old ones goes again with the new; a second refusal, or one of the headers it signs with now, means it needs a new sign-in or new values. Each change of what a connector signs with (new values, a sign-in, a new command line, the secrets becoming readable) starts a new generation of it, and a request records what its server said only in the generation it was sent in: a refusal of the old key that comes back after the operator gave a new one is dropped, and asks for no sign-in. A refusal of credentials read again after the request's first refusal, as when it went again with the new key, is of what signs requests now, and counts whenever the request began.
- **Limits.** Opening a session, or listing every page of its tools, may take 30 seconds, and a call, a resource's read or a prompt, any of which can wait on the operator, 10 minutes until its server answers. A call its server answers with a task follows the task until it ends, at most an hour from the call ([Tasks](#connectors)); the `j3` bridge waits 61 minutes and a harness 62, so the server's own deadline answers first. A connection may take 10 seconds to open, and once open the server has 11 minutes to begin its answer, which bounds what the SDK sends on its own. An answer is read up to 8 MiB. A listing is read page by page, up to 64 pages and 8 MiB of tools once cleaned, which is all it holds: past either, it fails as too large. A bot reads at most 100,000 characters of a tool's text: past that the text is cut, and the bot is told so. A call interrupted, as stopping the bot does, or out of time is cancelled at the connector's server (`notifications/cancelled`), and its request is aborted, closing its connection: the SDK alone does not close it (**Verified** at 2.4.0 with a test server), so the fetch J3 Bots gives it knows which call each request belongs to and aborts it with that call.
- **Where it connects.** A connector's address is `https:` with no user name or password. The name is resolved when each connection opens, and every address it resolves to is checked then, so a name that resolves to another address later (DNS rebinding) is checked again: loopback, link-local, unique-local, private and reserved addresses are refused, apart from the operator's own network for a connector they confirmed it for. The cloud metadata services' addresses are refused even then ([Security: connectors](security.md#connectors)). A redirect is never followed by the transport: each one is a new request, checked as the first. A refused address is reported as `private-network` only when the operator could confirm it; anything else is a refusal they cannot lift.
- **What a bot reads.** A tool's answer, a resource or a prompt is text from someone else's server. It is cut to size, any value the server echoed of the credentials it was sent or of the address' path and query is replaced with `[redacted]`, as given and as a JSON string holds them: a value of 8 characters or more wherever it stands, and a value known to be a secret (a secret field's of the form, or what a secret reference resolved to, but a login's user name, which is not one and goes only from 8 characters) of 4 to 7 characters only where it stands as a word of its own, so `4096` goes from `PIN 4096` and stays in `140960` and `C:\reports\r4096.csv`. A structured answer is scrubbed value by value before the bot reads it as JSON, and so is the answer an app is given, so an escape does not hide a value and a number taken out leaves JSON (as the string `[redacted]`). Below 4 characters nothing is taken out: such a value would match ordinary text, which is never rewritten for it (`scrubberOf`, `packages/connectors`). and it reaches the bot framed as data from that connector, between tags with a random nonce the text does not contain, its J3 Bots framing defused (`renderConnectorResult` in `packages/prompts`). When there is no answer the bot gets J3 Bots' own line saying why, as an error result, not a failure of the `j3` tool.

**Its tools.** A listing is cleaned before it is kept: a description capped, it and the strings of each input schema stripped of hidden characters and J3 Bots' framing, the connector's secrets taken out as from an answer, and a tool left out whose name or schema keys that would change, or whose schema holds a number that is one of them. A connector's tools are what its server lists, as it lists them now ([decision 0047](../decisions/0047-j3-bots-is-a-complete-mcp-host-at-the-current-specification.md)): the server keeps the last listing, each tool with its name, description, input schema and `_meta.ui`, and what the server said of itself (the revision they speak, its name and version, and how many tools, resources and prompts it lists), which the connector's page shows. A bot is offered every tool of a connector it may use, named `<slug>__<tool>` within the harnesses' limits on tool names; the slug comes from the connector's name. Its description reaches the bot after a line of J3 Bots' own naming the connector whose server wrote it, saying the tool writes only on the operator's ask or yes, and that the text tells what the tool does, never what else to do. The server lists a connector's tools again whenever a session opens and whenever its server says they changed: one listing at a time per connector, and one more after it however often the server says so meanwhile. It lists, in the background, every connector it has not seen listed since it started, but a command connector listed before: at start, when the secrets become readable, after a sign-in, and when a bot's tools are asked for. A tool the server adds, changes or removes reaches bots with the listing; bots whose offered tools change, by a listing or a grant, are told on `toolsChanged`, which the `j3` bridge follows.

**Resources and prompts.** A bot reads a connector's resources and prompts with four of J3 Bots' own tools ([Prompts: tools](../prompts/architecture.md)): `list_connector_resources` and `list_connector_prompts` (every connector the bot may use, or one, each line a resource, a template or a prompt, filtered by words), `read_connector_resource` and `get_connector_prompt`. What comes back is framed as data from that connector, like a tool's answer, and a listing is cut at 20,000 characters, on whole lines. A connector the bot may not use is refused as unknown; one that is not connected answers J3 Bots' own line saying why. The operator uses a connector's prompts from the composer of a bot's conversation: `/` offers the prompts of the connectors that bot may use (`connector.prompts`), and the one they pick is written by its server with the arguments they give (`connector.renderPrompt`), into the draft, for them to read and send as their own message. Rooms have no `/` menu: a prompt is read through one bot's grant.

**Skills it serves.** A connector's server can serve Agent Skills (MCP's Skills extension, `io.modelcontextprotocol/skills`), which bots load through three of J3 Bots' own tools, never through a harness's skill folder ([decision 0089](../decisions/0089-a-connectors-skills-load-through-j3-tools.md)). Every session declares the extension; when the server declares it too, its skills are listed with its resources and prompts, with the SDK's `SkillsClient` (every page, skipping an invalid entry; **Read**, `@modelcontextprotocol/client` 2.4.0, `ext/skills`), and kept with what signed the request while the listing's `ttlMs` holds. A listing is offered only while what signed it still signs the connector's requests (a new sign-in, new values, or its secrets becoming readable or not change that), so a private one (`cacheScope: private`) never reaches bots under another: when what signs the connector's requests changes, its skills are offered no more, what bots loaded of it is let go, and it is listed again, as a connector its add listed the tools of is; a remote add lists what it serves at once. A notice that a list changed lists them again; a listing that fails keeps the last one, of the same sign-in, until the next. A skill whose address, or a file's, could carry a secret, or with no description, is left out; a description is kept to one line, and one past the extension's limits (512 files, 16 MiB) is left out of what bots see and refused as too large when named (`packages/connectors`, `skills.ts`).
- **What bots see.** `use_connector_skill`'s description lists the skills of every connector the bot may use, each as `connector: skill: what it is for`, a name two of one connector's skills share by its address, cut at 2,000 characters on whole lines, after which a line points to `list_connector_skills`. A bot is told on `toolsChanged` when what it may load changes. The connector's page lists its skills, by name and description (`Connector.skills`, with each one's address to tell two of one name apart; absent until the server reached it).
- **Loading.** `use_connector_skill` names a connector and a skill by name, or by its `SKILL.md`'s address, which may be one its server does not list (`skills/get`). It asks as the bot's permission mode says. Its `SKILL.md` is read and checked against the skill's entry: a file the entry lists, its size and SHA-256, and the frontmatter field by field (`SkillsClient.read`, which also reads again past a stale cached copy). On a mismatch the server gets the entry again with `skills/get` and reads once more; a skill still not as listed is refused with J3 Bots' own line. A skill its server generates (`dynamic`) is checked by its frontmatter only, and at most 16 MiB of it, its `SKILL.md` included, is read for each load. An answer about one skill the SDK refuses as the extension does not allow it (`InvalidResult`) is that skill's, refused as not as listed, never a failure of the connector. The bot gets the `SKILL.md` framed as data from that connector, with J3 Bots' lines after it: the skill grants nothing, its scripts are only files, and how to read the rest. Its `allowed-tools` is ignored.
- **Its files.** The bot holds the entry of each skill it loaded, with what signed it, in the server's memory, at most 32 a bot, the one used longest ago let go first; a restart, or the connector's removal, lets go of them all. `read_connector_skill_file` reads a file of a skill the bot holds from that connector, by its path in the skill's folder, or lists a folder (from the entry, or `resources/directory/read` for a generated skill, whose file the server does not have is taken as a folder to list, or nothing there): an address, a path with `..`, percent-encoded or not, or one the entry does not list is refused before anything is asked. Each read first takes the skill's entry as its server serves it now (its listing while that holds, else `skills/get`): while the skill's `SKILL.md` digest, or a generated skill's frontmatter, is the one the bot loaded, and the connector's requests are signed as they were, the file is read against that entry, which the bot holds from then on, and an entry got again replaces the one its listing kept; once either is not, the bot is let go of the skill and told to load it again, whichever file it asked for. A `SKILL.md` among its files is only a file.
- **The work step.** A load is a `connector-skill` step whose `skill` names the skill and its connector; the server records it with the connector's id as for a connector call, and names the connector as the operator knows it (`connector_steps`). A bot working on one is `working` with that `skill`.

**When a server asks the operator.** A connector's server can ask for something in the middle of a call (elicitation): on 2026-07-28 by answering the call with `input_required`, which the client answers and retries (multi round-trip requests), before it with an `elicitation/create` request. Either way it becomes a `connector-input` card in the conversation of the bot whose call asked, which notifies, and the bot is `waiting` while it is open. A form's fields are checked against the server's schema before Send; a page is shown by its host and opened on the operator's device, `https:` only and with no credentials in its address. Accept, Decline and Not now (`cancel`) go back to the server only, never to the bot, as `card.answer` with `connector-input`; an answer that does not fit the form is `Invalid("connector-input")`. The card closes as `expired` when the call ends unanswered, and a late answer is `Conflict("card-closed")`. A form whose field's key, label or description names a password, passphrase or passcode, a secret or credentials, an API, private or access key, a token, a PIN, a one-time, verification, sign-in or recovery code, an authenticator or a second factor, a card's security code or a social security number is declined unseen, as MCP asks a server never to send one ([Security](security.md#connectors)). A request with no bot's call behind it, as during a listing, is declined, and so is one from a 2025-era server over a process's streams while several bots call it at once, the one case where the call that asked cannot be told. A 2025-era server can also refuse a call with `-32042` (URL elicitation required), naming steps the operator must finish on its own pages first; 2026-07-28 has no such error, and the SDK's server refuses to send it there (**Read** in `@modelcontextprotocol/server` 2.4.0). Each step, up to three, becomes the same `connector-input` card for a page. The call waits, for what is left of its time, the card's answer included, until the server says the step is finished (`notifications/elicitation/complete`, listened for before the card shows), then goes again, once, within what is still left: a server that asks again is not answered. A card left unanswered closes when the time runs out. A step the operator declines, one that is not a page J3 Bots shows, or one not finished in time ends the call, and the bot is told which in fixed words (`step-declined`, `step-unfinished`); neither says anything of the connector's status.

**Tasks, progress and logs.** A call its server answers with a task (the Tasks extension, 2026-07-28 only) follows it with `tasks/get` at the pace the server asks, answers its requests for input as above with `tasks/update`, and ends with its result, at most an hour after it began; stopping the bot's turn sends `tasks/cancel`. A task's `statusMessage`, and a call's progress notifications, show on the bot's work step, cleaned, on one line, at most once a second, and always the one that says the work is done. A call asks for its progress under its own tag, and the client takes those notifications from the connection as they arrive, in order, so each step is shown or passed over before the call's answer and the last one is never lost; the MCP SDK's own progress handling is not used, since it runs a notification's handler after it settles an answer that arrived with it, and drops that step (**Read**, `@modelcontextprotocol/client` 2.4.0, `Protocol._onnotification` and `_onresponse`). A server's log messages go to the server's log at debug level, at most 30 a minute of 500 characters each, scrubbed of the connector's secrets; J3 Bots never asks a 2026-07-28 server for them.

**Events.** The Events extension (`io.modelcontextprotocol/events`, Beta, SEP-3415) is spoken beside the SDK, which has none of it yet (`@modelcontextprotocol/client` 2.4.0): its five methods, `events/list`, `events/poll`, `events/stream`, `events/subscribe` and `events/unsubscribe`, and the stream's notifications, go on the session's tapped transport, as Tasks' requests do (`tap.ts`), in one module (`events.ts`, `McpEvents` on a session) to be swapped for the SDK's once it has them. A session offers it only when the server declares the extension; nothing of it is sent to one that does not. Each request carries `Mcp-Name` and the `Mcp-Param-*` headers its arguments ask for. Its answers are cleaned and scrubbed as any answer is; the extension's errors are told apart (`EventsRefused`: not offered, forbidden, invalid, exhausted, a delivery mode not supported, changed, a webhook not reached, a subscription no longer known). What uses it is the [Event routines](#event-routines) listener.

**Tasks' final text.** Checked against the extension as published: a task that says it completed must carry its result (one without is not an answer, as a malformed one is not), and a poll interval the server asks for that is longer than ten seconds is kept, no longer cut, since the task's own deadline bounds the wait.

**MCP Apps.** A tool's `_meta.ui` is kept with its listing and recorded on each call's work step with the connector, so a client can show the tool's page beside the call.

**Connector apps.** A connector's server can give a tool an interface, as the MCP Apps extension (`io.modelcontextprotocol/ui`, version 2026-01-26) defines it: an HTML document the clients draw in the bot's reply where the call happened ([decision 0048](../decisions/0048-a-servers-interface-runs-isolated-in-j3-bots-clients.md), [decision 0093](../decisions/0093-a-connectors-app-is-part-of-the-bots-reply.md)). The contract is `connectorApp.ts` and `ConnectorAppRpcs` in `packages/contracts`.

- **Declaring it.** Every session tells the connector's server that J3 Bots shows apps: its client's `capabilities.extensions["io.modelcontextprotocol/ui"]` carries the one type the extension defines, `text/html;profile=mcp-app`, next to Tasks (`makeClient`, `packages/connectors`). The MCP SDK (`@modelcontextprotocol/client` 2.4.0) sends it in `initialize` to a 2025-11-25 server and in each request's `clientCapabilities` envelope to a 2026-07-28 one.
- **Which tools.** A listed tool names its app in `_meta.ui.resourceUri`, a `ui://` address of one clean line of at most 2,048 characters (the extension's earlier flat `_meta["ui/resourceUri"]` is read too), and who may call it in `_meta.ui.visibility`, `model`, `app` or both, both when it says nothing or says it wrongly (`toolUiOf`). A tool without `model` is never offered to a bot; one without `app` is never run for an app. Both are part of the tool's digest, so a server that changes them changes what bots are offered as any change to its listing does.
- **Opening one.** A bot's call to a tool that names an app reads the app's resource (`resources/read`) beside the call, through the bot's own session, within the call's deadline. Reading it never fails the call: the bot reads its answer whatever became of the app, and a resource the server does not give, or gives too large, malformed, of another type, or with another document inside it (`visualEscapesGuard`), is no app, which the server logs (`appDocumentOf`). A resource is read up to the answer's 8 MiB and then to about 2.7 MiB of JSON (`MAX_APP_RESOURCE_JSON_BYTES`), and its document must be well-formed text of at most 2 MiB (`MAX_CONNECTOR_APP_BYTES`), as `text` or base64 `blob`; a `blob` that is text has the connector's secrets taken out as `text` has, in an app's document and in any answer an app is given. Of the policy it declares (`_meta.ui.csp`), the server keeps only origins an app may name (`ConnectorAppOrigin`: `https:` or `wss:`, a host of two labels or more, never an IP address or `localhost`, never a path or a keyword), lowercased, at most 32 in each of `connectDomains`, `resourceDomains` and `baseUriDomains`; `frameDomains`, `permissions`, `domain` and `prefersBorder` are not kept.
- **Keeping it.** The app is an entry of the bot's reply: a `connector-app` entry (`ConnectorAppEntry`), added to the conversation or room the call's turn speaks to as the call returns, in the same transaction that keeps its view (`ConnectorApps.record`, `apps/server/src/connectors/ConnectorApps.ts`). So it stands between what the bot said before the call and what it says after, and the call keeps its step in the fold. The entry names the connector, its glyph and the tool as the operator sees it (`ConnectorAppRef`). The view is kept in `connector_app_views` (migrations 0043 and 0063) with the bot, the connector, the tool as the app is told it, the call's arguments, its answer as MCP gives it (`CallToolResult`, the connector's secrets taken out, null past 2 MiB), the kept policy, and the turn whose call opened it; the document once per content, in `connector_app_documents`, by its SHA-256, gone when no view uses it. A turn shows at most 20 apps (`MAX_CONNECTOR_APPS_PER_TURN`). A routine's turn, a side turn, which speaks to no one, shows none; nor does a turn whose room is gone or archived, or that the bot left, while its call ran (`liveTarget`), or one that ended before its call came back, as when the operator stopped it; the bot still reads the tool's answer. After the tool's answer, the bot reads J3 Bots' line that its user sees the app where it called it, and need not repeat what it shows (`APP_SHOWN_LINE`), only when it was shown. A view goes with its entry. Before 0.14.0 a view was kept under its turn's fold, which lists it (`WorkFoldEntry.apps`); such a view names no turn, and its fold still shows it.
- **Drawing it.** `connectorApp.open` gives a client the app. What a client does with it is in [Clients: connector apps](clients.md#connector-apps).
- **Its requests.** `connectorApp.callTool` and `connectorApp.readResource` are the app's own `tools/call` and `resources/read`, which the operator's client passes on. A call says whether the operator allowed it in the app's card; one they did not is refused `needs-allow` unless the bot is in full access as the call goes out. Each goes through the session of the bot whose call opened the app, as that bot's request, under the same grant check as its own calls, checked again once the session is open, since that may wait on a command connector's process starting: refused `gone` when the connector was removed or the bot no longer has it, `unavailable` when it is not connected or does not answer, and `not-for-apps` for a tool the connector does not list or whose visibility leaves out `app`. Its arguments, as any call's, are at most 2 MiB as JSON; an answer past 2 MiB is refused `too-large`. An answered request counts as the connector answering, as a bot's call does; a failing one counts against it only as a bot's would.
- **Its context for the bot.** `connectorApp.setContext` is the app's `ui/update-model-context`: the text of its text blocks and its structured content, at most 8 KiB together (`ConnectorAppContext`). It is kept only while the bot has the connector: it is refused `gone` once the bot no longer has it, the grant read and the context written in one transaction, and what the connector's apps set before for a bot is deleted, unread, in the transaction that takes the connector from it (`ConnectorStore`: a grant that leaves the bot out, or the connector's removal), so a grant given back carries none. The server keeps one per view in `connector_app_contexts` (migration 0063), for the bot and where the app is shown: the bot's conversation, or the room. The app's next one replaces it and an empty one deletes it. The bot's next turn there reads each one not read yet, after the catch-up notes and held teammate messages, as the connector's untrusted content (`app-context`, [Prompts: the envelope](../prompts/architecture.md)); the dispatch that carries it marks it read, and one that ends undelivered leaves it unread. One the envelope has no room for is dropped, and nothing tells the operator. It wakes nobody and shows nowhere in the conversation. A view kept before 0.14.0 is shown where its fold is.
- **Logs.** For each app: the bot, the connector, the tool and the view, and whether it was shown. For each request: the same, with the method and the tool, and its outcome. Never the document, a request's arguments, its answer or a context's text.

**Status.** `connected`, `needs-sign-in` (with why), `needs-secret` (which secret its settings name, and why: `missing`, `no-value`, `not-shared`, or `refused`, the service having refused the credentials it signs in as a service with), `failing` (with why, in J3 Bots' own words, and since when) or `secrets-unavailable`, sent to every client on `connectors.subscribe`. The secrets come first, then a secret the settings name, then a sign-in, then a failure. The server works it out from the secrets, the sign-in and how the connector's server last answered: a listing or an answer clears a failure, and a refused key or token makes it need a sign-in until one works. A call's own trouble (a timeout, too large an answer, an error the tool returned) is that call's and leaves the status as it was; the same while connecting or listing means the server is failing. Bots get none of the tools of a connector that is not connected; a bot that calls one anyway gets a result saying why.

**Cards.** A connector that needs a sign-in asks for it with a `connector-auth` card in a bot's conversation: the bot that added it with `add_mcp_server` (the tool answers that it waits for the operator), and a bot whose call finds that the connector needs a new sign-in. That conversation gets one card for that sign-in's end: no second one while it is open, and every open one closes as `signed-in` when the operator completes a sign-in, or as `removed` with the connector. A sign-in that comes back by itself, as when the secrets become readable, closes none. Each notifies, as `connector`. A sign-in that ends while no bot calls the connector shows only in its status.

**Cost.** A connector started by command costs what its server costs while it runs, which is why it runs only while used and how many may run depends on the memory. A session idles at a few kilobytes, and its notification stream is one idle HTTP request; at the 32-session bound, the server's memory for them is **Assumed** to stay well under 20 MB. This fits a Raspberry Pi with room to spare. A connector's tools add to the tool list every model call pays for, which is what deferred tool loading in the harness keeps small.

**Logs.** A connector's id and host, the tool's name, the outcome, the HTTP status and how long it took. Never a call's arguments or result, and never a secret ([Security: connectors](security.md#connectors)). A sign-in logs its connector and, when it fails, a reason in J3 Bots' words; the request log shows the callback as `/oauth/callback`, without its query, however its path is spelled.

### Secrets in a connector's settings

The code is `apps/server/src/connectors/secretReferences.ts`, with the checks in `Connectors` ([decision 0049](../decisions/0049-the-bots-manage-mcp-servers-and-skills.md)).

**One value for the connector.** The listing, every bot's HTTP session and a command's one shared process send the same settings, so a reference resolves once for the connector, for the bots its grant names: every bot when it goes to every bot. A connector that references a secret is usable only while each of those bots may use it, by the secret's own rule ([Secrets](#secrets)): a secret some bots may use never covers every bot, since that takes in the bots still to come. One process per bot was rejected: it would multiply what a command costs to keep a bot's secret apart from another's, where the grant check already does.

**Granting checks it.** Every path that sets what a connector sends or who it goes to checks each reference in its settings against each bot it will go to: `connector.add`, `connector.update` and `connector.setBots`, from Settings or a bot's tools, and the default of every bot. A refusal is `SecretRefused`, which names the secret and the problem, and keeps nothing:

| Problem | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `missing` | No secret has that name. |
| `no-value` | The secret exists but lacks the value the reference takes, such as a single value's `.username`. |
| `not-shared` | A bot the connector would go to may not use the secret. |
| `not-yours` | A bot's change makes the reference, as its values render into the settings, and that bot may not use the secret. |
| `in-address` | A reference in the address, raw or percent-encoded, where none is resolved. |
| `destination` | A bot's change of a connector that references a secret would change where it sends it. |

The bots' tools answer each in fixed words (`MANAGEMENT_REFUSED`), naming the secret; the clients say it beside the field or the bots picker. Granting the secret later, or narrowing the connector's bots, makes it usable without adding it again.

**A connector keeps where it sends a secret.** The promise of [Secrets](#secrets) is that a bot uses a secret without reading it. A connector sends its secrets where the operator saw them go when it was added, from Settings or as a bot's step they allowed; a bot that could change that could send one to a server of its own and read it there. So once a connector's settings reference a secret, a bot's change must leave where they send it as it was: the address, the headers, one of which can choose where a gateway forwards the rest, the command's program and arguments, and its variables, each compared with its references taken out. Which secrets those places name it may change, under `not-yours` as above; anything else is `destination`, whatever the bot's permission mode, since this is the store's rule, not an approval. The operator can change a command connector's line on its page (web and desktop, [decision 0068](../decisions/0068-settings-lists-what-the-bots-added.md)); anything else takes the bot removing the connector and adding a new one, asking the operator for the secret again with `ask_for_secret`. What the rule does not cover:

- **A new connector.** A server a bot adds may reference any secret the bot may use ([decision 0046](../decisions/0046-secrets-the-operator-types-a-bot-uses-a-reference.md)), so a secret shared with a bot reaches whatever server it adds and its permission mode lets through: in Supervised, the operator approves the add, seeing its address or command. A secret is shared with the bots that need it for that reason.
- **A key typed as a plain value.** A command's variable the operator filled with a key itself, not a reference, is not one of their secrets: a bot that may change that connector can change its other variables, and the key goes with the process. A key kept as a secret and referenced is held where it is sent.

**Never a literal.** The references resolve when the server makes a request or starts the process, never before, and a value is not kept anywhere else. A secret that goes missing, loses its value or stops covering the connector's bots after the connector was added makes it `needs-secret`, naming it: its sessions close, its process stops, bots get none of its tools, resources or prompts, and a call answers why; a connector app's requests, which go through the bot's session, are refused `unavailable`. Nothing is sent, so no `${secret:…}` text reaches a server. A change that may alter what a reference resolves to (the secrets becoming readable or not, a secret added, changed, removed or shared differently) checks every connector again, with a SHA-256 of what its references resolved to; revealing a secret or recording its use changes none, and checks nothing (`Secrets.referenceStatus`): a new value closes the connector's sessions and stops its process, so the next request or start uses it, and forgets a refusal of the old value, as giving the connector its values again does. A session resolves for the bots the connector goes to at each request, not those of when it opened. Values given with a new command line are its variables', checked and written with it in one step.

**Values stay on the server.** None reaches a client, a log, an error or a bot's context. A server's answer is scrubbed of each value its settings resolved to, an argument's as a variable's, before a bot or the log sees it; the status and the refusals name only the secret.

### What the bots manage

The bots add, change and remove MCP servers and skills with the `j3` tools above, through the same services as the operator's clients, so the rules are the same, and so are the records ([decision 0049](../decisions/0049-the-bots-manage-mcp-servers-and-skills.md)).

**Who may do what.** The server decides on every call, for the bot whose running turn the call is matched to ([J3 Bots' own tools](#bot-tools)). The primary bot manages every server and every skill: its own, any other bot's, and those for every bot. Any other bot manages a server only while it alone uses it, and only the skills in its own workspace; a server the primary bot gave it too, or one for every bot, it uses and cannot change. A refusal says why, and changes nothing.

**Not pre-approved.** The tools that change something are left off the list of `j3` tools each harness runs without asking, so the bot's permission mode decides: an approval card in Supervised, the provider's reviewer in Auto, a run in Full access. J3 Bots adds no card of its own. A call the operator declined does nothing, as a declined connector call does.

**Servers.** A server is added as `connector.add` adds one ([Adding](#connectors)), with two differences: an address in the operator's own network, and a gallery entry that needs an app the operator registers, wait on the operator's yes ([The operator's yes](#the-operators-yes)); and a value may be a reference, `${secret:<name>}`, to a secret the operator saved ([decision 0046](../decisions/0046-secrets-the-operator-types-a-bot-uses-a-reference.md)), stored as written. A bot may write only a reference to a secret it may use itself (`not-yours` otherwise), so it cannot send another bot's secret to a server of its choosing, and the grant check applies as it does to the operator's ([Secrets in a connector's settings](#secrets-in-a-connectors-settings)). A bot never sees a value it did not write, and once a server's settings reference a secret, it changes only which secrets they name, never where they send them ([A connector keeps where it sends a secret](#secrets-in-a-connectors-settings)). A server that needs a sign-in is added, and its sign-in goes to the operator as a card in the bot's conversation ([Cards](#connectors)).

**Records.** Each server and each skill keeps who changed it last, the operator or which bot, and when (`lastChange`, migration 0040). The clients show it beside each one in Settings → MCP and skills while it exists; a removal leaves no record there. Each change by a bot, a removal included, shows as a step of its work (`mcp-server-added`, `mcp-server-changed`, `mcp-server-removed`, `skill-saved`, `skill-removed`, and the reads).

#### The operator's yes

An address in the operator's own network, and a gallery entry that signs in only with an app the operator registers (`own-app`: Google and Slack), need the operator's own word, which a bot cannot give ([decision 0068](../decisions/0068-settings-lists-what-the-bots-added.md)). For those, `add_mcp_server` adds nothing yet: it puts a `connector-ask` card in the bot's own conversation, also during a room turn, since only the operator answers it, and tells the bot it asked; the operator is notified as for an approval. Work the operator stopped shows no card, and the call says so.

- **What the card names.** An address by its host and port, as the bot wrote it (`192.168.1.20:8123`), so the operator reads where the server would connect; one whose name resolves into the operator's network is found by the probe that lists its tools, and asks the same way, under that name. A gallery entry whose address is in the operator's network is still refused: the bot can add it by its address, which asks. A gallery entry by its listing: the client reads its setup steps, in the operator's language, and the addresses its sign-ins return to with `connectors.get`.
- **The answer.** `card.answer` with `connector-ask`: Allow, for an address; for a gallery entry, Allow with the client ID and the optional client secret of the operator's app, redacted from the moment they are decoded. Decline is `dismiss`. Only that answer records the yes. A client that does not fit what the card asks for (one for an address, none for a gallery entry) is `Invalid("answer")`, and the card stays open.
- **The add.** On the yes, the server runs the add the bot asked for, by the same rules, with the operator's confirmation of the address or with their client, under the id the bot's call would have given it, and for no more bots than its call could have granted then and can now: a bot made primary meanwhile does not widen it. Once taken, the add and the close finish even when the operator's connection drops. The card closes `added`, the connector then waiting on a sign-in as any connector does ([Cards](#connectors)), or `failed`, with why for the bot. The client and the secret never reach the bot: they go to the connector's secret only.
- **What is kept.** What the bot asked to add can carry a key, in an address or a value, so it stays in the server's memory only, never in its database. A restart closes an open ask as expired, within a minute, and the bot can ask again. The table `connector_asks` (migration 0052) keeps each ask's audience, when it expires and how it closed. No receipt keeps an answer: it can carry a secret, and the add calls the connector's server, so no transaction is held while it runs. A second answer, or a Decline while the add runs, finds the card closed (`Conflict("card-closed")`).
- **Closing.** An ask nobody answered for 24 hours expires, and an answer after that is refused even before the sweep closes the card. Each close wakes the bot, for the audience of the turn that asked, with a system event that names the outcome (`added`, `declined`, `failed` or `expired`), the server's name and, after a yes, a note: what comes next, such as a sign-in, or why the add failed. An answer is the operator's word, which no Stop cancels ahead of time; an expiry continues the work that asked, so a Stop of that work leaves the bot asleep.

### Skills

The contract is in `packages/contracts` (`skill.ts`, `SkillRpcs`); the server serves it from `apps/server/src/skills`. A skill is an Agent Skills folder: a `SKILL.md` whose YAML frontmatter gives its `name`, which is the folder's, and a `description` of when it applies, and the files beside it.

**Where they live.** The shared skills are in `shared/skills/`, J3 Bots' own (`j3-…`) among them, which nobody saves, installs or removes. `skills.subscribe` lists them, marked `shipped`, and the clients leave them out of every list the operator sees ([decision 0068](../decisions/0068-settings-lists-what-the-bots-added.md)). A bot's own are in its workspace's `skills/`. A bot's skill cannot take a shared one's name, nor the reverse (`Conflict("skill-name-taken")`): Claude Code would list one of the two. The limits are a template's ([decision 0023](../decisions/0023-a-bot-template-is-a-file.md)): a name of one lowercase segment, a `SKILL.md` of 20,000 characters, a description of 1,024, 64 files of 2 MiB in all, and 64 skills in each place (`Conflict("skill-limit")`).

**Reaching the harnesses.** A change to the shared skills links them into each harness home at once, not only at the engine's next start; a change to a bot's links its `skills/` into `.claude/skills` and `.agents/skills` of its workspace, which Claude Code and Codex list. No session is restarted. Both harnesses watch those folders (**Read**); that a session already running lists a skill linked meanwhile is **Assumed**, so the tools tell a bot it has the skill from its next turn ([Engine](engine.md#behaviors-j3-bots-designs-around)).

**Saving.** `skill.save` and `save_skill` write a `SKILL.md`; the bot's tool also takes a folder of its workspace, read without following a link. The frontmatter is read as the harnesses read it, and one that does not name the skill, or gives no description, is `Invalid("skill-text")`. Every location is checked as other private state is ([Data](#data)), and a link where a skill goes is `Conflict("private-location")`.

**Installing.** `install_skill`, and `skill.install`, which no client sends since 0.10.0 and stays for clients on protocol 11, fetch one folder of a public git repository at a branch, tag or commit: an `https:` address, checked and pinned as a connector's is, so none in the operator's network; `git` 2.37 or later on the server's `PATH`, with none of the operator's configuration, no prompt, no credential helper, no redirect and no hook; two minutes in all. The folder is read from git's own objects at the commit, with nothing checked out and nothing run. A link, a submodule, a file past the limits or a `SKILL.md` whose name is not the skill's makes it `Conflict("skill-repository")` with the reason. The source is recorded with the commit fetched, and shown with the skill.

**Removing.** `skill.remove` and `remove_skill` delete the folder and its record, and the links with them.

## Budgets

Three limits keep bots from spending the operator's allowance on each other. All three have conservative defaults and are part of the operator's settings (`settings.get`, `settings.update`), with the time zone and the notification preferences. Only the operator changes them; nothing a bot or a message says raises one. A room is bounded by its own rounds of its bots answering each other, which are fixed ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)); hops and fan-out apply to teammate messages.

| Limit | Default | Applies to |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hops | 4 | Consecutive teammate messages in one chain since the operator last spoke |
| Fan-out | 1 | Teammates one bot turn may bring in |
| Concurrent turns | 2 | Turns running at once across all bots |

**Hops.** Every wake carries a hop count. The operator's wakes, a routine's, J3 Bots' system events and every room wake carry 0. A teammate message a bot's turn sends carries 1 when the turn carries the operator's word (their wake, or their steer the turn took), so a chain starts again whenever they speak; otherwise one more than the furthest wake or held message the turn delivers. A room turn delivers room wakes, which carry 0, so a teammate message a member sends from one starts a chain of its own. A steer riding a turn counts once the turn took it (`steer-taken`): the engine requeues as a follow-up turn only a steer it still holds back for one (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `Orchestrator.ts`, `requeueUnreadSteers`), so until then the operator's word may yet start a turn of its own, where it counts instead, and one message of theirs starts one chain. Codex takes a steer as it is sent. Claude Code takes it once the provider echoes it: the engine records a steer as handed over before Claude has it, and Claude's driver then holds the record back until the echo, so the adapter counts a Claude Code steer as taken only once its record has waited and moved on (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `Orchestrator.ts` L6660-6688, `ClaudeAdapterV2.ts` L15191 and L10171). A call the turn makes before the echo still counts without it, and a teammate message the limit stops then is refused, never sent past the limit. A turn the engine started on its own never carries the operator's word: one that continues an earlier turn counts as that turn ([Rooms](#rooms)), as does the turn that brings back a delegated task's report, whose dispatch carries only that report and held messages, counted on top of it, and one that names none takes, when it is admitted, the furthest count of the session's other turns whose background work is flagged, a flagged turn no dispatch began counting by the count it took itself, and keeps that count for the turns that go on with its own work. A flag may outlive its work until the engine's next report, so the count can come out too high, never too low; with none to trace it to, it may message no teammate, as its tool result says. A teammate message whose count would pass the limit is not sent.

**Fan-out.** It counts the distinct teammates one turn messages, with two exceptions. A turn the operator spoke in, by the wake that started it (their message or their answer to an `ask`, not a dismissed card), counts none: the operator asked for the work. A steer of theirs riding a turn does not count: the engine tells whether the agent took it only once the turn has ended. A continuation, or a turn the engine started for a turn's background work, inherits no such word: it counts the teammates its whole chain brought in. So does the continuation of a steer the engine made a turn of its own (`new-turn`). And a teammate whose message the turn carries, as a `bot` wake or a held message in its envelope, is answered, not brought in. Past those, a turn may bring in at most the limit; messaging a teammate it already messaged is no wider.

**Refused at the source.** A budget is checked by the tool call that would pass it, in the transaction that pins the call, against what is committed for the pinned turn: its wakes, its held messages, the teammates its earlier calls reached. SQLite serializes writes, so two calls never both take the last hop or the last teammate. Nothing past a budget is ever written, so the dispatcher checks none. A refusal wakes nobody, not even to report it: a wake to report a budget stop would itself be a bot turn spent. The tool's refusal tells the bot, and the sender's conversation shows it in one quiet `chain-stopped` line, once per turn however often the turn tries. Nothing is dropped silently. A room that goes quiet shows its own `chain-stopped` line ([The room's rounds](#the-rooms-rounds)).

The concurrent-turns budget counts one fact, read again before each first send: a session with work the engine may have, a turn its events show or a dispatch sent at least once and not settled. A dispatch never sent holds no slot, also while its files wait to be staged again. Every start, a staging retry included, needs a free slot or one its session already holds, checked again when its first send is claimed.

## Notifications

The server sends a notification when a bot adds an entry to a conversation or room and no connected client currently has it in view. A bot that starts waiting on the operator adds a card, so a question, an approval or a credential request notifies the same way.

**What notifies.** A bot's message and its question, approval, credential, browser help and connector cards, a run of its routine in steps that waits for the operator's yes or failed at a step other than by a Stop (as a `question`, with a `routine` field naming the routine and why in fixed words), a hidden bot's or in a hidden room as any other's: hiding leaves the list, not the notifications ([Visibility](#visibility)). Nothing else does: the operator's own entries, a turn's work fold, notices (quiet lines by design; the roster shows a blocked bot), routine changes (the bot's own message about one notifies) and teammate messages, urgent or not ([Teammate messages](#teammate-messages)). Every notification follows from an entry, so a routine run that ends silently, which adds none, never notifies, and one that speaks notifies like any message.

**What is in view.** Each socket reports what the operator is looking at through it (`client.view`): a conversation or room, or nothing. A client reports a target only while its window shows it uncovered (no details sheet or dialog, such as Settings, an editor or a confirmation, over it), is visible and has focus; otherwise it reports nothing. The server keeps each socket's report in memory, and forgets it with the socket, so a client reports it again on every connection. An entry is in view when a socket of a device paired and not revoked reports its target.

**Who is told.** The server knows which paired device each socket belongs to, and groups sockets by device. For an entry that notifies, it works out two sets of devices, both among those paired and not revoked, each device once: the **live recipients**, which have a socket open and are told on every one of them, on its `notifications.subscribe` stream; and the **devices to push**, which have none open and registered for push. Both sets are worked out once per entry, together with what is in view, so a device is told on its sockets or pushed to, never both; a device that connects in between is left to the push. The stream is live only: a notification sent while a device was disconnected is not replayed, since the roster shows the entry unread when it returns. An entry in view, or sent while the operator has notifications off, reaches neither set. A notification names the target, the entry, the bot and the kind of entry, and, when the operator allows previews, the entry's first line, at most 200 characters (`PREVIEW_LENGTH`): the message, the question or the action.

**The operator's time zone** is their device's until they choose one. The server starts at UTC, and every client gives its device's zone when its socket opens (`settings.deviceTimeZone`): the first that gives one the server knows, and that does not keep UTC's clock all year, sets the operator's, in a statement that changes the row only while no zone was set, and rewrites every bot's instructions. After that a device's zone changes nothing, so a second device in another zone, or a laptop on a trip, never moves the routines' clock; only the operator does, in Settings. A zone on UTC's clock all year (no offset in any month, so no daylight saving) is passed over by its clock, not by a list of names: it is what a browser that hides its zone, a machine whose zone was never set or an emulator reports, and it says no more than the UTC the server starts at, so the operator's real device still sets the zone after it. An operator who wants UTC chooses it in Settings, which sets it. A database from before this rule counts a zone other than UTC as set, and lets UTC give way to the first device. On a new server a browser in São Paulo set `America/Sao_Paulo`, and a phone in Lisbon paired after it left that zone, offering its own in Settings (**Verified** with Chrome and the Pixel 8 emulator, Android 16).

**The settings stream.** `settings.subscribe` sends the settings row and then every change to it, from the operator's edits, a device's time zone and a bot's `set_operator_name` alike, so Settings on every device shows the name a bot saved without being opened again. It does not resume: each subscription starts with the row as it is. Each publish, like each render of the base prompt, reads the row while it holds the stream's lock, so of two changes close together the later one is what stays.

**The operator's preferences** are in the settings row (`settings.get`, `settings.update`): whether the server notifies at all (on by default; a browser still shows nothing until the operator lets it) and whether a notification carries a preview (off by default, because a notification can show where others see it, such as on a locked screen). A change names only the switch it turns, and the server merges it field by field in the transaction that saves it, so a device holding a stale copy never turns the other one back. Each notification reads them as it is sent.

- **Web and desktop**: over the open connection, shown by the client.
- **Mobile**: a push through a push service, for a device with no socket open (Push delivery, below).

**Push registration.** A device that can receive pushes registers for them on its own socket (`push.register`): the push service (Expo's, for now), the token that service gave the app, the platform, and the language the device shows, which a push to it is written in. A device registers only itself, never another. A second registration replaces the first, and a token belongs to one device: registering a token another device holds takes it from that device, so an app that paired again is not pushed to twice. `push.unregister` ends the registration, and revoking the device, which signing out does, deletes it in the same transaction, so a revoked device is never pushed to. Both commands check that the device is still paired before they look for a receipt, so a command a revoked device sent before fails with `NotFound("device")` instead of being answered as it was. The Devices list shows which devices have push on.

**Push delivery.** For each device to push, the server writes a delivery in the transaction that adds the entry, worded then, in the language the device registered with, from the shared catalog (`packages/i18n`): the title is the bot's name, or the room's, and the body is the entry's first line when the operator allows previews, or else a fixed sentence saying what arrived, such as "Sent you a message"; the fixed sentence is kept as well. A bot's request for help in its browser never shows its note, which is the bot's own words and could quote a sign-in link or a code onto a locked screen: its preview is fixed words for why the bot asks and the site its tab is on, "Needs your help in the browser: Sign in on example.com", which a notification on an open connection carries as `browserHelp` in place of a text preview. One an earlier release wrote with the note and had not sent yet goes with the fixed sentence. A bot or room with no name to show gets none, nor does a registration the server can no longer read, which is logged. Neither working out who hears of an entry nor writing its pushes ever fails the entry: a failure is logged by the entry and the failure's tag, and the entry and its change commit without its pushes; the devices with a socket open are still told once they were worked out. The **push worker** sends each through the push sender, Expo's service for now ([decision 0014](../decisions/0014-push-goes-through-a-push-sender.md)), with data naming the device, the conversation or room and the entry, the delivery's id as its key, and, to an Android device, the entry's kind (`NotificationKind`) as the notification channel it shows in, which the app creates one of for each kind. Each send is claimed before it is made: recorded as begun, with the time the next may begin, only while its deadline has not passed and the device still has the registration the pass read, generation included: a delivery whose device was revoked, unregistered or registered again before its send was claimed, even after the pass read it, is not sent to the registration it no longer has. The claim reads the settings in its own transaction and words the send by them: none while notifications are off, which drops the delivery, and the fixed sentence once previews are off, never the other way. A change to the settings commits before a claim, and holds for its send, or after it, once that send is under way. After a crash or a lost reply, the same delivery goes again at that time with the same key, which the service uses to replace a notification still shown rather than add a second. A pass reads at most a hundred due receipts, then claims at most a hundred due pushes and sends them in one request, the most Expo takes in one, and runs again soon while more are due, so slow sends never keep a later pass from reading the receipts and ending what passed its deadline first. The service answers each message in the request on its own. Expo takes one request only for the apps of one EAS project, and a device may run a build of another: a request it refuses for that (`PUSH_TOO_MANY_EXPERIENCE_IDS`) is sent again one message per request, each answered as its own request is; once one of those goes unanswered, the rest are not sent then, and wait as unanswered. A send the service does not take is tried again, from 5 seconds doubling to 5 minutes: one in a request the service did not answer in 30 seconds, could not be reached for, answered with 429 or 5xx, or answered with a count of answers that is not its count of messages, so that none can be matched to its message; and one it refused because too many went to the device (`MessageRateExceeded`). A service that does not answer says nothing of any one message, so of the sends it did not answer only the one furthest along counts; the others give theirs back, and an outage spends one push's sends at a time instead of every push's. The server records, durably, when the service may next be tried, as long as that send waits: no send begins before then, one for a delivery written since included, so a service that does not answer is not asked again early, and receipts, read first, never wait on it. A request the service refuses as a whole, with any other HTTP error, is a refusal for good of each message in it. A send is tried at most eight times and within an hour of the entry; past either bound the delivery is dropped, logged, and counted on the registration's failures, as is a message the service refuses for good. Once the service took a message, its words are deleted and its receipt is read fifteen minutes later, then every thirty minutes for up to a day; each request for receipts stands alone, so one that fails does not lose another's answers. A receipt that says it was handed on ends the delivery; one that says too many went to the device drops it, logged but not counted as a failure, since its words are gone. A device the service says it no longer reaches (`DeviceNotRegistered`, in the answer to a send or in a receipt) loses its registration, and gets no pushes until it registers again; that answer, like a failure counted, holds only for the registration the send went to, by its generation, so a device that registered again since, even with the same token in the same millisecond, keeps its new one. The logs name deliveries and devices, Expo's documented error codes and a failure's tag, never a token, a failure's text or what a push says.

## The bot's browser

A bot that browses does it in the engine's browser ([Engine: the bot's browser](engine.md#the-bots-browser)). The server shows the operator what the bot has open and lets a paired device take a tab over: to sign in, to answer what the bot cannot, to show it where to click. It never acts on a page itself.

**The tabs.** `BrowserTabs` follows the engine's tabs of every session still in use for as long as the server runs, and gives each the bot whose session opened it. A tab of no bot's session is left out. Clients name a tab by a `BrowserTabRef` the server mints the first time it sees it. A ref names nothing once the tab closes, nor after a restart, so a client never keeps one. `browser.subscribe` streams one bot's tabs, a snapshot and then the whole list on every change, as the device that asked sees them:
- `controller` is `you` when this device holds the tab, `another-device` when another one does, and `bot` otherwise;
- `front` is the tab that most recently opened or navigated.

**The media socket.** `GET /api/browser/ws` shows one tab. The upgrade is authenticated as the RPC socket's is, before it becomes a socket ([Client API](#client-api)):
- a device token as a bearer, or a one-use ticket;
- the client gate's `Host` check first, as on every route;
- without a credential, a 401.

A query that does not decode becomes a socket that closes at once with 1008. A ref the server does not know gets `gone` and a normal close. The contract's `BrowserServerFrame` and `BrowserClientFrame` are what it carries, besides the pictures. A client frame that does not decode is dropped unread.

**One viewer per device and tab.** `BrowserViewers` opens one engine viewer for a device's first socket on a tab. The device's next socket on the same tab takes that viewer over, control included, and the socket before it closes with 4409. Two devices on one tab have a viewer each, so each can tell who holds it.
- A device's sockets on one tab take their turns, in the order they came: each finds the device's viewer, or opens it when there is none, and attaches to it before the next socket does. So a socket that comes while the viewer is still opening waits for it, and the device never has two engine viewers of one tab. The last socket to come is the one left attached.
- A viewer that ends while a socket attaches, its tab closing for one, is opened again once, if the tab is still listed. Otherwise the socket closes with 1013. A viewer that ended because its device was revoked is not opened again: the socket closes as the device's other sockets did, with 1008. So does a socket whose device is revoked while the engine is still opening its viewer: the open is abandoned without waiting on the engine.
- A client message over 4 MiB closes its socket with 1009. The limit is shared with the RPC socket, whose largest message is a bot template; the media socket's are a few kilobytes.

**Pacing.** One picture is in flight to a client at a time.
- A picture that arrives while one is in flight waits. A newer one takes its place, and the engine is acknowledged for the one dropped unsent.
- The engine is acknowledged for a picture only once the client acknowledged it, so a slow client slows the engine's capture rather than piling pictures up.
- A socket whose write buffer would pass 2 MiB is dropped: one picture in flight never comes near that, so only a client that stopped reading gets there.
- The first picture may never come for a page that does not repaint ([Engine: the bot's browser](engine.md#the-bots-browser)). A client shows that it waits for it.

**Dropped links, and the grace.**
- The server pings every 15 s, and a socket it has heard nothing from for 45 s counts as dropped.
- A device that does not hold the tab gives up its viewer when its socket leaves, however it leaves: closed, dropped for a full buffer, or cut off with its connection.
- A device that holds the tab keeps it, and its viewer, for 60 s. A socket of that device that comes back within that time gets the viewer as it was: control, the page's size and the last picture the client drew. After 60 s the viewer closes, which gives the tab back to the bot.

**Revocation.** Revoking a device closes its viewers at once, those in their grace included, and their sockets with 1008. A held tab goes back to the bot then, not after the grace.

**Ends.** When the engine's stream ends, the socket ends with it:
- the tab closed: `gone`, then a normal close;
- the host needs setting up before the browser can start: `setup-needed` with what it lacks;
- the browser restarted: 1012, and the client connects again;
- anything else: 1013.

**Nothing is kept.** A picture lives in memory only, for as long as its viewer: the one in flight, the newest waiting behind it, and the last one the client drew, for a socket that takes the viewer over. None is written to disk or to the database, logged, cached or sent in a push. What a client types goes to the engine and nowhere else. The request log records the socket's path without its query, so a ticket is not logged ([Security: the bot's browser](security.md#the-bots-browser)).

### Help in the browser

Some steps on a page are the operator's to take: a check that a person is there, a sign-in the bot has no credential for, a one-time code, a code to scan, a passkey. The bot asks for one with `request_browser_help`, giving a reason from that list (or `other`) and a note in its own words. It never names a tab: the server takes the bot's front tab, so the bot never sees, and cannot forge, a tab's id.

**The card.** The request adds a `browser-help` entry to the bot's own conversation, also during a room turn, as an engine request's card goes there; the room is told the bot waits in its conversation, as for a secret request. The entry shows the reason, the note, the page's host name (only for an `http` or `https` page, never its path or query) and the tab's `BrowserTabRef`, which a client opens the browser on. A row in `browser_help_cards` keeps what the entry does not show: the audience and the dispatch that began the turn's work, the engine's id for the tab, which never leaves the server, and when the card stops waiting. Like an `ask`, the request ends the turn's delivery. The tool's result tells the bot its turn ends there, and the server stops that turn itself ([A Stop of one turn](#stop)), so a bot that would carry on in the browser anyway cannot: the operator's answer wakes it in a fresh turn. It notifies as a card of its own kind, `browser-help`, which Android shows in a channel of its own.

The request is refused, with nothing written, in a routine's side session, where nobody waits to help; when the bot has no tab open; for work the operator stopped; while another of the bot's browser help cards waits; and for the tab of the bot's last card when the operator declined it or let it run out and has not written since, in the bot's conversation or the room the request is for. The refusal says the operator already answered, so the bot tells them what the step still blocks instead of asking again; their next message lifts it. "Since" is by the order of entries (the last entry when the card closed), not by time. A retried request, with the same call id, gets the answer the first one got, also once the tab is gone.

**How it closes.** Once, in one of three ways, each in the transaction that wakes the bot:

- **The operator answers** `done` ("Pronto") or `declined` ("Não consigo") through `card.answer`, with a receipt as for any card. The answer closes the card first, and only then does any device of theirs that holds one of the bot's tabs let go of it, with the server waiting up to 5 s for the engine to report the tabs released before it answers. In that order nothing that letting go sets off, the expiry below included, can close the card in between. The wake can start before the release is reported; its first call is a snapshot, which reads the page whoever holds it. The same answer from another of the operator's devices, to a card already closed with it, succeeds and changes nothing. A different answer to a closed card fails with `Conflict("card-closed")`.
- **The tab closes.** Once the card's tab has been missing from the engine's tabs for two minutes, the card expires (`tab-closed`). The grace keeps a short gap, while the server follows the engine's tabs again, from closing it.
