- **Nobody answers.** An hour after the request, the card expires (`timed-out`), but only while no device holds one of the bot's tabs: an operator still at work in the browser keeps it open, and for two minutes after they give the tabs back (`HAND_BACK_GRACE`), so giving back in the viewer and then tapping "Pronto" closes it as done.

The server keeps three things about the bot's browser in memory only, from its start: since when a card's tab has been missing, when a device took the bot's tabs, and when it last gave them back. After a restart a missing tab's two minutes count again from then, a card past its hour has no hand-back grace, and a takeover that spanned the restart wakes nothing when it ends. Each errs towards waiting longer for the operator or waking the bot less, never towards closing a card they are still answering.

Each close records a `system` wake, `browser-help`, with the outcome and the host name, for the audience of the turn that asked, so the bot carries on where it was asked. On `done` the envelope tells it to take a fresh snapshot before it acts. An answer is the operator's word, which a Stop never cancels ahead of time. An expiry continues the work that asked, so a Stop that covers that work drops it, as it drops the work's other wakes.

**A takeover without a card.** The operator can take a tab the bot did not ask about. The bot's actions on it then fail with `humanControl`, while its reads still work ([Engine](engine.md#the-bots-browser)), and its browser rules tell it to stop and end its turn. When the operator gives the browser back (no device holds any of the bot's tabs any more), the server wakes the bot once (`handed-back`) if a turn of its main session ran while they held it and nothing else will wake it about the browser: no card was open or closed meanwhile, no browser help wake waits, and the operator did not stop that turn's work. The wake goes to that turn's audience. A takeover while the bot was idle wakes nothing.

## Suggested replies

After a turn, a harness can guess the message the operator will send next, as T3 Code's own composer offers it. J3 Bots offers that guess in a bot's conversation only where it follows what the operator saw ([decision 0073](../decisions/0073-a-suggested-reply-follows-what-the-operator-saw.md)).

**Where a guess comes from.** The engine keeps the latest guess of each session, with the turn it followed, and streams it (`Engine.suggestedReplies`; **Read** at `bc0f04d`: `orchestration.subscribePromptSuggestion`, kept in memory for at most 256 sessions and lost when the engine restarts). It makes them only while its `promptSuggestionsEnabled` setting is on, which the server writes from the operator's switch (`suggest_replies` in Settings, on by default) when it provisions the engine and whenever the switch changes, trying again a few times while the engine does not answer. Claude Code makes a guess as part of the turn, with its SDK's prompt suggestions; Codex makes one with a low-effort request of its own after the turn, which asks for a message under 20 words (**Read**). Claude Code makes none for a delegated thread or for a turn it started itself, and Codex drops one that reads as a delegated task (**Read**).

**Which guess is offered.** A harness guesses from its whole session: the envelopes the bot was woken with, the working text `say` never showed, and every audience the main session serves. So the server follows only each bot's main session, and offers a guess in its conversation only while all of these hold:

- the turn it follows was woken only by the operator in that conversation: every wake it took, its first and those steered into it, is a `user` wake, so neither a teammate's turn the operator steered nor the operator's turn a teammate's urgent message was steered into counts; and it spoke for the conversation and completed;
- the last message the turn said there (`turns.said_entry_id`, written by `say` and by the host speech that stands for one) is still the conversation's newest entry, the turn's own work fold aside;
- the text, trimmed, is at most 300 characters and holds nothing that reads as an envelope's markup;
- the switch is on.

A guess after a room's turn, a teammate's message, a routine's run or anything the server or the engine started is never offered, in a room or anywhere. A room never gets one: the session that answers there also holds the bot's conversation and its other rooms, so its guess could carry one audience's words into another's.

**How it reaches clients.** An offered guess is on its bot as `Bot.suggestedReply`, its text and the entry it follows, which the roster carries. The server weighs it again whenever the bot's state or its conversation changes, and whenever the switch does: anything newer in the conversation takes it away. It lives in memory, as the engine's guesses do, and is weighed again from the engine and the database after either restarts. A client shows it only while the entry it follows is still the newest it shows ([Clients](clients.md#suggested-replies)), and never sends it by itself.

**Cost.** One guess per completed turn of every session the engine runs while the switch is on, rooms, teammate messages and routines included, though only some are offered: a cached request for Claude Code, one more low-effort request for Codex, each on the operator's own plan. Turning the switch off stops the offers at once; Codex stops guessing from its next turn, and Claude Code once its session's process starts again (**Read**).

## Dictation

The operator dictates into the composer; the words land there and nothing is sent by itself ([decision 0096](../decisions/0096-dictation-goes-through-the-engines-speech-recognition-and-stays-on-the-operators-machines.md)). Where speech becomes words is the operator's choice, one for the server and every device (`Settings.voice`, migration 0064): Automatic (a phone's own on-device recognizer when it has the language, the server otherwise), This device, This server, or a cloud provider (OpenAI, xAI, OpenRouter). A phone that recognizes on its own sends the server nothing but the message, once the operator sends it. Everything else comes to the server on the voice socket.

**The voice socket** (`HTTP_PATHS.voice`, `voice/VoiceSocket.ts`) is authenticated as the RPC socket is, before the upgrade: a device token as a bearer or a one-use ticket; without one it answers 401. One socket is one dictation: the client sends `start`, then the recording as binary frames (mono 16 kHz 32-bit float, at most a second each), then `finish`, with the draft around the words for the AI's tidy-up; the server answers as `VoiceServerFrame` says, and closes the socket after `done` or `failed`. A client that hangs up first cancels it. A revoked device's dictation ends with its sockets. Nothing it carries is logged or written anywhere: the audio passes through memory to the engine.

**On the server** (`voice/Voice.ts`), a dictation runs the engine's local model live:

- **One at a time.** The engine runs one recognition at a time, so the server does too; a second device's dictation is answered `busy` at once.
- **The model**: the one Settings chose, or J3 Bots' pick when that is null or no longer in the engine's catalog (`EngineSpeech.recommended`). Before the first dictation with it, the server has the engine download it and make it the one in use, and tells the client how far the download got (`installing`). What the client sends meanwhile is dropped: it shows the download, not a microphone. Every client sees the download in `voice.subscribe`. A dictation cancelled during the download leaves it running, so the next one finds the model ready.
- **Unloading.** Before each dictation the server sets how long the engine keeps the model loaded after its last use: two minutes on a host with 8 GiB of memory or less, fifteen on a bigger one.
- **Live words.** Each piece of audio is passed to the engine in order, and each answer that heard something new goes to the client as `words`, settled (`committed`) and still open to change (`tentative`). `finish` ends the recording and answers the whole text.
- **Limits.** Five minutes of audio per dictation, the engine's own; a frame over a second, or not a whole number of samples, fails the dictation. A client that sends nothing for 15 seconds while it records has gone, though its connection never said so, as a laptop put to sleep: the dictation ends and its socket closes, so the next can start. Its first piece may take up to two minutes, while a browser asks the operator for the microphone. The engine gives up on a stream after as long (**Read**, T3 Code v0.1.13, `apps/server/src/speech/stream.ts`). The wait for the model's download has no such limit, since the client records nothing then.

**In the cloud**, the server keeps the recording in memory until `finish`, then has the engine send it to the provider with the operator's key. The key is a secret of the operator's Secrets ([Secrets](#secrets)), a single value named `voice-openai`, `voice-xai` or `voice-openrouter` that no bot may use, saved and removed with `voice.setCloudKey`; Secrets shows it like any other. Only such a secret is a key: one the operator made under the same name, of another kind or open to a bot, is neither read nor changed, and saving a key then answers `secret-name-taken`. The server reads the key when the operator stops, so a key removed while they spoke sends nothing. It gives the key to the engine for that one recognition and removes it from the engine as soon as it ends, whatever the outcome. The engine keeps it meanwhile in its secret store, a file of mode 0600 inside the private home, as it keeps the provider connection's key ([Security](security.md)). Each time the engine starts, once no dictation runs, the server removes any key the engine still holds, as one a server stopped mid-recognition would leave. OpenRouter needs a model chosen (`Settings.voice.openRouterModel`), from those it lists as transcribing without keeping data (`voice.cloudModels`); without one the dictation fails `cloud-model` before it records.

**The AI's tidy-up**, when the operator turned it on (`cleanUp`, off by default), runs once the words are in: the engine's text generation, with the words to spell right and the draft around them. The client is told `tidying`; when it fails, the words as heard land instead.

**What a client offers.** A server whose settings carry `voice` serves the voice socket and the `voice.*` RPCs; an older one carries none, and a client then offers only what its own device recognizes ([Clients](clients.md)). `voice.subscribe` says whether the server can recognize speech now: `unavailable` with `platform` when the engine has no speech models for its system (a cloud provider still works), or `engine` while the engine is not running.

## Client API

Clients use one WebSocket to the server, with typed requests and subscriptions defined in `packages/contracts`, plus a few HTTP routes for what a socket does badly (pairing, file transfer, the [diagnostics zip](#diagnostics)), and the media socket that shows the bot's browser ([The bot's browser](#the-bots-browser)).

Each of the server's listeners (this one, the tool endpoint and the admin channel) binds only once its routes are built, so a request that arrives the moment a port opens is answered, never dropped. Node accepts connections as soon as a server binds and answers a request only once a handler is attached; bound first, a listener would leave a request that arrived in between waiting forever. Between the bind and the attach, Effect resumes from Node's listen callback synchronously, with nothing that waits in between, so no request can arrive unhandled. A step a listener takes after binding that does wait, such as setting the admin socket's mode, runs once its handler is attached.

**Over plain HTTP, a request names the server by an address.** On a client listener without TLS, a request whose `Host` is not `localhost`, an IP address or the name in `publicOrigin` is answered `403` before it is routed, the socket's upgrade included; the port in it is not compared. A page on a name rebound to this machine's address would send that name ([Security: web pages](security.md#j3-bots-own-surface)). With TLS, the certificate settles which names a browser reaches the server at, and `Host` is not checked. `POST /api/pair` reads a body only when it is declared `application/json`, and answers any other type `415` without counting an attempt towards the pairing lock.

**The socket is authenticated once, at the upgrade.** A client sends its device token as an `Authorization: Bearer` header, or, where it cannot set a header on an upgrade (a browser), a ticket it got from `POST /api/socket-ticket` with that bearer. A ticket works once and only within a minute. An upgrade without a valid credential is answered 401 and never becomes a socket. A browser cannot read that status, so a failed upgrade sends it back to the ticket route, whose own 401 is the signal to pair again ([Clients](clients.md#connecting)). The socket belongs to that device for its whole life, and every request on it acts as that device. Revoking the device closes its sockets at once. This is how T3 Code's own socket works (**Read** at `bc0f04d`, `apps/server/src/ws.ts`).

**A failure stays with its request.** Each socket runs its own RPC server. A handler that fails unexpectedly fails its own request with a generic "Internal server error", and the cause is logged on the server; the socket and its other subscriptions carry on.

**Subscriptions** send the current state and then changes, as whole entities:

- The roster (every bot and room, with its state, a preview of its last entry and whether it is unread, and the operator's sections in their order) and the engine status are small, so they always start with a snapshot and do not resume. A change carries the sections only when they changed, all of them; a bot that moves into or out of one is sent as a changed bot, and deleting a section sends each bot it held.
- A conversation or room resumes. Its snapshot holds the latest entries and the change sequence it was read at, its watermark; every later change follows, and nothing at or before the watermark does. A client that reconnects passes the last sequence it applied and gets the changes since, or, when the server no longer has them or there are too many, a fresh snapshot that replaces what the client holds. A frame is bounded by size as well as by count, since a visual makes one entry up to half a megabyte: a snapshot or a page of history stops once its entries' stored bodies pass 8 MB (always holding at least one), and the rest comes as older history; a replay that would pass it is answered with the snapshot instead.
- Routines resume the same way.
- Teammate conversations travel beside the roster: `pairs.subscribe` lists the pairs, and `pairs.messages.subscribe` follows an open one. Neither resumes ([Teammate conversations](#teammate-conversations)).
- The steps of a work fold stream while the operator has the fold open: every step so far, then each step as it changes, until the fold's turn ends. Work the turn handed off (a background task it started) keeps adding its steps to the same fold, as part of the turn's work, with nothing on the fold saying it still runs: the operator reads them the next time they open it, and a client says a step is running, and marks it so, only while the fold's turn runs. A fold carries when its turn started, which can be before its first step (`turnStartedAt`), which ties it to the turn, and when and how the turn ended; it carries no time worked, and a client shows none. A turn stopped or failed before its first step still writes a fold, of no steps, as it ends, whether the engine reports the end or the server settles it for a session the engine lost, so its conversation says how it ended and when, also after a reload; one that completed leaves only its reply, and a routine's turn, whose steps are never shown, leaves none, nor does a quiet turn ([Reactions](#reactions)). An operator's answer to a card counts as its turn's activity when the card closes, so a turn that waited long on the operator is not stalled the moment it resumes.
- A step names a tool the operator can meet in J3 Bots' own words (`WorkTool`), which the engine adapter maps from the names the engine and the harnesses give their tools; a client words it in the operator's language. A harness's own file and search tools read as what they worked on, as the harness's other steps do. Its plan and its subagents, and a task the engine delegates, write no step: they are the turn's work (below). A tool it cannot name keeps the engine's title of the call, else its own name made readable, never an engine's id. A working bot's state and a roster preview of a work fold carry the step that says what the work does or did, and the preview how the fold's turn ended, which is all a fold of no steps has to say (a failed turn's notice, with its Try again, is written after its fold, so the roster previews the notice): one still running, else the newest that is not housekeeping (looking in on a background task), else the newest. A bot whose opening wake is still to come or under way says so (`Bot.opening`), since its state passes through idle before its first turn.
- A turn's plan and its little ones reach only the turn's audience ([A turn's plan and its little ones](#a-turns-plan-and-its-little-ones)): a private turn's on its work fold (`WorkFoldEntry.work`), which a conversation's snapshot and changes carry, and a room turn's on the room in the roster (`Room.memberWork`), by the member whose turn it is, while the room's work runs.

Unread has one source of truth: the conversation's read mark against its newest entry that a bot or J3 Bots wrote, other than a teammate message's card, which never calls for the operator; nor is such a card the roster's preview of the conversation, or its latest activity. A bot that finished its turn is idle, and its reply is unread until the operator reads it; there is no separate "done" state. A client moves the mark forward only through the latest entry, by order, of those the operator can see: on screen, in a window that is showing, with no dialog over the conversation. Marking a conversation or room unread (`entries.markUnread`) moves the mark back to just before the last entry a bot or J3 Bots wrote, other than a teammate message's card, never forward, and only from past it: that entry reads as new again, and the next read past it clears it, with nothing else stored. A target whose only entries are the operator's has nothing to read again, and stays as it is. It is a command, with a receipt that commits with the move: since it moves the mark back, a connection that sends it again after a read made since, on this device or another, must find it applied rather than undo that read. A client sends a read it already sent only once, until the roster says the target is unread again: then the next read goes out, so a conversation marked unread elsewhere is read again when the operator comes back to it.

**Commands** carry a client-generated id and are idempotent: send a message, answer a card, stop a bot or a room's work, create or change a bot, a room, a section or a routine, mark a conversation or room unread, edit memory, change settings, register this device for push or end that registration. `secret.provide` is the exception: it keeps no receipt, because a receipt would hold the value; a retry that finds the card closed fails with `Conflict("card-closed")` instead. `message.react` keeps none either: it sets the operator's reaction, so sending it again leaves it as it is.

**A room's past entries after it ends.** Archiving a room expires its open `ask` cards, so a client's stale answer to one fails with `Conflict("card-closed")`; a Retry of its message or notice fails with `Conflict("room-archived")` until the room is restored. Deleting a room deletes its entries, so a stale answer, and a Retry of anything that was in it, fails with `NotFound`.

The server pages history by `order`; a client asks for older entries as the operator scrolls.

## Release feed

The server learns of newer releases on its own, so a client can offer to update it (`UPDATE_HTTP_PATHS`, [Clients: updates](clients.md#updates-outside-the-protocol)). `ReleaseFeed` (`apps/server/src/releases/`) does it:

- **The channel** is a setting of the server, `Settings.updateChannel`: `stable` or `nightly`. It starts as the channel of the release that first runs with the setting (migration 23), so a home installed from a nightly build follows nightly builds, and one installed or updated from any other release follows stable releases. Stable reads GitHub's REST API `releases/latest`, the newest published release that is not a pre-release. Nightly reads `releases?per_page=30` and takes the newest release that is either a nightly build, a pre-release tagged with its bare version `<X.Y.Z>-nightly.<YYYYMMDD>.<N>`, or a stable release, tagged `v<X.Y.Z>`. Nightly tags carry no `v`, so they stay outside the `v*` tags only a maintainer may push. A draft, a release candidate, any other tag, a pre-release flag that contradicts the tag, or a release page that is not `https:` is passed over. A release's files are downloaded from the folder its own tag names. Versions order as semantic versioning does (`@j3-bots/release`, `compareVersions`), so a nightly sorts after the stable release it follows and before the next one.
- **Only newer, only verified.** The newest release on the channel is offered only when it sorts after the running version, never when it is the same or older: no downgrade. Before it is offered, the feed downloads its `SHA256SUMS` and `SHA256SUMS.sig` from the releases page and verifies them ([Security: updates](security.md#updates)). A release that fails is reported as `refused`, with why, and is not offered; a release that verified is not verified again, and one that was refused is verified again at each check, since its files can be fixed in place. A server on stable that runs a nightly, after its operator moved it from nightly, is offered nothing until a stable release newer than that nightly exists, and says it is waiting for one (`waitingForStable`).
- **When.** A minute after the server starts when no check is due later, then every 6 hours, give or take 30 minutes so servers started together do not ask together, and when the channel changes. A client may ask for a check. No check starts within a minute of the last, whatever asked for either and whether or not it finished, nor before a rate limit's reset: a check asked for then answers what the last one found, and a new channel is checked once one may start. Checks run one at a time; a check asked for while one runs waits for it.
- **Asking little.** No token: the API allows an address 60 requests an hour without one, shared by every machine behind it. A check is one request (two downloads more, from the releases page, only for a release not verified yet), so a server makes one every 6 hours on its own (four an hour at most while checks fail), and the checks clients ask for add at most one a minute. Each request carries the ETag of the last answer, which GitHub answers 304 when nothing changed, with nothing to decode. A 304 still counts against the limit without a token (**Verified** against `api.github.com`, 2026-11-24: `x-ratelimit-remaining` fell by one for each 304). A 403 or 429 is a rate limit: no check starts, for any reason, before `retry-after`, or `x-ratelimit-reset` when no requests are left, never more than 6 hours away whatever the header says. After a check that does not finish, the next scheduled one waits at least 15 minutes, doubled after each scheduled check that does not finish, up to 6 hours; a check a client asked for that does not finish leaves the wait as it was. What the last check found still stands meanwhile.
- **Where it asks.** Requests go only to the API's origin, the releases page's, and `release-assets.githubusercontent.com`, where the releases page redirects each download (**Verified** 2026-11-24). A redirect to any other host is refused before a request is sent there, and the check ends unreadable (`onlyToReleaseHosts`). A release's file is read as it streams and given up past 1 MiB, whatever its length said.
- **Its highlights.** A release's notes give up to five highlights, which the update status sends as `available.highlights` for the update prompt ([Clients: the update prompt](clients.md#the-update-prompt)): the bold lead of each top-level item under "What's new", with links and code marks removed and each cut to 160 characters (`highlightsOf` in `@j3-bots/release`, [the notes template](../releases/TEMPLATE.md)). Notes without that section give none. They are read from the answer the check already has, and kept with what it found; a release found before a server read them shows none until a newer one is out.
- **Kept across a restart.** What the feed found, the ETags, the rate limit's reset and the verdicts are written to `state/release-feed.json`, so a restart, an update's included, asks nothing before the next check is due. A verified verdict keeps its evidence, the `SHA256SUMS` and `SHA256SUMS.sig` it verified, byte for byte. At start the evidence is verified again against the keys trusted then, and a verdict whose evidence does not verify is set aside, so a file that says a release verified offers nothing on its word, nor does one verified with a mirror key no longer trusted. A file that cannot be read is a cache lost: the feed starts again from nothing.

`J3_RELEASE_FEED_URL` replaces the API (default `https://api.github.com/repos/JPSAUD501/j3-bots`), for tests and mirrors: it must answer `<base>/releases/latest` and `<base>/releases`. Downloads come from `J3_RELEASE_BASE_URL`, as `j3-bots update` and `install.sh` take them.

## Updating from a client

A client asks the server to update itself through `POST /api/update/v1/start`, with the version the status offers ([Clients: updates](clients.md#updates-outside-the-protocol)). The update is the same transition `j3-bots update` makes from a shell ([decision 0016](../decisions/0016-an-update-keeps-a-snapshot-to-roll-back-to.md)): the server only starts it, and reports what it records ([decision 0033](../decisions/0033-a-client-update-runs-in-a-transient-unit.md)). `UpdateRuns` (`apps/server/src/update/`) does it.

**Where it can run.** The update stops the service, so it cannot run in the server's process or as its child, whose cgroup ends with the service. It runs as a transient unit of the same systemd user manager, `j3-bots-update-<run>.service`, which `systemd-run --user --collect` starts. Only a server `install.sh` installed and its service runs can do that, on a manager that outlives the operator's sessions. Anything else answers `canUpdate: unable`, with a reason and the command that updates it by hand, checked in this order each time the status is read:

| Reason | When | Command |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `desktop-managed` | The desktop app runs the server (`--admin-fd`). The app updates it with itself, and its own surface reports that update ([Operating: the desktop app](../operations/operating.md#the-server-the-desktop-app-runs)). | none |
| `checkout` | A checkout runs it. | `git pull && pnpm install --frozen-lockfile && pnpm build && j3-bots service install` |
| `unpacked` | A release unpacked by hand, not under `install.sh`'s folder, or one whose manifest cannot be read. | the `install.sh` line |
| `started-by-hand` | The service's main process is not this server: `j3-bots serve` in a shell. | `j3-bots update` |
| `no-systemd` | `systemd-run` is not on the service's `PATH`. | `j3-bots update` |
| `no-linger` | The user does not linger, so the manager, and the update with it, would end with the operator's last session. | `sudo loginctl enable-linger "$(id -un)"` |
| `rollback-unfinished` | A rollback was cut off ([Operating](../operations/operating.md#update-and-roll-back)). | `j3-bots update --rollback` |

**What starts it**, in this order, each refusal an `UpdateRefused` with nothing changed:

1. `unable`, as above.
2. `not-available` unless the version is exactly the feed's `available` one: verified, newer, on the channel. Right after a channel change, until the feed has heard of it, its offer is the old channel's and is not taken. A change of the channel, through this API or `settings.update`, and a start never interleave: a start reads the offer and records its run with the channel held steady, so a change commits before the offer is read or after the run is recorded. No request names a file or an address.
3. `busy` while a run has not ended, or while the home's update lock is held, as by `j3-bots update` or `--rollback` from a shell. Steps 3 to 5 run under one lock, so two clients cannot both start.
4. `work-running`, with the number of bots, while bots have work running and the client did not send `stopWork`. The update asks again when it is ready to stop the service, and takes the client's `stopWork` as the answer: work begun meanwhile is stopped only if the client sent it, and otherwise the run ends as `rolled-back`, having changed nothing. When that last count finds no work, none begins before the stop: the server holds every new first send from the count until it stops (`POST /running-work`).
5. Otherwise the server writes the run record and starts the unit. A unit that does not start ends the run as `rolled-back` with `other` and `systemd-run`'s output: nothing was changed.

**The unit.** It runs the release that runs now, `<install>/versions/<current>/bin/j3-bots update --version <V> --run <run>`, as the same user, with no privilege the user does not have (`NoNewPrivileges=yes`). Its environment is the service's: `J3_HOME`, `PATH`, and `J3_RELEASE_BASE_URL` and `J3_RELEASE_PUBLIC_KEY` when the service has them, so it downloads from where the feed found the release. It also unsets the variables a shell may set to override the configuration file, as the service's unit does. systemd expands `$NAME` in a transient unit's arguments, as in `ExecStart=`, but takes the program's path as it is, and expands no `%` specifier there (**Verified** with systemd 255 on a test machine: in a `systemd-run --user` command, an argument's `$HOME` was expanded and `$$` gave one `$`; a path with `$HOME` in it ran, and the same path with `$$` was not found; `%h` was not expanded). So the path is passed as it is, and the arguments, a version and a run id, have no `$`. The service's own unit doubles each `$` in its arguments and none in Node's path. The update downloads `SHA256SUMS` and its signature again and verifies them itself before it reads a digest, whatever the feed found ([Security: updates](security.md#updates)). A release already in `versions/` is taken only when it is proven to be the signed one: each release an update unpacks keeps the SHA-256 of its archive, which must be the verified list's; one without it, as from a mirror or an install that verified nothing, is downloaded again and replaces it. Once it holds the update lock, the update checks that the release it was asked from still runs; if another update ran first, it changes nothing and says so, so a delayed run never takes the server back to an older release. It is collected when it ends, failed or not; what it printed stays in `journalctl --user -u j3-bots-update-<run>`.

**The record.** `<home>/state/update.json`, readable by the user alone, holds the run: its id, the release it came from and goes to, the device that asked and whether it let the update stop bots' work, when, its stage, and once it ends, how. The server writes it before it starts the unit. From then on only the update writes it: `downloading`, with bytes so far and the total once known, at most once a second; `verifying`; `installing`; `restarting`; then the outcome. Each write replaces the file whole and flushes it, and an update writes only a run that has its id and has not ended. Since the record is in the home, it outlives the restart: whichever release runs afterwards reads it.

**How it ended**, as the status' `lastResult`, the one entry every client sees for it (an update belongs to no bot, so it is no conversation's entry):

- `done`: the new release runs.
- `rolled-back`, with a reason: either the new release did not get ready and the release before runs again on the data as it was (`health-check`), or the update stopped before it changed anything (`download`, `verification`, `engine`, `disk`, `other`). Either way the release before runs.
- `failed`: bringing the release before back failed too, and `j3-bots update --rollback` finishes it; or the unit ended without recording how, as when the machine restarted under it (`interrupted`). A run whose unit has ended while it has not is ended that way the next time the status is read. The detail of any ending but `done` names the unit's journal, where what the update printed is.

While a run has not ended, the status' `state` is its stage and `run` names it, so a client follows it by polling `status`. A stream would end with the restart, and a client already polls `GET /api/server` while the server is down. The server logs the run, the versions and the device when it starts one, and how it ended when it finds one cut off, never a token.

## Diagnostics

A diagnostics zip says how the server runs, for whoever helps the operator, and holds nothing of the person ([decision 0100](../decisions/0100-a-diagnostics-zip-holds-what-the-server-knows-and-nothing-of-the-person.md)). The files it holds, each explained in its own `README.md`, are listed in the decision record. Two ways make it, and both make the same zip (`apps/server/src/diagnostics/build.ts`):

- **`POST /api/diagnostics/v1`** (`DIAGNOSTICS_HTTP_PATH`).
  - It takes the device token as a bearer, and a JSON body naming the client that asks (`DiagnosticsRequest`).
  - It answers `415` for any type but JSON, before anything else. Without a valid token it answers `401`; for a body over 4 KiB, `413`; for one that does not decode, `400`.
  - Otherwise it streams the zip as `application/zip`, with a file name of `diagnosticsFileName` and `cache-control: no-store`.
  - Any paired device may ask, as for every route: a paired device holds the operator's authority.
  - The server names the route's version in `ServerInfo.diagnostics`.
- **`j3-bots diagnostics`** opens the database read-only, and makes its file with mode 0600, refusing anything already at its name, a link included. It removes the file again when no whole zip was written. Over the admin channel, it asks a running server for the engine's state and when the server started ([Operating: diagnostics](../operations/operating.md#diagnostics)). Only the running server knows what in its environment keeps usage data off, so the command's zip says it does not know.

**Where the logs come from.**

- **The server's log.** The server keeps the same JSON lines it writes to the console in `logs/server.log`, batched once a second, so a zip has them however the server runs (`ServerLogFile`). It is private state: each write checks the folder with `Layout.writable` and opens the file so that a link at its name is refused (`writeInPlace`), and a write that fails is dropped, never stopping the server. Only a line that decodes as the server's own log shape enters the zip, re-encoded without its fiber, scrubbed, its cause then cut at 8 KiB. A line that names something of the operator's network names it in an annotation the zip leaves out (`logs/annotations.ts`): the name a refused plain-HTTP request asked for is `refusedName`, and the name `publicOrigin` serves the server at, which the line gives beside it, is `publicName`: the scrubber takes out the name set now, and the annotation keeps an earlier one out of a line written before it changed.
- **The engine's log.** It comes from the engine's trace file, through the adapter ([Engine: what J3 Bots uses](engine.md#what-j3-bots-uses-and-what-it-leaves-alone)).

Each log is read newest first, file by file, and stops at the first file older than 7 days. It keeps the newest lines that fit its budget: 14 MiB for the server's and 6 MiB for the engine's. The zip is at most 24 MiB (`MAX_DIAGNOSTICS_SIZE`).

**The zip is made as it is sent.**

- Each file is made in turn, scrubbed, deflated and passed on before the next is read.
- At most one log's kept lines are in memory at a time.
- A file that cannot be made says so: `{ "unreadable": … }`, with why. The other files are still made.
- A failure to read the secret values the scrubber needs stops the zip instead, since without them a secret could pass: a query that fails, or a secret store, the engine's included, that is there and cannot be read.

**One scrubber** (`scrub.ts`) goes over every file's text before it enters the zip: a log line by line, a JSON file value by value (`scrubJson`), so it still parses whatever a secret looks like, and every text whole before it is cut. It replaces:

- the values the server holds as secrets, read fresh for each zip: the vault's, the webhooks', the push registrations', the bridge key, and the engine's, as the adapter lists them; of a record kept as a JSON document, such as a connector's sign-in, only the values typed as secret;
- credentials, and anything shaped like a token;
- email addresses;
- the home folder, the OS user name, the host name and the name in `publicOrigin`;
- IP addresses other than loopback.

Ids stay, so a flow can be followed across files.

## J3 Bots' own harnesses

When the engine finds neither Claude Code nor Codex where the server runs, the server installs its own copy of each, starting as it starts, and it keeps the copies it installed up to date ([decision 0045](../decisions/0045-j3-bots-keeps-its-own-copies-of-the-harnesses.md), [decision 0064](../decisions/0064-the-harnesses-install-as-the-server-starts-and-fail-with-the-true-reason.md); what keeps it safe is in [Security](security.md#j3-bots-own-copies)). A harness the operator installed is theirs: the server never installs beside it, and its update is the engine's ([decision 0044](../decisions/0044-j3-bots-installs-and-updates-the-harnesses.md)).

**Which harness a provider runs.** At start, before the engine is created, the server reads each `harnesses/<provider>/current.json`; a copy whose executable is there is the one the provider runs (`binaryPath`, [Engine](engine.md#the-adapters-interface)). Without one, the engine looks on its `PATH`, as before. `j3-bots doctor`, the sign-in buttons and `j3-bots provider` look the same way. Each Claude Code and Codex provider's status carries `copy`: where its harness comes from (`j3`, `operator` or `none`), the version of J3 Bots' copy, the newest version the maker listed at the last check, whether J3 Bots has a build of it for the server's system and processor (`installable`), an install or update under way (`task`), and how the last one ended (`last`, kept in `state.json`). A provider on J3 Bots' copy carries no engine `version` or `update`, and `engine.updateProvider` refuses it, so the engine never runs the harness's own updater on it. The engine's status carries the same `copy` of each harness in `harnesses`, from the server's start, before the engine runs and reports any provider, so a client can follow the harnesses' downloads beside the engine's own (`preparation` in `packages/client-runtime` reads them, with the engine's, as one list).

**As the server starts.** Each harness with no copy of J3 Bots', none of the operator's on the `PATH`, a build for the server's system, and whose last install was not cancelled, starts installing at once, as if a client had asked, whether or not `autoUpdateHarnesses` is on: that setting is about updates. Both run beside the engine's download, each in its own background task. The check a minute later starts one the same way, so a harness the operator removed from the `PATH` meanwhile is installed then. A cancelled install stays so, across starts, until a client asks again.

**Install and update are one request.** `provider.installHarness` installs the newest version the maker lists, for a provider whose harness is not the operator's, when J3 Bots has a build of it for the server; it answers once the install has started, and from then on it reads `downloading`, with Cancel, and how it goes follows on `engine.subscribe`. `provider.cancelHarnessInstall` stops it. A second request while one runs is taken as the same. Each runs in order:

1. **The listing.** The maker's channel and the version's checksums, read over HTTPS from the maker's origin, following no redirect, at most 2 MiB each and 30 seconds to the last byte.
2. **The download** (`downloading`, with how far it has come, at most once a second) into `<v>.partial`, resumed with a `Range` request when an earlier one was cut off; an answer that is not the rest of it starts it over.
3. **The check** (`verifying`): its SHA-256, and its size where the maker gives one, against the listing; a mismatch removes the download. Then it is unpacked into `<v>.staging/`, moved to `versions/<v>/`, and only there run once with `--version`, as the engine would run it; it must say the version listed. A copy that does not is removed. It runs after the move, not before: Windows refuses to move a folder while another program holds a file in it, and a new executable is held for a moment after its first run, by a scanner or by its own process as it ends.
4. **The switch** (`waiting` while any bot has a turn or background work running in a session on that provider, or the engine is not ready). The checked copy waits in `versions/<v>/`; every 30 seconds the server looks again. Once no bot's work runs there, with new first sends held (`SendPause`) for an update (a first install holds none: no bot's turn could run on a provider with no harness, and a bot on another one answers meanwhile), it looks last at whether the copy is still wanted: a first install whose harness the operator installed on the `PATH` meanwhile is given up, its copy removed, and so is an update the check started, when no client asked for it and `autoUpdateHarnesses` was turned off. Then the engine is given the new executable (`setHarnessExecutable`), which answers once the engine's check of the rebuilt instance shows the new version running without an error, within 90 seconds; `current.json` is replaced whole, and the copy recorded, at once. From the moment the switch begins, a cancel no longer stops the install: it finishes, and one that made its copy current ends recorded as installed. The server's stop does stop it, at once, without waiting for the engine: the next start provisions the engine with whichever copy `current.json` names. Still with first sends held, the server has the engine release each of its sessions on that provider (`configureSession` with `reloadTools`), since a session the engine has open keeps running the copy it started on; the next turn of each starts the new copy. The old version's folder is removed once every session was released, else once the engine starts again. If the engine may have taken the new executable without showing it runs it, or `current.json` cannot be written, the engine is given the old one back and the install fails (`engine`, or for `current.json` the reason its file failure comes down to, below); if that cannot be shown either, the new copy is kept on disk, and the next install of that version reuses it, until the engine starts again.

A failure names its reason in `last`, and the provider offers to try again: `unreachable`, `checksum`, `check`, `engine`, or, for a file the server could not write, move or remove, what the system's code comes down to: `storage` only for a full disk or quota (`ENOSPC`, `EDQUOT`), `in-use` for a file another program held, `permission` where the system does not let the server write, `outside-home` for a location that leads out of the home, and `files` for any other code. The log keeps each with the code, the file and the call, as a warning that starts "A file of a harness's copy could not be written". On Windows a rename or a removal refused with `EBUSY`, `EPERM` or `EACCES`, as for a held file, is first tried again, from 50 ms, doubling up to a second apart, for 30 seconds at most. A Codex package the system's `tar` could not unpack is `files`, with `tar`'s own words on the server's stderr. A cancel removes what was downloaded; a stop of the server keeps a partial download, which the next install of that version resumes.

**Updates by themselves.** For each harness with J3 Bots' own copy, the server reads the maker's newest version a minute after it starts, then about every six hours, up to half an hour later so the checks spread out, and an hour after a check that failed. With `autoUpdateHarnesses` on, as it is unless the operator turns it off in Settings, a newer version is installed as above, and so waits for the provider's bots like any update. Turning it on checks at once. A harness on the operator's own `PATH`, or one J3 Bots has no build of for the server, is never checked.

## Moving to another server

`j3-bots export` writes a home's bots to one file, and `j3-bots import` puts them in another home, on this machine or another, on Linux or Windows ([decision 0062](../decisions/0062-a-home-moves-as-an-export.md); the commands are in [Operating](../operations/operating.md#move-to-another-server)). The desktop app runs the same `export` on the bots it runs, from Settings ([Clients: Desktop](clients.md#desktop)). Both run with no server on the home. Each holds `server.lock` while it works, so neither starts while a server or an update holds it, and no server starts while either runs.

**The archive.** It is a gzipped tar that the server writes and reads in its own code, so it needs no system `tar`. Its first file is `manifest.json`, which holds:

- the format (`j3-bots-export-1`);
- the release that wrote it and that release's protocol;
- the database's last migration, when it was written, and how many bots it holds;
- what it holds of the secrets;
- every file after it, in order, with its size and SHA-256.

Then come these files, each named by its path in the home:

- `j3.sqlite`: a copy of the database as it stood (`VACUUM INTO`), with no paired device, push registration or push delivery, since every device pairs with the new server, no webhook secret, since each is a credential, and no command receipt, since each answers a device's retry on this server, and a webhook routine's holds its address with the secret.
- `vault/vault.sqlite`: the secrets, sealed again (below), when there are any.
- `state/shipped-skills.json`.
- Each bot's `memory/`, `skills/` and `files/`, and the shared `memory/` and `skills/`. These hold regular files only, each with whether its owner may run it, as a skill's script. A link inside them is left out, and so is a folder that is a link or leads out of the home; the export counts each one it leaves out. A file whose name has a `\` in it, which no import takes, is left out too, and the export names it.
- Each attachment the database names. One whose bytes are missing is left out and counted.

The archive does not hold:

- the configuration;
- the engine's folder: its database, its credential, the provider key;
- the providers' homes and their sign-ins;
- J3 Bots' own copies of the harnesses;
- the update snapshots;
- the secrets' key file.

What the server renders into a workspace, its instructions and `.j3/`, is rendered again on the new server. The import gives each webhook routine a new secret, so its address is new: the new server's origin and the new secret.

**Writing the file.** The export first takes the file's name, as an empty file made new, mode 0600: something already at that path is refused and left as it is, and of two exports to one name the second is refused before it asks anything. The archive is written in a folder beside it, made private first (mode 0700, or on Windows an access list for this user, SYSTEM and Administrators alone, which the file takes), and renamed over the name once whole, so it is never half an archive, nor one another account may open. A file that changes between being measured and being written fails the export, and nothing is left behind.

**The secrets.** The key file never leaves its home.

- **The export.** The export copies the secrets' database. In the copy it opens every value under the home's key and seals it again under a key that scrypt derives from a passphrase of the export's own: N 2^17, r 8 and p 1 (128 MiB), with a fresh 16-byte salt. It seals with AES-256-GCM, with the record id and field name as associated data, as the secrets are sealed at home. The key check is sealed again too, so the import tells a wrong passphrase. The manifest records the secrets' last migration and the scrypt settings.
- **The passphrase.** It is typed twice and has at least eight characters. The command says it cannot be recovered: without it, the secrets in the export are lost. A program gives it as the first line of stdin (`--passphrase-stdin`), which is read up to its end only.
- **Secrets this release cannot read** are left out, and the export says why: a key file that is missing or holds another key, or secrets a newer release changed. A vault from before secrets that was never opened stops the export instead, with the line the server logs for it.
- **The import.** The import asks for the passphrase, three times at most at a terminal. In one transaction of the copy it seals every value again under the key of the home it lands in. When that home has no key yet, a new one goes into place with the secrets.
- **Redaction.** No value is in the archive or the manifest unsealed, or in a log or an error: those name a record and a field only. A test reads every file of an export, and every log line of the export, the import and the first start after it, for a value.

**The import.**

1. **The manifest is read first.** An archive in a format this release does not read is refused. So is one a newer release wrote, by its release or by a migration of its database or secrets this release does not know: this server is updated first. An older one is migrated. Each name must be one of the files above, listed once, and a name this system can keep: on Windows, no reserved name or character, and on Windows and macOS, no two names that differ only in case. The archive's reader takes regular files only, with one extended header at most before each, of 64 KiB at most.
2. **The home must hold nothing the import would delete.** A home that holds a bot, a secret, or a file of the bots' folders, the shared memory or the attachments (as a deleted bot's files that were kept) is refused unless the import is given `--replace`, which deletes them. So is one whose shared skills hold anything but this release's own copies of the skills it ships, and one where a link or a file stands in for the bots' folder, the shared memory or skills, or the attachments, whatever it leads to now. The home's `state/`, `vault/` and `shared/` must be folders, not links, so nothing the import writes or deletes is outside the home.
3. **It unpacks into the home.** It needs free room for every file and 256 MiB more. The archive is unpacked into `state/import/`, each file written whole, flushed to the disk and checked against the manifest's size and SHA-256, then each folder made for them flushed. A file the manifest does not list, out of its order or of another size, is refused.
4. **It readies the copy.** The secrets are sealed again. The database is migrated and checked: a bot whose id is not a plain folder name, or an attachment kept anywhere but under its digest in `attachments/`, refuses the archive, since each becomes a path in the home. Then it is pointed at this home, in one transaction:
   - each bot's workspace path is set here;
   - its real path (`workspace_root`) and its engine project are cleared, and set again when it is provisioned;
   - each webhook routine gets a new secret;
   - no paired device remains;
   - the engine instance is recorded as one this home does not have.
5. **It puts the copy in place.** It writes the mark `state/import-underway`, which names the archive by its manifest's SHA-256, and puts each entry in place of the home's: the database (with its log files removed), the secrets' database, the key when it is new, `state/shipped-skills.json`, `bots/`, the shared `memory/` and `skills/`, and `attachments/`. It flushes each folder it changed, the home's last, then removes the mark.
   - A failure before the mark leaves the home as it was.
   - While the mark is there, the server refuses to start and `export` refuses the home. The import of the same archive, run again, finishes it, with or without `--replace`. Another archive goes in its place only with `--replace`.

The home it lands in keeps its configuration, its engine's folder and its providers' sign-ins. On the first start the server finds that its engine is not the one the sessions belong to ([Another engine](#another-engine)). Every session is lost, one still to open included, and each bot is told once. Each bot then opens a new session in a new project, with its conversation, memory and files as they were.

## Usage data

The server sends anonymous usage data to J3 Bots' PostHog project while its environment allows it and the operator's switch is on, which it is by default ([decision 0099](../decisions/0099-usage-data-is-the-servers-typed-and-on-until-turned-off.md); what people are told is in [Usage data](../product/usage-data.md)). The code is in `apps/server/src/telemetry/`; every event is declared in `packages/contracts/src/telemetry.ts`.

- **Typed.** An event is recorded as a `TelemetryEvent` and leaves as a `SentTelemetryEvent`, encoded against the contract with excess properties refused, so a property no event declares makes the event fail to leave rather than leave with it. Its values are closed literal sets, booleans, bucketed counts (`CountBucket`), whole seconds and release versions. A model is the engine's catalog id, found in the bot's harness's catalog in the engine status, not marked as one someone added, and of the shape Anthropic or OpenAI publish, with versions of one or two digits and a date as `20YYMMDD` (Claude Code lists a gateway's models as its own, **Read** at `bc0f04d`, `claudeReportedModels`), or else `custom`; an effort the engine does not name is `other`; a typed RPC failure is its tag and code (`request.failed`, recorded by the socket's defect guard), never its message. Every event carries the install's id hash as `distinct_id`, the J3 Bots and engine versions, the system and processor families, how the server runs (`service`, `desktop`, `manual`, `development`), `$process_person_profile: false` and `$geoip_disable: true`.
- **Where events come from.** The domain records most of them as what they describe is kept, after its transaction commits: a bot or a room made, a message sent, a turn's end as the observer closes it, a routine's run, a connector added or removed. A report loop records `server.started` and an update the server sees on starting (`update.applied`, from the release it ran last), and every ten minutes an update a client started that ended rolled back or failed, and once a UTC day `install.active` with the counts of bots, active rooms, enabled routines and connectors. What it already told (the day, the release, the update run) is kept in the database, in `telemetry_install` (migration 0065), so a restart does not tell it again.
- **Clients.** A client calls `telemetry.hello` once its socket opens with what it is (`TelemetryClient`: surface, release, system family and the hash of its own random id), but only against a server whose settings carry `telemetry`. The handlers keep it for that socket and attach it to the events that socket's requests cause (`surface`, `clientVersion`, `clientOs`, `clientDevice`). The first hello of a device in a UTC day records `client.connected`, counted in memory, so after a restart the device's first socket that day counts again. `telemetry.record` takes the events only a client sees (`ClientTelemetryEvent`: `connector-app.shown`, `feature.used`). While usage data is off, the server drops those events and records no `client.connected`; what a hello said stays with its socket, in memory, and leaves only with an event that socket's requests cause once usage data is on. A change of the settings is kept and applied together, even when its request is cancelled, so a switch turned off takes effect.
- **Delivery.** What the server says of itself is read once there is something to send, and its install kind within five seconds or `manual`, so nothing of it is on the start's way. Events wait in memory, at most 1,000, the oldest dropped past that. Every ten seconds up to 50 go to `<host>/batch/` in one request, each with its own UUID, with a ten-second timeout. A failed batch is sent again before anything newer, after an exponential backoff with jitter from two seconds to five minutes, and dropped after six tries, or at once on `400`, `401`, `403` or `413`. A batch on its way as the switch goes off is never tried again, and nothing is taken to be sent while the server's own details are read. The answer is read to its end within the timeout, whatever its status. The server's stop waits at most a second to send what waits. Sending never fails or holds anything else. J3 Bots never calls PostHog's management API.
- **What was sent.** Each batch that went is kept, every event as the JSON text that left, in `telemetry_sent`, the newest 100 (`telemetry.sent`, which every client shows as "See what is sent", and the diagnostics zip holds as `usage-data.json`, both read by `sentBodies`).
- **On and off.** `TelemetrySwitch` combines the environment's rules, read once at start, with the operator's switch in the settings (`settings.update` with `telemetry`), which takes effect at once and drops what waits when it goes off. `Settings.telemetry` tells clients whether the server offers usage data (`enabled`, and `heldOff` while the environment keeps it off whatever the switch says). The environment's rules, first match wins:
  1. `DO_NOT_TRACK` set to a yes keeps it off.
  2. `J3_TELEMETRY_ENABLED` set to anything but a yes keeps it off; a value that is neither a yes nor a no is logged as a warning.
  3. A `J3_TELEMETRY_HOST` that is not an `http` or `https` address, or an empty `J3_TELEMETRY_KEY`, keeps it off.
  4. `J3_TELEMETRY_ENABLED` set to a yes allows it.
  5. A CI runner (`CI` and the variables CI services set, T3 Code's list) or a source checkout keeps it off.
  6. Otherwise it is allowed.

  Yes and no are read as T3 Code reads them: `true`, `yes`, `on`, `1`, `y` and `false`, `no`, `off`, `0`, `n`, in any case. The rules follow T3 Code's at `bc0f04d` (`AnalyticsService.ts`, `otelEnvironment.ts`). A server that keeps it off logs why once as it starts.
- **The engine's own.** T3 Code's analytics and tracing are always off ([Engine](engine.md)).

## Configuration

The server reads its settings from `<J3_HOME>/config.json`. On first start, once the configuration and the home pass their checks, it writes that file whole, with every setting at its default but `scriptedAgent`, which is for test installs; it never replaces one that exists. A setting in the environment overrides the file, and a default applies only when neither has the setting. A setting that is present but invalid stops the start, so a typo never becomes the default. The file refuses unknown keys for the same reason. A port another program holds stops the start with a message that names the port and its setting. Start also refuses two listeners on one port, port `0` for the tool endpoint or the engine (both are dialed at the number configured), and a certificate and key that Node cannot load as a pair. `j3-bots doctor` runs the same checks without starting anything. A service runs with the file alone: it does not get the variables of the shell that installed it.

| File | Variable | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| | `J3_HOME` | `~/.j3-bots` | Created readable by the operator only. The server does not start while it is owned by another user or open to a group or to other users. Refused inside a Git checkout, as git decides it (an empty `.git` folder is none), including a folder that does not exist yet whose parent is one |
| `host` | `J3_HOST` | `lan` | `lan` listens on every IPv4 interface and serves private addresses only, with or without TLS. Start refuses it while any of the machine's addresses is public, and each connection to an address that is not loopback or private is closed unread. Any other value is one address, which must exist when the server starts. Without TLS it must be an IP literal in loopback or a private range: the private IPv4 ranges, IPv4 link-local, carrier-grade NAT (100.64.0.0/10, where Tailscale puts its addresses), and IPv6 unique-local and link-local. `0.0.0.0`, `::` and host names, `localhost` included, are refused |
| `port` | `J3_PORT` | `3737` | `0` lets the system choose; `j3-bots pair` prints the port the server got |
| `tls` | `J3_TLS_CERT`, `J3_TLS_KEY` | none | A certificate and key (`{ "cert", "key" }` in the file), which serve HTTPS. With them, `host` may be any address, public ones included; `lan` still serves private addresses only. The two variables are set together and replace the file's pair |
| `enginePort` | `J3_ENGINE_PORT` | `3738` | The engine's loopback port |
| | `J3_ENGINE_BINARY` | the pinned release | For testing an engine build. Not in the file, and a service never uses it. The release that runs is neither checked against the home's data nor recorded ([An older engine](#an-older-engine)) |
| `toolPort` | `J3_TOOL_PORT` | `3739` | The bot tool endpoint's loopback port, which the `j3` bridges call |
| `webClient` | `J3_WEB_CLIENT` | the one that comes with J3 Bots: `web/` in a release, `apps/web/dist` in a checkout ([decision 0015](../decisions/0015-a-release-is-a-self-contained-archive.md)). The server runtime a desktop package carries has none: the app loads its own page and calls only the server's API, which is all that server serves | The built web client, served on the API's origin. A folder named here must hold an `index.html`. Everything in it is public, so it may not overlap private state in either direction, symbolic links resolved: it may not hold the home, the TLS certificate or key, or anything the home keeps private, nor lie inside any of them. The one place inside the home it may be is `<J3_HOME>/web`. The default is served once it is built |
| `scriptedAgent` | `J3_SCRIPTED_AGENT` | none | The scripted ACP agent of a source checkout (`packages/engine-t3/scripted-agent/agent.ts`), offered as a provider named `scripted` and listed first, so new bots run on it: turns with no model, for development and for test installs such as the [upgrade check](../operations/releasing.md#the-upgrade-check). Never for real use: release archives do not carry the agent, which runs only beside a checkout's installed dependencies; start refuses a path that is not a file; and `j3-bots doctor` warns while it is set. A new `config.json` does not list it. The variable is for development, and a service never gets it |
| | `J3_TELEMETRY_ENABLED` | on | `false` turns usage data off; `true` also turns it on for a source checkout or a CI runner ([Usage data](#usage-data)). Not in the file: the switch in Settings is the operator's |
| | `J3_TELEMETRY_HOST`, `J3_TELEMETRY_KEY` | J3 Bots' PostHog project | Where usage data goes, for a fork |
| | `DO_NOT_TRACK` | unset | Set to a yes, keeps usage data off whatever else says |
| `publicOrigin` | | none | The `https` origin other machines reach the server at when it is not where it listens, such as a domain a proxy or a tunnel serves: `https://bots.example.com`, with no path, not even a trailing `/`, written in lower case without a default port. A routine's `webhookUrl` names it, `j3-bots pair` lists it first, and over plain HTTP the server answers requests whose `Host` is its name, as a proxy that keeps the client's `Host` sends them. It changes nothing about where the server listens. Not taken from the environment |

Relative paths in the file resolve against `J3_HOME`.

**The web client** is served by the same listener as the API, so the two share an origin. The server looks for a built client when it starts and registers its routes then; a client built later is served after a restart. Paths under `/api/` and the server's own routes are never answered with the client. A request for a path without a file extension that accepts HTML, and that names no file, gets `index.html`, for the client's own routing; anything else that names no file is a 404. Files under `/assets/` carry content hashes in their names and are cached for a year as immutable; everything else is revalidated on each use. Each request is resolved once: the request target is parsed as a URL (which drops the query and the fragment and resolves dot segments), its path is decoded and resolved inside the folder, and the file's real path, symbolic links resolved, is checked to lie inside the folder and is the file sent. No second parser reads the target. Paths that leave the folder are refused, and so is a file whose real path lies outside it, a folder's `index.html` and the app's `index.html` included. A folder named without its trailing slash is redirected to it.

**One server per home.** Right after it loads its configuration, before it opens the database or starts the engine, the server checks that the home is private and takes an exclusive lock on `<J3_HOME>/server.lock`, which it holds until it exits. A second server for the same home stops there, with an ordinary failure rather than a refusal (exit status 78): the server that holds it may be one still stopping, or an update copying the databases, so the desktop app starts it again after its pause, as after a crash, and it passes once the holder lets go. The lock is SQLite's, which the kernel releases when the process ends, so a crashed server never leaves the home held.

## The admin channel

The admin channel is how `j3-bots` commands on the host reach the running server, and how the desktop app that runs its own server gets its device and stops it. It needs no token, because only the server's own user can reach it, and nothing on the network does. It serves the same routes two ways:

- **The admin socket**, `<J3_HOME>/admin.sock`: a Unix socket that only the operator's user can open. Two things keep others out: the home is private, and the socket is created with no access for group or others, since the server narrows the umask for the call that binds it, so there is no moment between the bind and a `chmod` in which it has the process's usual mode. Node binds the path within that call (**Read** in Node's `lib/net.js`, `createServerHandle`, v24.x at `221ffa5`). The umask is the whole process's, so a file Node's thread pool happens to create in that instant gets no access for group or others either, which in a private home changes nothing. Just before the bind the server checks that the home is private and notes which folder it is (device and inode), and once the socket is served it checks again: a home moved, replaced or opened to others in between stops the start. A server holding the lock removes a socket left by one that stopped without closing, once it has seen that the file is a socket and that nothing listens on it. Anything else at that path stops the start, and so does a home whose path is too long for a socket's address (103 bytes for `admin.sock`'s). The server binds no admin socket on Windows, where Node would serve the path as a named pipe, which other users of the machine could open (**Assumed** from Node's and Windows' documentation, not measured); there the path's length is not checked either.
- **An inherited pipe**, with `j3-bots serve --admin-fd <n>`: the descriptor `n` the server inherited from the process that started it, which the desktop app passes as 3. Only that process holds the other end, and the server's end cannot be opened by name: on Linux and macOS Node makes it a socket pair (**Read** in libuv's `src/unix/process.c`, v1.x at `f08538c`), which has no name. On Windows libuv makes it a named pipe of a single instance (`FILE_FLAG_FIRST_PIPE_INSTANCE`), which the parent opens itself right after creating it, before the child starts (**Read** in `src/win/pipe.c`, `uv__create_pipe_pair`, same commit). The processes the server starts, the engine and the harnesses, do not inherit it. On Windows a child gets every handle its parent may pass on: the libuv of the Node the server pins, 1.52.1, names no list of them (`src/win/process.c`, **Read** in Node v26.13.1's `deps/uv`; libuv's v1.x has since named one, `PROC_THREAD_ATTRIBUTE_HANDLE_LIST`). What keeps fd 3 out is that Node, as it starts, makes every descriptor it inherited one it does not pass on, as it does its standard handles (`uv_disable_stdio_inheritance`, called from `src/node.cc` and defined in `deps/uv/src/win/process-stdio.c`, **Read** in v26.13.1). That they do not is **Verified** in CI with that Node, with a server started with `--admin-fd 3` and a stand-in engine that starts a harness of its own. On Linux it is killed, and the parent's end of the pipe closes while the engine and its harness still run, which it would not if either held the pipe. On Windows Server 2025 the engine ends with the server, since Node puts every child it does not detach in a job that ends with it (**Read** in v26.13.1's `deps/uv/src/win/process.c`), so the first process the server starts with the engine's binary, its CLI minting the engine's credential, compares its file objects, as kernel objects (`CompareObjectHandles`), with every handle of the server and of the process that started the server. Handles that process may pass on reach it through the server, and are both processes'; fd 3 is the server's alone, as its parent closed its copy of the child's end once the server started. None of the stand-in's files is the server's alone. It asks no file anything: a query on a synchronous file object can wait in the kernel for as long as another process's I/O holds it, and a process with a thread waiting there does not end when it is killed. On Windows it is the only admin channel.

  The start fails on a descriptor below 3, on a TCP socket, listening or connected, and, on Linux and macOS, on anything that is not a socket: a FIFO, a one-way pipe, a file, a terminal. Windows has no such file types to tell apart, and Node's stream over the descriptor refuses a file or a console there. A Unix socket that listens is not told apart from a socket pair; it would fail at its first read, and only a parent can hand one over.

  It is one HTTP/1.1 connection for the life of the process, and only the parent writes to it. The server never closes it for being idle: it ends when the server stops, when the parent closes its end, after which the server goes on without it, or when a request on it is malformed: Node's HTTP server answers it 400 and destroys the connection (**Read** in `lib/_http_server.js`, `socketOnError`, v24.x at `221ffa5`), since nothing would tell where the next request starts, so the channel is gone for the rest of the process. A request sent before the server serves waits in the pipe and is answered once it does. Requests go one at a time, each read to its end. The client in `AdminClient` waits for an answer as long as on the socket (10 seconds, or what the call gives, such as a first `/status` that waits for the server to start) and no longer, so a parent never hangs on a server that stopped answering or a descriptor that is not the channel. A request that times out or is abandoned closes the pipe, for the same reason, and the parent then treats the server as lost: the desktop app kills it, as it does when `/shutdown` gets no answer. Node creates every `pipe` it passes to a child readable and writable on every platform (**Read** in Node's `src/process_wrap.cc` on its main branch, November 2026). On Windows the parent should pass this one as `overlapped`, which is the same as `pipe` elsewhere: without it the server's end is a synchronous handle, and Windows serialises reads and writes on such a handle, so an answer could wait behind the read that waits for the next request (**Assumed**, from Windows' I/O rules and libuv's `src/win/pipe.c`, not measured).

| Route | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| `GET /status` | The server's version, its process id (which tells the server that started from another process on the same socket), when it started, where it listens (scheme, address, port and, with TLS, the certificate's concrete names), its tool and engine ports, the engine's state, how many devices are paired, whether it serves the web client, its `publicOrigin`, and the names its client listener refused over plain HTTP since it started, the first 16 (`refusedNames`, which `doctor` warns of under Refused names) |
| `POST /pairing` | A pairing code, or, while pairing is locked after failed attempts, until when |
| `POST /pairing/unlock` | Lifts that lock, for `pair --unlock`, then answers as `/pairing` |
| `POST /running-work` | How many bots have work running, which a stop would end, read with every new first send held. When none runs, they stay held until the server stops, or for two minutes if it is not stopped, so `j3-bots update` stops the service with no work begun since it counted. A stop does not wait for the hold to end |
| `POST /provider-connection` | Stores `provider connect`'s new provider connection and restarts the engine with it, unless bots have work running that the restart would stop and the request did not agree to that |
| `POST /local-device` | `{name, replaces}` → `{deviceId, token}`: pairs a device without a code, as a successful pairing does (a new row, a new token of which only the hash is stored), marked as this computer's (`devices.signed_in_locally`), and in the same transaction revokes, as `device.revoke` does, every device this route paired before and the one `replaces` names: their push registrations go, and once the transaction commits their tickets and sockets do too. So this computer is one device at a time, also when the desktop app lost the id it kept, which it names as `replaces` for a device paired before the mark was recorded (migration 44). A `replaces` that is unknown or already revoked is left as it is, and `null` names none. A device paired with a code is never revoked here. `name` is held to a pairing device's rules. It neither needs nor counts towards the pairing lockout. 400 when the body does not decode. This is how the desktop app gets the device it uses with the server it runs ([decision 0020](../decisions/0020-the-local-desktop-gets-its-device-through-the-admin-channel.md)); the client listener has no such route |
| `POST /shutdown` | Answers 202, then stops the server as SIGTERM does. How the desktop app stops the server it runs on Windows, which has no SIGTERM |

**Stopping.** SIGINT, SIGTERM, `POST /shutdown` and, with `--exit-with-stdin`, the end of stdin all stop the server the same way: it releases what it holds in the reverse of the order it took it: the admin channels and the tool endpoint first, the admin channels each answering the requests in progress for up to a second, then the client listener, the engine, which stops the harnesses, the database and last the home's lock. **Verified** on Linux with the engine then pinned, 0.1.11, ready, and not re-checked with `0.1.13`: once stdin of a server run with `--exit-with-stdin` closed, it exited with 0, no process it had started was left, and the lock was free. A stop through `/shutdown` or stdin ends with exit code 0.

**Nothing remote holds a stop.** Each request the server, or `j3-bots update`, sends out of the machine and reads an answer to (the engine's archive, a harness's listing and download, the release feed and a release's files, a push, the MCP registry, a connector's OAuth endpoints) owns that answer only for as long as the read that sent it: however that read ends, the answer ends with it, a body never started too. Unowned, an answer left unread was ended only by a garbage collection, which a stopped server never runs; on Node 26 a download still arriving over TLS then kept the process running, past every stop's wait ([#374](https://github.com/JPSAUD501/j3-bots/issues/374)). **Verified** on Linux with Node 26.13.1: an unread `fetch` body from GitHub's release downloads kept a process running past 40 s, and an aborted one let it end at once.

**A stop that runs long says why.** A server still running 15 s after its stop began logs a warning with whether it had released everything it held, and what keeps its process running: each handle Node's diagnostic report lists as active and not unreferenced, by kind and where it goes or listens (`tcp to 203.0.113.7:443`, `pipe on /run/example.sock`, `process pid 4242`). A pipe, a Unix socket or a Windows named pipe, is named by its path, which the report gives as a string where a TCP or UDP socket gets an address and port (**Read** in Node 26.13.1, `src/node_report_utils.cc`). The wait for it never keeps the process running itself.

**`--exit-with-stdin`** stops the server once its stdin reaches its end: when the process that started it closes it, or exits, which closes it too. So a server the desktop app runs never outlives the app, even one that crashed. A process whose stdin is already at its end, such as a service's, stops at once with it, so the flag is only for a parent that holds stdin open. A stop asked for while the server starts ends the start there.

The protocol is J3 Bots' own: the CLI and the desktop app ship with the server they talk to, so it is not a client contract. Its status reply stays readable both ways between releases, since an update has one release's CLI wait for the other's server ([`protocol.test.ts`](../../apps/server/src/admin/protocol.test.ts)).

## Failure handling

| Failure | What the server does |
| --- | --- |
| The engine process exits | Restarts it and reconnects. Each time the engine becomes ready, the server opens each session's stream again from its stored cursor, by replay or replacement, then releases the workers and nudges each, and checks the secret submissions left undecided alongside them. While the engine is not ready the workers hold. The order within a session comes from its own state, not from the sequence: nothing is planned into a session before its events are known, and nothing new is sent into one with an unsettled Stop or a dispatch whose answer is not known, so the Stop runner sends the rounds never acknowledged, and the recovery settles those dispatches with their key, before anything new reaches that session; each worker's deadlines are stored, so none starts afresh. The engine cancels the turn that was running and its open requests expire; the turn's dispatch is recorded as interrupted and handled like a failed one (the engine's restart continuation is turned off). The server resumes each reported hold once, except in a session with an unsettled Stop |
| The engine socket drops | Reconnects with backoff, then runs the same sequence as after an engine restart |
| The engine has no such session | Records the session lost, shows it once, and plans nothing into it ([Sessions](#sessions)) |
